->Now begins <term>The Sun of Auspicious Moments</term>.<-
<note>original: "Atha Muhūrtamārttaṇḍaḥ prārabhyate." In Sanskrit tradition, the word "Atha" signifies an auspicious beginning. The title "Muhūrtamārttaṇḍa" suggests a work that shines light (like the Sun) on the selection of favorable timings (Muhūrta) for various life events.</note>

<summary>This is the opening line of the manuscript, presented within a highly decorative border, marking the formal commencement of Narayana’s 16th-century treatise on electional astrology.</summary>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory portion of the manuscript. Following the initial title on the previous page, the author now provides the traditional invocatory verse and begins the formal commentary (Mārtandavallabhā), explaining the philosophical and structural framework of the work.</meta>

## Salutation to Lord Ganesha, Goddess Sharada, and the Preceptors. Let there be no obstacles.

May the **Sun** <note>original: "Mārtanda." This is a double-entendre referring both to the Sun deity and the title of this book, "The Sun of Auspicious Moments."</note> protect our speech; he who, through his feet <note>original: "pādaiḥ." In the context of the Sun, this means "rays"; in the context of the book, it means "verse quarters."</note>, destroys the darkness of dullness. He possesses a body of many circles <note>original: "vṛtta." For the Sun, this means the solar orb; for the book, it means poetic meters.</note>, is worthy of service, grants desires, and is the eye of the universe. ॥ 1 ॥

This verse has a dual meaning. First, regarding the **Sun**:
May the Sun, the divine *Mārtanda*, protect our speech. What is the nature of this Sun? He possesses a body that is abundant in its orb. As stated in the *Smṛti* <note>Traditional legal and religious texts</note>: "The eternal, infallible Lord, holding the conch and wheel, resides within the solar orb," and so on. The *Śruti* <note>The Vedas, or revealed scripture</note> also says: "The Person who is in this orb is Agni (Fire)," and so on. Furthermore, what is his nature? Through his "feet" (rays), he removes the darkness of dullness. "Dullness" here refers to sin or cold, and "darkness" refers to literal gloom; he removes these. Again, he is "worthy of service," meaning he is fit to be worshiped because he grants desired fruits. Being worshiped, he becomes a "giver of desires," meaning he bestows the sought-after goals. Furthermore, how is he described? He is the "eye of the universe." The universe is seen through him; thus, he is the eye of the universe. This is appropriate as he is the "eye of the world" and the "Lord of the planets," serving as the Great Illuminator.

Now, regarding the **Book**:
May the *Mārtanda*—the text *Muhūrtamārtanda*—protect our speech. What is its form? It has a "body" consisting of many "meters" <note>original: "vṛtta." Specifically naming meters like Praharṣiṇī and Śārdūlavikrīḍita.</note>, as it is composed of these poetic structures. Furthermore, it "destroys the darkness of dullness through its feet." Through the "feet" (the quarters of its verses), it removes the "dullness" of ignorance <note>original: "mūrkhattva."</note> or lack of knowledge. Furthermore, it is "worthy of service," meaning it should be studied constantly. It is a "giver of desires" because, by providing expertise in determining auspicious moments (*Muhūrta*), it causes the accomplishment of desired goals. It is the "eye of the universe" because it illuminates the nature of Time. ॥ 1 ॥

I create this commentary on the *Muhūrtamārtanda* called the *Mārtandavallabhā* <note>meaning "The Beloved of the Sun."</note>. Having understood this, one might think they have attained a home of joy. ॥ 2 ॥

In this science, the **Four Preliminary Requirements** <note>term: "Anubandha Catuṣṭaya." A standard Sanskrit scholarly convention stating the Relationship, Subject Matter, Purpose, and Eligibility before the main text.</note> are now stated.
The **Relationship** is that this work is a "Limb of the Veda" (*Vedāṅga*) descended from Brahma and others. It was said by the sage Nārada in his own compendium (*Saṃhitā*):

---

The science of the shining stars (*Jyotiṣa*) is the stainless and supreme light of the Veda. The relationship of this science is that it is a "Limb of the Veda" given by the Creator. It is also said here: "This book was composed by the wise, adopting the meaning of earlier statements."

The **Subject Matter** <note>original: "Abhidheya."</note> consists of the discussion of: avoidable lunar mansions (*Nakshatras*), ascendants, planetary transits, the sacraments beginning with conception, marriage, the establishment of the sacred fire, architecture, journeys, false omens, forbidden days for study, the movements of the planets, and the entry of the sun into zodiac signs (*Saṅkrānti*). As Nārada says: "The subject matter is the determination of the auspicious and inauspicious for the world."

The **Purpose** <note>original: "Prayojana."</note> of this is the accomplishment of various tasks through the use of auspicious moments (*Muhūrtas*). Or, the purpose is the attainment of liberation through its perfect knowledge.

If one asks, "Why state these things?" it is replied: People only develop an interest in a science when its relationship, subject matter, and purpose are explained. It is said: "Since the engagement of listeners is achieved by stating the relationship, therefore the relationship is stated first in all scientific treatises." If the subject matter is not stated to one who asks, the result for them would be emptiness. Until the purpose of any science or action is stated, it will not be taken up by anyone.

Now, **Eligibility** <note>original: "Adhikāra."</note>—who is authorized to study this science—is stated: Only the twice-born <note>term: "Dvija." Historically referring to the Brahmin, Kshatriya, and Vaishya classes who underwent the sacred thread ceremony.</note>, because it is a "Limb of the Veda." Since the Veda with its six limbs must be studied and known by the twice-born. As is stated in Bhāskara’s *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* <note>A famous 12th-century astronomical work.</note>: "Therefore, this merit-giving, secret, supreme, and sacred science must be studied by the twice-born. The man who knows the science of the stars perfectly attains Virtue, Wealth, Liberation, and Fame."

Learned authors, at the beginning of a work, perform an **Invocatory Act** <note>term: "Maṅgalācaraṇa." An opening prayer to ensure the completion of the work.</note> for the daily successful completion of the book, based on the principle that "all undertakings are enveloped by flaws as fire is by smoke" and seeking to ward off obstacles. This is supported by the *Śruti* (Veda) which says, "The Truth destroys sin through Dharma," and uses Vedic markers like "May Indra of great fame grant us well-being." Therefore, the Brahmin Nārāyaṇa <note>The author of the text.</note>, with his mind fixed on the Lord, performs this salutation to his chosen deity to pacify obstacles in this compendium of the three branches of the science of the stars.

<summary>This page introduces the "Mārtandavallabhā" commentary, providing a dual interpretation of the opening verse and defining the four foundational requirements (Anubandhas) for studying the "Muhūrtamārtanda," a text on Vedic astrology.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurtamartanda, Martandavallabhā, invocation, Anubandha Catuṣṭaya, Jyotisha, Vedanga, Narada Samhita, Siddhānta Śiromaṇi, twice-born, electional astrology</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page opens the formal commentary of the manuscript. It begins with a traditional vow of authorship and an invocation to the deities, followed by a list of specific astrological periods considered inauspicious for performing "good works" (sacraments, travels, or ceremonies). The author, Narayana, establishes the purpose of the *Muhurta Martanda*: to provide a concise yet comprehensive guide to electional astrology (the science of picking the right time).</meta>

<margin>
M. M. 
1
</margin>

The author states his vow to compose this work in the *Praharṣiṇī* meter, beginning with the words "Shining with vermilion." He bows to **Gajanana** <note>original: "Gajānana"; the elephant-faced Lord Ganesha</note>, who is made beautiful and radiant by vermilion; to **Mother** <note>original: "Ambā"; referring here to Saraswati, the goddess of learning</note>; and to **Lord Shri Vishnu**. He further bows to the **planets** <note>original: "viyaticarān"; literally "those who move in the sky"</note> and to his **Gurus** <note>teachers and spiritual preceptors</note> who taught him the scriptures. I, Narayana, expand upon this book titled **Muhurta Martanda** <note>original: "Muhūrta Mārtanda"; 'The Sun of Auspicious Moments'</note>. For what purpose? For **success** in the accomplishment of tasks. And for what other reason? For the **delight of the learned** <note>original: "vibudha-mude"; to bring joy to scholars</note>. He describes the nature of this joy through the book's qualities: it is **brief** <note>original: "laghu"</note>, being extremely concise. Lest one suspect that a short book lacks depth, he adds the qualifier: **rich in meaning** <note>original: "bahvartham"</note>, containing a great deal of significance. To remove the doubt that a short but dense book might be difficult, he adds: **easy to understand** <note>original: "sugamam"</note>. Now, intending to explain the auspicious moments of the half-years (*ayanas*), he first describes those times that should be avoided in auspicious ceremonies using three verses in the *Śārdūlavikrīḍita* meter, beginning with "half a watch." These periods include "half a watch" and others.

<margin>
Comm. 
1
</margin>

# Having bowed to the elephant-faced one [Ganesha] shining with vermilion, to Mother [Saraswati], to Lord Shri Vishnu, to the planets moving in the sky, and to the preceptors; I compose this Muhurta Martanda—rich in meaning, easy to understand, and brief—for the joy of the learned.

One should avoid these times in auspicious works; this is the connection with the world. The **Half-watch and Kulika** <note>specific segments of the day ruled by inauspicious forces</note> will be defined later. **Mid-day** <note>original: "dinārddha"</note> refers to a situation where a lunar day (*tithi*) lasts for sixty *ghatikas* <note>24 hours</note> from sunrise, and is seen again on the following day; that is "mid-day." The **Lost Day** <note>original: "avama"</note> is well-known in astrology. The **First Half of Parigha** refers to the first part of the *Parigha* yoga <note>astrological alignment</note>. **Vishti** refers to *Bhadra* <note>a period considered unlucky for new ventures</note>, which will be explained. **Vaidhriti** is a specific yoga, as is the **Vyatipata** descent. **Transit** <note>original: "saṅkramaṇa"</note> refers to the Sun and other planets moving from one zodiac sign to the next. The specific number of hours to be avoided during the transits of all planets will be mentioned later. **Juncture** <note>original: "gaṇḍānta"</note> is of three types and will be explained. **Ekargala** is an alignment that will be described. The **Four Days of the Dark Moon** <note>original: "kṛṣṇānaṅgacaturdina"</note> spans from the 13th of the dark fortnight to the 1st of the next month. This is avoided because the **Moon is weak** <note>original: "kṣīṇatvāt"</note>. Some authorities avoid a period of five and a half days starting from the 12th. Among these options,

---

the middle path is accepted here, namely the "four days of the dark moon." This period marks the **Equality of Declination** <note>original: "krāntisāmya"</note> of the Sun and the Moon, which is determined through calculation. Nevertheless, regarding when this should be observed, he says: during the **Hour of Malefics** <note>original: "khalāhorā"</note>—the specific hour of a malefic planet—and at midday or midnight. It is stated in other texts that the Great Time <note>fateful time</note> resides in the "Great Night" <note>midnight</note>. Therefore, for two days, specifically ten *palas* <note>units of time</note> before and after, actions should be avoided. **Day of Loss of the Parents** <note>original: "kṣayadināpittroḥ"</note> refers to the death anniversary (*shraddha*) of the mother or father. **Mother's Menstruation** <note>original: "jananyārtavaṃ"</note> refers to the mother's monthly period. **Birth-related times** <note>original: "māsādyaṃ"</note> refers to the month of birth and the **Birth Star** <note>original: "janisambhava-graha-nakṣatraṃ"</note>. 

### Half-watch, Kulika, Mid-day, the Lost Day, the first half of Parigha, Vishti, Vaidhriti, the transit of malefics, the Juncture, Ekargala, the four days of the dark moon, the equality of declination of Sun and Moon, the hour of malefics, the first day of fire, the death anniversary of parents, the mother's menstruation, and the month and star of one's birth.

The star in which a planet is situated during birth and the **Eclipsed Star** <note>original: "grahaṇabhṃ"</note> <gloss>The lunar mansion where an eclipse occurs and the lunar mansion of birth</gloss>. The terms **Da, Su, Ya, Dhu** <note>Mnemonic syllables for astrological calculations</note> will be explained later. **Durmuhurta** <note>original: "duḥṣaṇo"</note> is the "bad moment" to be explained. One should avoid the **Month of the Eldest** <note>original: "jyeṣṭha"</note> for the first-born child. Regarding **afflicted stars**, those joined by **malefic planets** <note>original: "pāpagrahāḥ"</note>—which will be listed—should be avoided, as well as the stars immediately preceding or following them. These are called the **Pierced Stars** <note>original: "vidbha-nakṣatraṃ"</note>, whose characteristics will be defined. The **Diminished and Additional Months** <note>original: "ūnarvyādhikamāsakau"</note> refer to the *Kshaya Masa* and *Adhika Masa* <note>intercalary months in the lunar calendar</note> which will be explained.

<vocab>
**Muhurta Martanda**: "The Sun of Auspicious Moments," a title of this astrological treatise.
**Gajanana**: "Elephant-faced," a name for Lord Ganesha, the remover of obstacles.
**Vishti**: An inauspicious astrological period also known as Bhadra.
**Vaidhriti**: One of the 27 yogas, considered particularly unfavorable for auspicious starts.
**Sankramana**: The transition of a planet (especially the Sun) from one zodiac sign to another.
**Kshaya Masa**: A "lost" month in the Hindu lunar calendar where two transits occur in one lunar month.
**Adhika Masa**: An "extra" or leap month added to the lunar calendar to keep it aligned with the solar year.
</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the "Muhurta Martanda," beginning with a prayer to Ganesha and other deities before listing a variety of inauspicious times—such as eclipses, transits, and specific lunar alignments—that must be avoided for successful undertakings.</summary>
<keywords>Narayana, Ganesha, Muhurta, Astrology, Inauspicious times, Lunar calendar, Sanskrit commentary, Birth star, Eclipses</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of inauspicious times. The previous page introduced the author's intent to catalog these moments; here, he details specific planetary combinations, durations of "poisonous" moments, and physical or mental conditions that prohibit the performance of sacred rituals.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>M. M. 3 | Comm. 3</header>

<margin>Muhurta Martanda 3</margin>
<margin>Commentary</margin>
<margin>3</margin>

Regarding the three dangerous junctions (*Ganda-anta*): for the six combinations known as **Vishkambha**, **Vyaghata**, **Shula**, **Vajra**, **Ganda**, and **Atiganda**, specific durations at the beginning are avoided. In order, these are 3, 9, 5, 9, 6, and 6 **ghatikas** <note>original: "ghaṭikā"; a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>. To clarify: in *Vishkambha*, the first 3 *ghatikas* are excluded; in *Vyaghata*, 9; in *Shula*, 5; in *Vajra*, 9; in *Ganda*, 6; and in *Atiganda*, 6. Similarly, one must consider the "poisonous" *ghatikas* (*Visha-ghati*) of the **Nakshatras** <note>lunar mansions/stars</note> such as Ashvini and the rest, which have characteristics that will be described later. || 3 || 

The text mentions **Latta** <note>literally "a kick"; a malefic planetary influence on a star</note>, the group of five (**Panchaka**), and the "malefic group" (**Papa-varga**). All of these, along with the inauspicious transits of the Moon, should be understood through the section on planetary transits (*Gochar*). One must also know the misfortunes arising from planetary periods (**Dasha-arishta**) as taught in the science of horoscopy (*Jataka*). Further, one must avoid times when the **Lord of the Birth** <note>the planet ruling the sign one was born under</note>, **Jupiter**, or **Venus** are "set" <note>combust, or too close to the sun to be seen</note>. 

Also avoided is the "Scissor" formation (**Kartari**) <note>a "scissor" occurs when a house or planet is flanked on both sides by malefic planets, "cutting" its power</note> created by malefic planets affecting the **Ascendant** <note>Lagna</note> or the **Moon**. One must also avoid the ill effects arising from transits—specifically when many malefic planets dominate—and the various types of **Sutaka** <note>ritual impurity</note> arising from eclipses, births, or deaths. Among these, the impurity of an eclipse will be explained further.

---

One must also avoid **bad omens** <note>original: "duś-cihna"</note> at the start of a task, such as sneezing or hearing harsh words. **Mental distress** <note>original: "mano-vibhanga"</note> or a lack of internal will, and **disease** <note>original: "vyādhi"</note> such as a fever, are also reasons to pause. As the medical authority **Vagbhata** says: "Disease is a transformation characterized by pain, born of past sins." This means one should not perform an auspicious ceremony for a person suffering from fever. 

As stated in the *Jyotish-Prakasha*:
"The loss of radiance <note>combustion</note> of the birth lord, the lord of the ascendant, the Moon, Venus, or Jupiter; the birth month; the birth day; bad omens; mental distress; the intercalary and deleted months <note>Adhimasa and Kshaya-masa</note>; the impurity of death, birth, or an eclipse; the onset of fever; the mother's menstrual period; or the anniversary of a parent's death—all these are to be avoided. 

So too are the three dangerous junctions (*Ganda-anta*); the transitions between signs, lunar days, or stars; the astronomical alignments of **Vyatipata** and **Vaidhriti**; the first half of the **Parigha** combination; the Sun’s entry into a new sign (*Sankranti*); and the inauspicious periods of **Kulik** and **Ardhyama**. Furthermore, avoid a constellation occupied by malefic planets, or even a benefic constellation if it is joined by **Rahu** or **Ketu** <note>the lunar nodes</note>. One must know if the Ascendant is joined by malefic planets, if the lord of the Ascendant is in an 'enemy' or 'death' house, or if the birth Moon is poorly placed. 

Avoid the Ascendant if it is caught in a 'Scissor' between two malefic planets, if the Moon is waning, or if it falls in a bad division of the sign. Avoid malefic days of the week, malefic hours, and inauspicious combinations (*yogas*). These faults are denounced when performing any good work. Other misfortunes arise from planetary periods or transits, such as **Latta** (the kick), **Argala** (the bolt), or the poisonous minutes of a star. These faults are specified according to the region and the time." || 4 ||

Now, regarding the nine "secondary planets" (**Upagraha**). After discussing these faults, the author explains through a verse-and-a-half what should not be done. One should not perform auspicious works when certain stars, counting forward from the Sun's position, are afflicted. This refers to the 7th, 14th, 15th, 18th, 19th, and 21st through 25th years of a cycle. In such times, one should not engage in activities that might lead to injury or the use of weapons.

<summary>This page lists specific inauspicious time units (ghatikas) within planetary combinations and catalogs various physical, mental, and astronomical "faults" (doshas) that prohibit sacred ceremonies, citing authorities like Vagbhata and the Jyotish-Prakasha.</summary>
<keywords>Ghaṭikā, Yoga, Nakshatra, Kartari, Sutaka, Vagbhata, Jyotish-Prakasha, Dosha, combustion, omens, fever, Ganda-anta</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first chapter on 'Forbidden Times' (Tyajyaprakaranam) and begins the second chapter regarding the 'General Section on Constellations' (Sadharananakshatraprakaranam). It maintains the structure of the root text (Muhurtamartanda) accompanied by its commentary (Martandavallabha) by Narayana.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 4 / Ti. 4</header>

"One should expand," meaning one should perform. It is also stated: "There will be tremors caused by the gathering of lightning. The *Shula* <note>a specific inauspicious time-marker, literally 'a spear'</note> occurs on the seventh [constellation] from the Sun; the *Prashanishva* on the eighth; the *Ketu* <note>often referring to a comet or the descending lunar node</note> on the tenth; likewise the *Danda* <note>literally 'staff' or 'punishment'</note> on the twelfth; the *Nipana* is declared by the wise on the fourteenth; and the *Ulka* <note>meteor</note> is said by those who know time to be on the nineteenth from the Sun's birth constellation." In the *Daivajnavallabha* <note>a famous astrological treatise meaning 'The Astrologer’s Favorite'</note> as well, it is said: "The *Moha*, *Nirghata* <note>thunderous sound without clouds</note>, and earthquakes are to be avoided. Halos <note>original: "pariveṣa"</note> should be known starting from the twenty-first constellation. When these coincide with certain points <note>vinda</note>, one should not perform any auspicious work. However, [actions involving] fire, weapons, and poisons may attain success through effort." I shall mention other faults in due course. Now, in this book, the names of months and so on are to be known as established by common worldly usage. Furthermore, the author addresses the lack of fault in repetition by saying: "Months, seasons, and solar paths..." The months are Chaitra and the others. 

One should perform... from the poison of the great elephant... in the pairs... fifty... for the sake of the heavens... beautiful... cattle and so on. The established names of months, seasons, solar paths, days, planetary turnings, and lunar dates are to be understood. If there is repetition in some places, it is for the sake of easier memorization. || 5 ||

Thus ends the section on Forbidden Times in the *Muhurtamartanda* <note>The 'Sun of Auspicious Moments'</note> composed by Narayana, the son of the priest of the four-month sacrifice named Ananta. || 1 ||

->Here, the seasons are Spring and the others; the solar paths are Southern and Northern; the days are Sunday and the others; the turnings are Vishkumbha and the others; the lunar dates are the first and the others.<-

The names of the Sun and the Ashvins, and the established designations for the *Karanas* <note>half-lunar days</note>, zodiac signs, and houses, are to be known as established in other scriptures. Thus, if there is repetition here and there in this work, it is not a fault, as it is intended to aid the memory from a distance. || 5 || 

Thus ends the Section on Forbidden Times in the *Martandavallabha* <note>The 'Beloved of the Sun'</note>, the author's own commentary on the *Muhurtamartanda*. || 1 || 6 ||

---

Now, wishing to explain the General Section on Constellations, the author first gives the presiding deities of the constellations in a single verse. In this section, all verses are in the *Shardulavikridita* <note>a majestic poetic meter consisting of 19 syllables per line</note> meter. Starting from Aries—that is, from the constellation Ashvini—the lords of the constellations are declared by the wise as follows: the Ashvins <note>the divine twins</note> for Ashvini; Yama <note>Lord of Death</note> for Bharani; Agni <note>Fire</note> for Krittika; Brahma <note>the Creator</note> for Rohini; Indu <note>the Moon</note> for Mrigashirsha; Girisha <note>Shiva, Lord of the Mountain</note> for Ardra; Aditi <note>Mother of the Gods</note> for Punarvasu; Angiras <note>Jupiter/Brihaspati</note> for Pushya; the Serpents for Ashlesha; and the Ancestors <note>Pitris</note> for Magha.

> Starting from Ashvini, the lords are the Ashvins, Yama, Agni, Brahma, the Moon, and Shiva; next are Aditi, Jupiter, the Serpents, the Ancestors, Bhaga, Aryaman, the Sun, Tvashtar, and Vayu;
> Then Indra-Agni, Mitra, Indra, Nirriti, the Waters, the Universal Gods, Brahma, Vishnu, the Vasus, Varuna, Aja-Ekapada, Ahirbudhnya, and Pushan. || 1 ||

Bhaga for Purva Phalguni; Aryaman for Uttara Phalguni; the Sun for Hasta; Tvashtar <note>the divine architect</note> for Chitra; Vayu <note>the Wind</note> for Svati; the dual deity Indra-Agni for Vishakha; Mitra for Anuradha; Indra for Jyeshtha; Nirriti <note>the deity of destruction</note> for Mula; the Waters for Purva Ashadha; the Universal Gods for Uttara Ashadha; Brahma for Abhijit; Vishnu for Shravana; the Vasus for Dhanishtha; Varuna <note>Lord of the Ocean</note> for Shatabhisha; Aja-Ekapada for Purva Bhadrapada; Ahirbudhnya for Uttara Bhadrapada; and Pushan for Revati. The purpose of knowing the names of these deities is to identify the constellations and for the sake of performing sacrifices and rituals. || 1 || 7 ||

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on inauspicious times, defending the use of repetition for memory, and begins a new chapter listing the 28 constellations (Nakshatras) and their corresponding presiding deities.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurtamartanda, Narayana, forbidden times, portents, constellations, Nakshatras, Hindu deities, astrology, memory aids, ritual timing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of electional astrology (Muhurta), focusing on the classification of the 27 (or 28) lunar mansions (Nakshatras) and the planets (Grahas) into specific functional categories. It transitions from the attributes of stars to the inherent nature of the planets and the divisions of the day.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 5 | Ti. 5</header>

Now he explains the designations of the constellations <term>Nakshatras; the 27 or 28 divisions of the sky through which the Moon passes</term> such as "fixed," "movable," and so on. **Brahma** refers to the star Rohini. What are its characteristics? It is joined with the **three Uttaras** <note>the constellations Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>. The combination of these three Uttara stars along with Rohini is remembered by the lords of the sages as having the name **Fixed** <note>original: "sthiram"</note> or **Constant** <note>original: "dhruvam"</note>. 

Next, the **three Purvas** <note>Purva Phalguni, Purva Ashadha, and Purva Bhadrapada</note>, **Magha**, and **Bharani**—these five are called **Cruel** <note>original: "kruram"</note> or **Aggressive** <note>original: "ugram"</note>. 

The **three stars beginning with Shravana** <note>Shravana, Dhanishtha, and Shatabhisha</note>, along with **Aditi** <note>the star Punarvasu</note> and **Swati**, are called **Movable** <note>original: "charakhya"</note> or **Unsteady** <note>original: "chala"</note>. This group of five is known for its shifting nature.

-> **Rohini and the three Uttaras are Fixed. The three Purvas, Magha, and Bharani are Fierce. Shravana, Dhanishtha, Shatabhisha, Punarvasu, and Swati are Movable. Ashwini, Hasta, Abhijit, and Pushya are Light and Swift. Anuradha, Revati, Mrigashira, and Chitra are Soft. Vishakha and Krittika are Mixed or Ordinary. Mula, Ashlesha, Jyeshtha, and Ardra are Dread or Sharp. The wise perform actions according to these names to ensure success in their endeavors.** 1 <-

This is the meaning of what was stated. The group consisting of **Dasra** <note>Ashwini</note>, **Arka** <note>Hasta</note>, **Abhijit** <note>the intercalary 28th star often used in Muhurta</note>, and **Ijya** <note>the star of Jupiter, Pushya</note>—these four are called **Light** <note>original: "laghu"</note> or **Swift** <note>original: "kshipra"</note>. 

**Maitra** <note>Anuradha</note>, the **Final** one <note>Revati</note>, **Indu** <note>Mrigashira</note>, and **Tvashtra** <note>Chitra</note>—these four are called **Soft** <note>original: "mridu"</note> or **Friendly** <note>original: "maitra"</note>. 

The star of **Agni** <note>Krittika</note> and **Vishakha**—these two are called **Mixed** <note>original: "mishra"</note> or **Ordinary** <note>original: "sadharana"</note>. 2

**Mula** is well known; **Ahi** is Ashlesha; **Indra** is Jyeshtha; and **Shiva** is Ardra. These four are called **Dread** <note>original: "daruna"</note> or **Sharp** <note>original: "tikshna"</note>. 

In this world, regarding these stars designated as "Fixed" and so on, the **learned** <note>original: "panditah"</note> perform actions that correspond to the name of the star. For what purpose? For the successful accomplishment of their goals. This means that one should perform "fixed" actions (like the consecration of a building) during "fixed" stars, and "movable" actions during "movable" stars. This is clear. The use of two names for the same meaning here is done to enhance the poetic quality of the verse. 

In this context, he also states the "fixed" and other designations for the **Sun and other planets**, as well as their status as **benefic or malefic**. The **Sun** is fixed; the **Moon** is unsteady; **Mars** is aggressive; **Mercury** is mixed (depending on its associations); **Jupiter** is swift; **Venus** is soft; and **Saturn** is dread. 

Now he identifies the **malefic and benefic planets**: the **Waning Moon** <note>from the eighth day of the dark fortnight to the first day of the light fortnight</note>, the **Sun**, **Saturn** <note>original: "yama"</note>, **Mars** <note>original: "ara"</note>, **Rahu**, and **Ketu** <note>original: "shikhi"</note>.

-> **The Sun and others are Fixed, Unsteady, Fierce, Mixed, Swift, Soft, and Dread. The Waning Moon, Sun, Saturn, Mars, Rahu, and Ketu are malefic. Mercury is malefic when joined with them. 3 The portions of the day are governed by Shiva, the Serpent, Mitra, the Ancestors, the Vasus, Water, the Universal Gods, Abhijit, Brahma, Indra, the Night-Walkers, the Lord of Waters, Aryaman, and Bhaga. For the night, they are the Slayer of Love, the three-eyed one... [and others]. These fifteen portions of the day are remembered for use in ritual actions.** 4 <-

These mentioned beginning with the Waning Moon are **Malefics** <note>original: "papah"</note> or **Cruel**. Mercury joined with these malefics also becomes a malefic. As stated in the *Jatakottama*: "The Waning Moon, the Sun, Saturn, and Mars are malefics. Mercury is a benefic unless joined with a malefic. Rahu and Ketu are even more malefic, especially when joined with other malefics." This implies that the others are **Benefics** <note>original: "saumya"</note>. Their purpose is to determine the success of cruel or gentle deeds. 3 

Now, in connection with the constellations, he uses a single verse to state the presiding deities of the **portions of the day and night** <term>Muhurta; a unit of time approximately 48 minutes long</term>, which are used for achieving all works. There are 15 such divisions <note>original: "lavas"</note> of the day. They are: 1. Shiva, 2. the Serpent <note>Sarpa</note>, 3. Mitra, 4. the Ancestors <note>Pita</note>, 5. the Vasus, 6...

<summary>This page classifies the constellations and planets into psychological and functional categories (such as Fixed, Movable, or Cruel) and begins listing the presiding deities of the 15 daily Muhurtas (auspicious time-slots).</summary>
<keywords>Nakshatra classification, Sthira (Fixed), Chara (Movable), malefic planets, Muhurta deities, electional astrology, Jyotisha</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the classification of the lunar mansions (Nakshatras) to the specific deities ruling the divisions of the day (Muhurtas). It concludes the section on stars and begins the second chapter regarding the 'Sacraments' (Samskaras), specifically focusing on the astrological timing for 'Garbhadhana' (the sacrament of conception) and the significance of a woman's first menstrual cycle.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 6 / Ti. 6</header>

...The seventh [division of the day] belongs to **Shambhu** <note>original: "shambhu"; an epithet of Shiva</note>, the eighth to **Vishnu**, the ninth is **Abhijit** <note>the "victorious" time</note>, the tenth to **Brahma**, the eleventh to **Indra**, the twelfth to **Indrani**, the thirteenth to **Varuna** <note>the lord of the waters</note>, the fourteenth to the one called **Aryaman**, and the fifteenth to **Bhaga**. 

Now, for the first division of the night: it belongs to **Smaraha** <note>original: "smaraha"; Shiva as the destroyer of the god of desire</note>. Here, the sequence follows the lords of the constellations: **Ajaikapad**, **Ahirbudhnya**, and **Pushan**. Specifically, the first half-step belongs to Ajaikapad, the third to Ahirbudhnya, the fourth to Pushan, and the fifth to **Chitra**. The **Ashvins** <note>the twin celestial physicians</note> follow, then **Yama** is the sixth, **Agni** <note>fire</note> the seventh, **Ka** <note>Brahma</note> the eighth, and **Indu** <note>the Moon</note> the ninth. Then for the tenth of the day, the eleventh is **Jiva** <note>Jupiter</note>, and the twelfth is **Vishnu**. The three "Lords" follow: the **Sun**, **Tvashtar**, and **Anila** <note>the Wind</note>. Specifically, the thirteenth is the Sun, the fourteenth is Tvashtar, and the fifteenth is Anila (Wind). 

The purpose of these names is explained: they are remembered for the success of actions. In these specific Muhurtas <note>astrological hours</note>, one should perform the rituals prescribed for the corresponding star. This is what the great sages intended. This means: in the Muhurta of Shiva, one should perform actions prescribed for the star **Ardra** <note>which is ruled by Shiva</note>. In the Muhurta of the Serpent, one does what is prescribed for **Ashlesha**. In the Muhurta of the Friend, one performs actions for **Anuradha**, and so on. As stated in the *Ratnamala*: "Whatever action is prescribed for a specific star, that same action should be performed in the Muhurta ruled by the deity of that star." In common worldly usage, people are seen acting as servants to these names; therefore, the names of the deities are used to recall the stars and dates. 

Furthermore, regarding the visual unions [of planets], a "Serpent" [yoga] spoils the results with eighteen *nadis* <note>units of time, approx. 24 minutes each</note>, a "Direction" with fifteen, and a "Ghost" with eighteen, particularly affecting the latter half. Just as the "Serpent" is the 5th, "Direction" is the 10th, and "Ghost" is the 14th lunar day.

### This concludes the Section on Stars in the Commentary on the *Muhurta Martanda*.

---

## Chapter 2: The Chapter on the <term>Garbhadhana and other Sacraments</term> <note>Garbhadhana: the ritual to ensure healthy conception; Samskara: life-cycle rites of passage</note>

Wishing to discuss the sacrament of conception in relation to the menstrual cycle, the author first describes the results of the very first menstrual period using a verse in the *Sragdhara* meter:

**"In the first menses..."**

First, he gives the results based on the month. If the first menses occurs in the months of **Pausha** <note>Dec/Jan</note>, **Shukra** <note>Jyeshtha; May/June</note>, **Urja** <note>Karttika; Oct/Nov</note>, **Madhu** <note>Chaitra; March/April</note>, **Shuchi** <note>Ashadha; June/July</note>, or **Nabhasya** <note>Bhadrapada; Aug/Sept</note>, they result in "offspring" <note>here used ironically or requiring specific conditions</note>. By implication, these six months are not considered auspicious. The others are auspicious. 

As stated in the *Jyotir-nibandha*:
"If a woman's first menses is in **Chaitra**, she will surely become a widow; in **Vaishakha**, she will be rich with sons; in **Jyeshtha**, she will be plagued by disease; in **Ashadha**, her children may die; in **Shravana**, she will be devoted to her husband; in **Bhadrapada**, she will be unfortunate; in **Ashvini**, she will be ascetic; in **Karttika**, she will have few children; in **Margashirsha**, she will have many offspring; in **Pausha**, she might become unchaste; in **Magha**, she will be blessed with sons; and in **Phalguni**, she will be a virtuous and wealthy lady." Such are the remembered results of the months.

Next, he gives the results of the "unions" <note>original: "yoga"; specific longitudinal joins of the Sun and Moon</note>. The verse mentions **Cruel Unions**: these are the divisions like *Vishkambha* and others. Among the yogas, nine are considered "corrupt" or "wicked": **Vishkambha**, **Atiganda**, **Vyaghata**, **Vajra**, **Vyatipata**, **Parigha**, and **Vaidhriti**.

> If the first menses occurs in Pausha, Jyeshtha, Karttika, Chaitra, Ashadha, or Bhadrapada; or during cruel yogas, on the eighth or fourteenth lunar days, on the 'Empty' days (Rikta), or on the days of the Sun, Mars, and Saturn; or during the evening, twilight, or afternoon; or under the stars Mula, the 'Sharp' stars, or during eclipses—the woman becomes unchaste or a widow. One should avoid clothes that are red, old, or blue-dyed during this time. 1

This means these yogas are inauspicious. Others are auspicious. It is said: "A woman menstruating for the first time in **Vishkambha** becomes unfortunate; in **Atiganda**, she becomes barren; in **Shula**, she is 'rooted' in problems; in **Ganda**, she becomes unchaste; in **Vyaghata**, she may harm herself; in **Vajra**, she becomes overly independent; in **Vyatipata**, she is a 'husband-slayer'; in **Parigha**, her children may die; and in **Vaidhriti**, she outlives her husband. The remaining yogas are auspicious and give results according to their names."

Now he gives the results of the days of the week: **"Malefic Days"** <note>original: "papa-vara"</note>. The days of the waning Moon, the Sun, Saturn, and Mars are malefic and not auspicious. Others are auspicious. As stated in the *Karika*: "On **Sunday**, she becomes a widow; on **Monday**, her children die; on **Tuesday**..."

<summary>This page lists the ruling deities for different times of the day and night, establishing a link between hourly timing and stellar influences. It then begins a new chapter on life-cycle rituals, detailing how the timing of a girl's first menstruation (menarche) is used in astrology to predict her future character and prosperity.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 'Samskaras' or life-cycle rituals, specifically focusing on 'Rtu-phala'—the prognostications derived from the timing and circumstances of a woman's first menstrual period. It transitions from the general timing of the ceremony to specific outcomes based on the day of the week, the lunar date, the location, and the time of day.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>Subodhini Muhurta-martanda Commentary</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 7</margin>
<margin>Comm. 7</margin>

One who destroys her own health [will be born] on Wednesday; she will be the mother of daughters. On Thursday, she will be a woman with many daughters. On Friday, she will have both daughters and sons. On Saturday, however, the woman will be unchaste <term>pumshchali</term> <note>literally "one who runs after men"; a term for an unfaithful or promiscuous woman in traditional texts</note> at her first menses—so say the wise. And thus says Narada: "One is sickly [on Sunday], devoted to her husband [on Monday], a wicked woman [on Tuesday], a mother of many daughters [on Wednesday], one who enjoys pleasures [on Thursday], beloved of her husband [on Friday], and a woman of misfortune [on Saturday]"—thus the effects from the Sun's day (Sunday) onwards to Venus's day (Friday). In the verse from the *Karika*, the result for Monday is missing; however, in Narada’s verse, it is stated as "devoted to her husband." Thus, where there is a contradiction—such as the risk of "dead offspring"—it should be understood that "devoted to her husband" applies specifically to the subjects of Monday and Friday. Therefore, according to the words of Ananda, [the exclusion of] the "cruel days" (Tuesday and Saturday) is highly recommended.

**The results of the lunar days are stated:**
"The empty days <term>rikta</term> <note>the 4th, 9th, and 14th lunar days, considered 'empty' or inauspicious for new beginnings</note>, the New Moon, the twelfth <note>called 'arka' here because its presiding deity is the Sun</note>, the eighth, and the sixth—these seven lunar days are not auspicious." Or, the word "arka" can represent the number twelve. This is also stated in the *Ratnamala*: "The five lunar days at the face of the season (menses)..." and so on. The eighth and sixth are well known. These seven are inauspicious; by implication, the others are auspicious. It is said: 
- On the first day: the woman becomes very fortunate.
- On the second: she is lucky. 
- On the third: endowed with sons. 
- On the fourth: she becomes a widow. 
- On the fifth: a giver of wealth. 
- On the sixth: a woman of bad character. 
- On the seventh: an increaser of wealth. 
- On the eighth: a "demoness" <term>rakshasi</term> <note>implying a fierce or cruel temperament</note>. 
- On the ninth: an increaser of sins. 
- On the tenth: a virtuous woman. 
- On the eleventh: endowed with sons. 
- On the twelfth: unfortunate. 
- On the thirteenth: a giver of gold. 
- On the fourteenth: she will be unchaste. 
- On the Full Moon: a mother of good sons. 
If it happens during the *Bhadra* <note>an inauspicious astrological period</note> or on the New Moon, she will be a thief.

**The results of the location are stated:**
"In another's house, or in a bad place <term>kupada</term>." "Another's house" means a house other than the husband's. "Bad place" refers to a contemptible location. In these, the menses are censured; by implication, other places are auspicious. It is said: "In a place of the gods (temple), a place of the ancestors (cremation ground), a contemptible place, or the house of an outcaste—the fruit for the woman in menses in these places is [inauspicious]." "Place of the gods" means a temple; "Place of the ancestors" means a cremation ground; "Contemptible place" means a vile location.

---

"Another's residence" means a house other than the husband's house. Another point to consider: in this verse, much has been said, yet why does the text only mention "another's house" and "bad place"? Truly, the two locations mentioned encompass everything said in all the verses. For example: "place of the gods," "another's residence," and "house of an outcaste"—these three terms are contained within the term "another's house." Others mentioned in the verses are included under "bad place." But one might ask: how is a "road" a "bad place"? It is not inherently contemptible, but a road is considered "ancestral" <note>original: "pitrvat"</note> because it is used for the movement of all people, as is well known in the Law Books <term>dharmashastra</term>. Therefore, the lengthy explanation in the previous verse is not useless; rather, it was spoken to emphasize the varying degrees of faults. There is no error here.

**The results of the time of day are stated:**
"Night, the two twilights <term>sandhya</term>, and the afternoon <term>aparahna</term>." Night is well known. The two twilights are [dawn and dusk]. The afternoon is the third part of the day when divided into three. These four times are inauspicious. By implication, the other two—forenoon and midday—are auspicious. It is said: "A woman in her menses in the forenoon becomes a mother of sons, fortunate, and meritorious. In the midday, she is auspicious. In the afternoon, she becomes self-willed <term>svairini</term> <note>implying an independent or unfaithful woman</note>. In the two twilights, she becomes a courtesan. In the night, she becomes a widow."

**The results of the constellations are stated:**
"The mixed, the fierce, and the sharp Mula." The "mixed" <term>mishra</term> stars are Vishakha and [Anuradha]. The "fierce" <term>ugra</term> are the three Purvas, Bharani, and Magha. The "sharp" <term>tikshna</term> are Ashlesha, Jyeshtha, and Ardra. These constellations are not auspicious; by implication, the others are. This is stated in the *Narada Samhita*:
1. **Ashvini**: Endowed with wealth, fortunate, mother of sons, happy, and stable; she is superior in her lineage and honorable.
2. **Bharani**: Ill-behaved, self-willed, prone to miscarriage, and sterile.
3. **Krittika**: Worthless, unchaste, sterile, prone to miscarriage, a servant, or one whose children die.
4. **Rohini**: Well-behaved, beautiful, honorable, devoted to her husband, and always engaged in the worship of the home.
5. **Mrigashira**: Endowed with virtues, devoted to righteousness, enduring, beloved by her husband, and wealthy in sons.
6. **Ardra**: Born of a good family, but unfortunate, suffering, and a wife whose sons die...

### [Folio 8 - Upper Section]

<margin>
Mu. Ma.
8
</margin>

<margin>
Comm.
</margin>

One who has fallen from difficult vows and is cruel [will be born if the menses begins] in the **Ardra** <note>original: "Raudra"</note> Nakshatra (06). Devoted to her husband, having sons and excellent offspring, joyful, and attached to family traditions in **Punarvasu** <note>original: "Aditibhe"</note> (07). Beloved of her husband, possessing sons, enjoying various pleasures, auspicious, devoted to her own work, and skilled in **Pushya** <note>original: "Tishya"</note> (08). Attached to another’s husband, hateful, irritable, lacking in virtues, lazy, a liar, and having bad sons in **Ashlesha** <note>original: "Bhujange"</note> (09). Free from enemies, endowed with health, always fond of music, and residing in her father's house in **Magha** <note>original: "Pitre"</note> (10). Intent on others' work, miserable, having bad sons, a partaker of distress, dirty, harsh, and angry in **Purva Phalguni** <note>original: "Bhagye"</note> (11). Having children, devoted to righteousness <term>dharma</term>, without enemies, honored by friends, chaste, and attached to her mother’s home in **Uttara Phalguni** <note>original: "Aryamne"</note> (12). Without hatred, possessing great wealth, rich in sons, a partaker of enjoyments, prominent, and skilled in giving in **Hasta** (13). Skilled in handiwork <note>original: "Chitra-karma"</note>, fond of enjoyment, skilled in buying and selling, with many desires and ornaments in **Chitra** <note>original: "Tvashtrabhe"</note> (14). Having great wealth, healthy, skilled in artisan work, having sons and grandsons, and virtuous in **Svati** <note>original: "Vayubhe"</note> (15). Devoted to low deeds, attached to wicked drinking, beloved of other men, without sons, dirty, and angry in **Vishakha** <note>original: "Dvidaiva"</note> (16). Concerned for her husband’s kin, adorned with her own virtues, having good sons, fond of garlands and beauty, and lovely in **Anuradha** <note>original: "Mitrabhe"</note> (17). Attached to bad conduct, distressed, useless [unlucky], unchaste <term>pumshchali</term> <note>literally "one who runs after men"</note>, short-lived, and having bad offspring in **Jyeshtha** (18). Possessing the virtues necessary for offspring, modest, quiet, and always devoted to her own work in **Mula** (19). Secretly sinful, having bad sons, intent on harming living beings, and constantly attached to vices in **Purvashadha** <note>original: "Toyabhe"</note> (20). Skilled in her duties and purpose, always following righteousness, a refuge of virtues, and fortunate in **Uttarashadha** <note>original: "Vishvabhe"</note> (21). Endowed with sons and grandsons, possessing enjoyments, wealth, and grain, chaste, timely, and honorable in **Shravana** <note>original: "Vishnubhe"</note> (22). Having wealth and grain, enjoyments, sons and grandsons, devoted to her work, and virtuous in **Dhanishtha** <note>original: "Vasubhe"</note> (23). Having many sons and wealth, devoted to her work, chaste, pleasing to the family, and honorable in **Shatabhisha** <note>original: "Varune"</note> (24).

---

### [Folio 8 - Lower Section]

A harlot <term>bandhaki</term>, hateful toward relatives, always attached to wicked things, and weak, yet skilled in artisan work in **Purva Bhadrapada** <note>original: "Agnibhe"</note> (25). Distinguished, having sons, honorable, cheerful, beloved of her husband, worshipped by relatives, and righteous in **Uttara Bhadrapada** <note>original: "Ahibudhnye"</note> (26). Firm in her vows, righteous, joined with the happiness of sons and prosperity, famous, and endowed with virtues in **Revati** <note>original: "Paushnabhe"</note> (27).

**Now the results of the Junction <term>Sandhi</term> are stated:**
Regarding the "junction" <note>the transition point between two astrological signs or lunar days</note>, it is said to be not auspicious. It is stated: "A woman is unfortunate if the menses begins during any of the junctions."

**The results of the blood <note>original: "Rakta-phala"</note> are stated:**
Regarding blood that is not the right red <term>anaruna</term>, or is too little or too much: "Anything other than a bright red <note>original: "Aruna," the color of the dawn</note> is considered improper." Specifically, if the blood is not the color of a red lotus, it is not considered auspicious. Elsewhere it is stated: "If the menses is the color of a red lotus <term>shuka</term>, she will be lucky and have sons. If it resembles the blood of a rabbit or lac-dye <term>alaktaka</term>, she will bear both sons and daughters. If it is variegated <term>karbura</term>, she may die; if it is yellowish-brown <term>pingala</term>, she will have dead offspring. If the blood is black <term>krishna</term>, the woman will become a widow." If the blood is only in droplets, she will be a loose woman; if there is very little blood, she will be of middle status; and if the blood is excessive, she will be unfortunate. In another text it says: "Red blood brings a son; black brings a dead son; foamy blood leads to barrenness; and pale/whitish blood <term>pandura</term> leads to 'crow-barrenness' <note>a term for a woman who bears only one child in her life</note>." If yellow, she is said to be unchaste; if the color of a Gunj-berry, she is lucky; if the color of vermilion <term>sindura</term>, she will have daughters. Such are the characteristics of the menstrual blood.

**The results of the astrological divisions <term>Karana</term> are stated:**
Starting from **Gara**, there are eight divisions including **Nirvriti** <note>likely referring to the fixed Karanas like Shakuni/Nagavanija</note> that are not auspicious. Regarding the others: In **Bava**, the woman will be barren or a widow. In **Balava**, she will have sons. In **Kaulava**, she will be a woman of pleasure. In **Taitila**, she will be respected and virtuous. In **Gara**, the woman is ruined or loses her offspring. In **Vanija**, she has no offspring. In **Vishti**, she is barren.

<summary>This page details the astrological prognostications for a woman's future character and family based on which Nakshatra (lunar mansion) her first period falls under, as well as the significance of the blood's color and the timing within the lunar cycle (Karana).</summary>
<keywords>Nakshatra, Menarche, Prathamartava, Astrological Omens, Women in Astrology, Karana, Blood Characteristics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the astrological portents of a woman's first menstruation (menarche). Having previously covered lunar dates and mansions, it now details the specific effects of the Ascendant (Zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon) and the condition of the clothing worn at the time. It concludes with specific ritual remedies (Shanti) for inauspicious timings and begins a general discussion on the strength of the Ascendant for subsequent rituals like conception.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 9</header>
<header>Comm. 9</header>

...abandoned by wealth, and if [the planets] are in the fourth or other positions, she might suffer the destruction of her husband (widowhood). If it occurs at the end of the night, she will certainly become a widow. Now, the results of the "rising" <note>likely referring to the posture or the act of rising at the time of the event</note> are explained: Rising is of three types, and [in some cases] no son shall be born. 

Thus says Narada: "In the twilights, or during an eclipse, the first menses is considered afflicted and inauspicious." Now the author explains the results of the Ascendant <term>Lagna: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</term>. **The Ascendant of a malefic planet** <note>original: "papasyapalagnam"</note> means the sign of a malefic planet is not good. This refers to the signs of the waning Moon, Sun, Saturn, and Mars. Specifically, the signs of Aries, Leo, Scorpio, Capricorn, and Aquarius are considered inauspicious. By implication, the others are auspicious. The Ascendant of Cancer is auspicious <gloss>during the bright half of the month</gloss>, but in the dark half, it is inauspicious due to the nature of its lord [the Moon]. The results of various Ascendants are stated as follows:

In **Aries**, she becomes a "slayer of the fetus" <note>original: "bhruunaha," implying miscarriage or infertility</note> and loses her wisdom; in **Taurus**, she will possess many sons; in **Gemini**, she will give birth to many daughters; in **Cancer**, her children may die; in **Leo**, she suffers widowhood; in **Virgo**, she will give birth to daughters; in **Libra**, she will be rich with many sons; in **Scorpio**, she will be devoted to wicked deeds; in **Sagittarius**, she will be rich in wealth and sons; in **Capricorn**, she will be miserable; in **Aquarius**, she will have offspring only once; and in **Pisces**, she will have few children. 

Narada also says: "Cancer, Taurus, Sagittarius, Pisces, Gemini, Virgo, and Libra are known to be the signs that give auspicious results for the first menses of women." Here too, for the Cancer Ascendant, the specific outcome should be understood based on whether the Moon is a benefic <term>Saumya</term> or malefic <term>Papa</term> at that time.

Now the results of the clothing are explained: **Red, old, blue, or multicolored clothing** <note>original: "aruna-jarannila-citrambaram"</note> is not auspicious. "Aruna" means reddish; "Jaran" means worn-out or old; "Nila" refers to the well-known blue/dark color; "Citra" means dyed with various colors. Such garments are not good. By implication, firm, white, or yellow garments are auspicious. It is said: 

"One wearing new clothes becomes fortunate; one in red clothes becomes sickly. A woman wearing blue cloth becomes a widow [if it is her first menses]; one in yellow cloth is a woman of enjoyment, and one in firm (undamaged) cloth is devoted to her husband. One in old clothes is unfortunate, while one in beautiful clothes is blessed." Other details regarding the avoidance of specific periods should be considered from the relevant chapters.

In the event that the menses occurs at an inauspicious time according to these results, a sacrifice <note>original: "yajana"</note> should be performed using sesame, ghee, and offerings to the gods. 

---

The sacrifice should be performed with the **Gayatri Mantra**, following the prescribed number of steps, offering 108 oblations of unbroken rice, sesame, and ghee. One should also donate gold, wheat, and oil according to their capacity. 1. Thus ends the chapter on the first menses.

3. Now, the general strength of the Ascendant is described according to the *Svarayodutta*: **Generally**, for rituals such as conception <term>Garbhadhana</term> which will be discussed later, one should **avoid the Moon when it is in the 8th, 6th, or the Ascendant itself.** This means avoiding the 12th, the well-known 8th, the 6th (the enemy house), and the 1st (the body/Ascendant). Similarly, avoid the **portion of the sign** <note>original: "bhvamshako"</note> belonging to the lords of the "deadly" portions. This refers to the degrees of the Ascendant that fall into inauspicious divisions. Furthermore, avoid the **degrees of malefics** <note>original: "khalamsham"</note>, such as those belonging to the cruel planets or the Moon when it is in its waning/malefic phase.

> **Generally, avoid the Moon in the 8th, 6th, or Ascendant; avoid the degrees of the lords of death, the degrees of the Sun, or malefics at the end of a sign.**
> **The benefics in the center (1, 4, 7, 10) or trines (5, 9) are praised. The other planets are good in the 6th, 11th, and 3rd houses. All of these should be considered while avoiding the specific faults mentioned in the chapter on prohibitions.** 2. 

Thus ends the section on the general strength of the Ascendant. In any Ascendant, avoid the Navamsha (ninth-part) of a malefic planet. Also, avoid a planet in the house of death—meaning, avoid a planet in the 8th house from the Ascendant. Also avoid [the lords of] the sign of death or the sign of birth if they are in the Ascendant. <insert>Chapter 3</insert> This means if the lord of the 8th house or the lord of the birth sign is positioned in the Ascendant, it should be avoided. Regarding the movement of the planets: **Benefics** <note>original: "saumyah"</note> are the auspicious planets. The "centers" <term>Kendras</term> are the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses. The "trines" <term>Trikonas</term> are the 9th and 5th houses. Benefic planets in these houses are highly praised. Other planets (malefics) are good in the 6th, 11th, and 3rd houses.

<meta>Mu. Ma. 10</meta>
<meta>Comm.</meta>

The eleventh house <note>original: "Bhava"</note>, the third house <note>original: "Sahaja"</note>, and the sixth—in these positions, other planets should be placed. In these specific places, even the **malefic planets** are considered praiseworthy. This rule should be applied everywhere, as it is stated in the verses for all planetary actions due to its general applicability. What should be done first? One should understand the specific details taught in this section. First, one learns the specifics 3; thus ends the section on the **General Strength of the Ascendant (Lagna)** 3.

### On the Rite of Conception (Garbhadhana)

Now, the author addresses the rite of conception <note>original: "Garbhadhana"</note> along with the planetary conditions. He says: **"O seeker of a son! In the fertile season, being well-disposed, go unto your own wife."** The connection is that one should go. One who desires a son is a "seeker of a son" <note>original: "sutarthi"</note>; this is an address to him. O seeker of a son! The "fertile season" <term>Ritu: the period of sixteen nights starting from the first appearance of the menses</term> is when this should occur. One who is filled with "good joy" <note>original: "suditah"</note> should go unto his wife—

**Avoid the first four nights, uneven days, the junctions of time, and days of ancestral offerings. Abandon the Nakshatras of Mula, Magha, and the last sign. Avoid the eighth and fourteenth days of the lunar fortnight, your birth star, and the twilights. Go [to her] when Jupiter, the Sun, and the Moon are in the Ascendant, in odd signs and divisions, O seeker of a son. Conversely, O seeker of a daughter, go on even days when the Moon is in even signs.** 1 <gloss>meaning: because the woman carries the womb</gloss>

Here, the woman must also be joyful. The natural fertile season <note>original: "Ritu"</note> of women is considered to be sixteen nights. What should be avoided? One must avoid the "first four nights" <note>original: "pragnit-catushka"</note>, "uneven days" <note>original: "asama-dina"</note>, and "junctions" <note>original: "vivarana"</note>. Junctions refer to the transitions between day and night, or between lunar dates <note>original: "tithis"</note> and asterisms <note>original: "nakshatras"</note>. "Ancestral offerings" <note>original: "shraddha"</note> refers to the day of a funeral offering or the day preceding it; these days should be abandoned. Similarly, one must avoid **Mula** and **Magha** <note>two specific constellations considered sharp or fierce</note>, and the "last" <note>original: "antya"</note> which refers to the constellation **Revati**. These must be abandoned.

---

Furthermore, one must avoid the "year-eight" <note>original: "vasatkali"</note>, which refers to the eighth day of the year/month, the "Kali-fourteenth" <note>the 14th day of the dark fortnight</note>, the "birth star" <note>original: "janibha"</note> or the constellation under which one was born, and the daytime. Likewise, the "junction days" <note>original: "parvani"</note> must be avoided. These junctions include the **New Moon** <note>Amavasya</note>, the **Full Moon** <note>Purnima</note>, and specific astrological disturbances like *Vyatipata*, *Vaidhriti*, and the entry of the Sun into a new sign <note>Sankranti</note>. One should go to his wife only after avoiding these. 

As the sage **Narada** says: "From the appearance of the menses, women are considered untouchable for four days; then they are purified for these rites. This is the rule for all classes." The sage **Vasishtha** also states: "One should avoid union during eclipses, the *Vaidhriti* and *Vyatipata* disturbances, the *Vishti* period, the first part of malefic asterisms, the twilights, and during the mourning period for parents." On **even-numbered days** and with the astrological combinations to be described, a seeker of a son should approach his virtuous wife. On **odd-numbered days**, a seeker of a daughter should do so, while avoiding the "joints" <note>Ganda</note> and the junctions of lunar dates.

### Rites for the Male Child and Hair-Parting
### The Sun and Jupiter in male signs or odd divisions favor the male child rite. The Hair-Parting rite and the rite for the son should not be performed on the transition days or the empty lunar dates.

In the auspicious house, when Jupiter <note>original: "Ijya"</note>, the Sun <note>original: "Arka"</note>, and the Moon <note>original: "Indu"</note> are in the Ascendant <note>Lagna</note> in **odd signs and divisions** <note>original: "vishamabhalavargai"</note>. "Bha" means the zodiac sign and "lavana" means the divisional sign <note>Navamsha</note>. This means they should be situated in an odd-numbered sign and an odd-numbered ninth-part division. Not only should they be in odd divisions, but they should also possess "excellent strength" <note>original: "rudulla"</note>.

Now, the specific rule for one desiring a daughter is given: **"O seeker of a daughter!"** In this same time described above, but on **even days** <note>original: "ayugahani"</note>, being joyful, go unto your wife. With what conditions? With the opposite <note>original: "vyasaih"</note> of the previously mentioned ones. That is, with Jupiter, the Sun, and the Moon in the Ascendant in **even signs and even divisions**. As stated in the *Brihat Jataka*: "If the Ascendant, the Sun, and the Moon are powerful in male signs and odd divisions, one should predict the birth of a male; in even divisions, a female."

<page-num>11</page-num>
->**Commentary on the Muhurta Martanda**<-

With those [planets] in even signs, it is not performed; this is the case when there is a "Good Moon" <note>original: "Su-chandra"</note>, meaning when the Moon is favorable according to its transit. "With a Good Moon" means that all other negative factors are discarded, and the strength of the Ascendant should be applied as before. 

### On the Rites of Pumsavana and Simantonnayana

Now, the author speaks in the *Sragdhara* meter regarding the **Pumsavana** <term>Pumsavana: a ritual performed during pregnancy to ensure the birth of a male child and the health of the fetus</term> and the **Simantonnayana** <term>Simantonnayana: the "parting of the hair" ritual, performed for the mental health of the mother and the protection of the unborn child</term>. He says: **"In a male Nakshatra..."** These male Nakshatras <note>original: "Puṃ-nakṣatra"</note> are: Pushya, Hasta, Punarvasu, Shravana, Ashvini, Anuradha, Mrigashira <note>original: "Shashi"</note>, and Purva Bhadrapada <note>original: "Proshthapat-purva"</note>. In any of these male asterisms, on the days of the Sun, Jupiter, or Mars <note>original: "Arka-jya-ara-varai"</note>, in an odd Ascendant and an odd ninth-division <note>original: "vishama-anga-amsha"</note>—excluding the sign of Gemini <note>original: "vi-mithuna"</note>—the Pumsavana and the Simantonnayana should be performed. These two are mentioned together as they share similar requirements.

# In the second or third month, the first [rite] is performed; the other in the fourth, sixth, or eighth. The planetary strength for the male fetus and the Ascendant must be applied to these two, and the bright fortnight is the preferred time. 2

Regarding the selection of the names of the two [rites]: when the houses of Expenditure <note>the 12th house</note> and Children <note>the 5th house</note> are occupied by beneficial planets and are free from malefic ones. Avoiding the "empty" lunar days <note>original: "rikta-itara-tithya"</note> and the junctions <note>original: "parva"</note>. The "first" rite, **Pumsavana**, should be performed in the second or third month from conception. The "other," **Simantonnayana**, should be performed in the fourth, sixth, or eighth month of pregnancy. The strength of the Ascendant for a male fetus—as previously mentioned regarding Jupiter, the Sun, the Moon, and the Ascendant—should be applied to both rituals. The first half of the month (the bright fortnight) is the preferred time. It will be mentioned later that in rituals from pregnancy until the feeding of solids, the combustion of Jupiter and Venus need not be considered. The strength of the Ascendant remains as previously stated. Sage Jadukarnya says: "The Pumsavana should clearly take place in the second or third month."

---

The Pumsavana should be performed once the pregnancy is evident, or it may be performed alongside the Simantonnayana. Alternatively, Sage Nrsimha says: "Avoiding the empty lunar days and junctions, the Pumsavana is auspicious." When the Moon is in a male asterism, the wise should perform the Pumsavana on the days of the Sun, Mars, or Jupiter. Sage Brihaspati states: "Leaving aside Cancer, Gemini, and Virgo, the remaining signs are auspicious." Sage Narada says: "In the fourth, sixth, or eighth month, when the lord of that month is strong, when the couple has the strength of the Moon and the stars, on a day that is not an empty lunar day or a junction, on a Tuesday, Thursday, or Sunday... avoiding the sharp, mixed, or fierce stars, in a male Ascendant and male ninth-division..." The eighth house from the birth Ascendant and the eighth house from the ritual Ascendant must be pure, occupied or aspected by benefics, and not aspected or occupied by malefic "sky-goers" <note>planets</note>. For women, the Simantonnayana is especially auspicious during the first pregnancy when the benefics are well-placed.

# At the birth, perform the birth-rite even during the impurity of birth or death. Then, the Brahmins, using the "Fixed" and "Upward-facing" stars, shall perform the naming according to the syllables.

When the benefics are in the 5th, 9th, or center houses <note>1st, 4th, 7th, 10th</note>, and malefics are in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th, and the Moon is not in the 8th... if a malefic planet is in the house of death <note>the 8th</note> from the Ascendant, it surely destroys the woman or the strength of her fetus. <margin>2</margin> Now, the author describes the **Jatakarman** <term>Jatakarman: the birth ceremony performed before the umbilical cord is cut</term> in the *Sragdhara* meter. When a birth occurs, one should perform the birth-rite. If birth occurs during a period of ritual impurity <note>original: "sutaka"</note>, there is a general rule that "religious rites are abandoned during impurity except during an eclipse." To clarify this exception, he says: **"even during the impurity of birth or death."** This means that if a birth occurs during an existing period of impurity, there is no fault in performing the *Jatakarman*. Even if there is overlapping impurity, the father should bathe with his clothes on upon the birth of a son—

It also clarifies that the birth ritual (Jatakarman) must be performed immediately, even if the family is currently in a state of ritual impurity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of life-cycle rituals (Samskaras) from page 11. It moves from the rites performed during pregnancy to those performed immediately upon birth (Jatakarma) and the subsequent naming ceremony (Namakarma). It focuses heavily on the timing (Muhurta) and the purification of the father.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Subodhini-Martanda</header>
<header>Commentary</header>

<margin>Su-Ma 12</margin>
...[The father] should perform the birth ritual <term>Jatakarma: the ritual performed at the birth of a child, involving the father feeding the infant honey and ghee</term>. The meaning is that the father's purification occurs through the pre-existing ritual impurity <note>original: "ashaucha"</note>. This is a defining characteristic of the performer. At the end of the initial impurity period, he should perform the rite under "Swift" <note>original: "Kshipra"</note> and other favorable Nakshatras. 

The **Swift** asterisms are: Ashvini, Hasta, Abhijit, and Pushya. 
The **Brahma** asterisms are: Rohini and the three "Uttara" stars (Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada). 
The **Movable** <note>original: "Chara"</note> asterisms are: Shravana, Dhanishtha, Shatabhisha, Punarvasu, and Swati. 
The **Soft** <note>original: "Mridu"</note> asterisms are: Anuradha, Revati, Mrigashira, and Chitra. 

The strength of the Ascendant <note>original: "Lagna-balam"</note> should be determined as previously described. It is also said: "Even during the birth-moment of the child, if there are many impurities, how can the birth rite be done? The **Jatakarma** must be performed after first worshipping the ancestors <note>original: "pitri-pujana-purvakam"</note>. In the Swift and Movable asterisms, on the twelfth day or the first day, the Jatakarma is eternal," according to the sage Prajapati. The sage Samvarta adds: "When a son is born during a period of ritual impurity, the father attains immediate purification for that specific rite; he is purified by the pre-existing impurity."

->**Upon the birth [of a son], on the 12th, 16th, 20th, or 22nd day, using the birth-star or a movable sign, avoiding the 4th, 9th, 14th, and 8th lunar days, and Tuesday or Saturday, and omitting the 12th house—perform the rite during the day.** 3 <-

When a son is born, the father is prescribed a bath while still wearing his clothes. According to Ajabali, this should be done with cold water: "One should perform the incidental <note>original: "naimittika"</note> bath with cold water." This must be done even if the birth occurs at night. On this, the sage Vasishtha says: "At the birth of a son, during a sacrifice, at the transit of the Sun <note>original: "Sankranti"</note>, or at the sight of Rahu (an eclipse), a bath at night is highly recommended; otherwise, it is not." That bath should be performed facing North. Vasishtha also states: "Having heard that a son is born, the father should bathe with his clothes on, facing North, in a river or a natural pond." 

Regarding the specific purification of water at night, Shankhyayana says: "If using water drawn during the day, one should put **gold** in it. If a night-bath is required, it should be done near a fire." 

### On the Naming Ceremony (Namakarma)

Now the naming ceremony is discussed. The different social classes <term>Varnas: the four social orders—Brahmana, Kshatriya, Vaishya, and Shudra</term> should perform the naming after birth on the 12th, 16th, 20th, or 22nd day. <margin>Comm. 12</margin>

Specifically, the author means: After birth, the classes—starting with the **Brahmanas**—should perform the naming on the 12th (Sun), 16th (Ashti), 20th (Vimshati), or 22nd (Akriti) days. <note>The Sanskrit uses numerical synonyms: Surya for 12, Ashti for 16, etc.</note> This means: a **Brahmana** names on the 12th day, a **Kshatriya** on the 16th, a **Vaishya** on the 20th, and a **Shudra** on the 22nd day. This is confirmed by Brihaspati: "On the 12th day, or a lucky day from the father's birth, specifically on the 16th, 20th, or 22nd in order." 

There are other options as well. The sage Vyasa says: "Some wise men prefer the 10th day for naming; others say the 12th; others when a full month has passed; and other scholars say the 18th day." 

Regarding special circumstances even when the time is right, Garg says: "During a Vyatipata <note>an inauspicious astrological yoga</note>, a Sun-transit, an eclipse, or on a day dedicated to ancestors <note>Pitru-tithi</note>, a person should never abandon the ancestral offerings <note>Shraddha</note>, even if the proper time for naming has arrived. One should not perform the rite on the New Moon, during a transit, or during Vishti <note>the Karanas/unlucky periods</note>, even if it is the prescribed time." When there is a "blockage of time," there is no fault in delay. As it is said: "In any ritual where the time is fixed by months or days, there is no fault if Jupiter or Venus are combust or retrograde."

Regarding the procedure if the time is exceeded, the text says: **"Using the birth asterisms..."** If the time is missed due to local conditions or disasters, the rite should be performed when Jupiter and Venus are not set (combust) and during the Northern course of the Sun <note>original: "Uttarayana"</note>. It should be done on a lucky day with the Moon and stars being strong, using Movable, Fixed, Soft, or Swift Nakshatras. 

Regarding what should be avoided: **"Movable sign..."** <note>original: "Charangam"</note>. One should avoid a "Movable Ascendant" <note>original: "Chara-lagna"</note>, the **Rikta** lunar days (4th, 9th, 14th), the 8th lunar day, Tuesday <note>original: "Ara"</note>, and Saturday <note>original: "Manda"</note>. One should also avoid the 12th house and the afternoon (the third portion of the day). After abandoning these, the strength of the Ascendant is determined as before. Thus Brihaspati says: "The morning is considered best, and midday is considered middling."
