<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>Subodhini-Martanda - 13</header>

### The Naming Ceremony and Early Rites

According to the sage **Nṛsiṃha**, one should avoid the late afternoon and the night for the **Naming Ceremony** <note>original: "Namakarma"</note>. The sage **Bṛhaspati** adds that one should also avoid the evening twilight, times of "bad unions" <note>original: "dushta-yoga," referring to inauspicious planetary alignments</note>, and the days of Saturday or Tuesday. Furthermore, "Empty" lunar days <term>Rikta: the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of the lunar fortnight, considered unfavorable for new beginnings</term>, full/new moon days <note>original: "Parva"</note>, the eighth day of the fortnight, and the specific astrological period called **Kimstughna** should be avoided. Even at night, the ceremony should not be performed if these defects are present. 

For the success and prosperity of the name, the ritual should occur on auspicious days of the week and under favorable planetary divisions. **Fixed** zodiac signs <note>original: "Sthira Rashi," such as Taurus or Leo</note> are considered best, or **Dual** signs <note>original: "Dvisvabhava," such as Gemini or Virgo</note> if they are influenced by "benefic" planets. **Nṛsiṃha** notes that if a planet is in the eighth house from the Ascendant, it is inauspicious for the naming rite.

The collection of laws <note>original: "Sangraha"</note> specifies who or what may be named:
*   Temples, elephants, and horses
*   Trees, step-wells, and regular wells
*   All types of tools and professional equipment
*   Signs or insignia
*   Women and men
*   Works of poetry and literature
*   Animals, specifically
*   Royal palaces

Naming these is considered a specialized and significant action.

---

### The Four Early Milestones

Now, the four rituals of **placing in the cradle**, **sitting on the ground**, **drinking milk**, and the **first outing** are described. On the twelfth day after birth, the infant should be placed in a cradle <note>original: "palaka"</note> or a small cot <note>original: "khadva"</note>. One must not delay this. If the specific time passes, it should be done under Nakshatras that are "Auspicious," "Soft," or "Light" <note>original: "Shubha, Mridu, Laghu"</note>, ensuring the strength of the Ascendant as previously described.

A calculation is made using the stars: counting from the Sun and Mars, place five stars in each of the four cardinal directions and seven in the center. If the child's birth star falls in the **East**, it indicates disease; in the **South**, certain death; in the **West**, obstacles; and in the **North**, the possibility of illness. If the star falls in the remaining positions, it is middling and beneficial for the child. **Bṛhaspati** states in his compendium that the "climbing into the cot" should be done on the 10th, 12th, 16th, or 22nd day. 

The **Bhavishya Purana** <note>a sacred text focusing on future events and rituals</note> adds: On a desired auspicious day, endowed with the strength of the Moon and the stars, and under Soft, Fixed, or Swift Nakshatras, the family women should worship **Lord Vishnu reclining on the serpent** <note>original: "Sheshashayi"</note> and then place the infant down with the head toward the auspicious direction.

#### Sitting on the Ground
Regarding the first time the child sits on the earth: under the stars Mrigashira, Ashvini, Pushya, the three Uttaras, Rohini, or Jyeshtha, on an auspicious day (excluding "Empty" days), during the fifth month from birth, the child should be placed on the ground. The **Brahma Purana** states: "In the fifth month, do not fail to let the child sit on the earth."

---

In this rite, all planets are favorable, especially Mars. Avoid the "Empty" lunar days. Listen to the favorable stars for this: the three Uttaras, Mrigashira, Pushya, Hasta, Anuradha, and Ashvini. One should worship the **Boar incarnation** <term>Varaha: the third avatar of Vishnu who lifted the Earth from the cosmic ocean</term>, the **Earth Goddess**, and the priests. The area should be well-smeared (purified), a sacred circle <note>original: "mandala"</note> drawn, and amidst the sounds of prayers for a "holy day," the child should be seated. 

**The Sitting Prayer:**
> "O Goddess Earth, protector of the world, always protect this child who moves upon you. Grant them a full span of life, O beloved of Lord Vishnu <note>original: "Hari-priye"</note>."

#### First Milk and Feeding
Regarding the first feeding of milk: On the thirty-first day, the infant should be given milk <note>original: "payah"</note> to drink using a conch shell <note>original: "kambu"</note>. **Nṛsiṃha** suggests this can be done on the twenty-first day as well. 

-> On the twelfth day of birth, under Fixed, Soft, or Light stars, place the infant in the cradle. Under Mrigashira, Ashvini, Pushya, or Jyeshtha, on a bright auspicious day in the fifth month, let the child sit on the earth. <-

For the **First Solid Food** <note>original: "Annaprashana"</note>, use the appropriate stars. The child should face a "clear direction," meaning one not occupied by **Rahu** <term>Rahu: the shadow planet or "demon" in Vedic astrology responsible for eclipses; facing his 'mouth' is considered dangerous</term>. The strength of the Ascendant must be checked. One must also avoid the "Rudra-yoga." The text notes that Rahu moves through the eight directions throughout the day starting from sunrise: Rahu resides in the East and North-West during the Sun's time, in the South during Mars' time, and so on...

<summary>This page details the astrological timing and ritual procedures for a child's naming ceremony and early life milestones, including the first time they are placed in a cradle, seated on the ground, and fed milk.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early childhood rituals from page 13. While the previous page focused on the naming ceremony, this page describes the child’s first outing (Nishkramana) and the ritual of ear-piercing (Karnavedha).</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>Subodhini-Martanda-Dipika</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 14</margin>In the auspicious moments associated with the gods of the directions—Hari (Indra), Soma, Agni, Rakshasa, Yama, Varuna, Anila, and Hansa—one should calculate the appropriate timing. Specifically, for the Great Home <note>original: "Mahalaya," referring to the ancestral rites or a significant dwelling</note>, the presiding deity is Indra. Now, the author describes the **First Outing** <note>original: "Nishkramana"</note>. This outing is performed in the fourth month of the child’s life. It is said by the ancient sages that this ceremony follows the same rules as those for starting a journey. Regarding this ritual, the sage **Brihaspati** says: "The First Outing is the act of leaving the house for the first time. If this action is neglected, it results in the destruction of the child's lifespan and prosperity." The sage **Yama** states: "One should perform the First Outing in the fourth month of the solar calendar." The **Karika** <note>a collection of ritual verses</note> adds: "In the fourth month, under an auspicious lunar mansion and during the bright fortnight, the outing should occur. The child should be bathed, well-dressed, and surrounded by auspicious sounds."

**On the twenty-first day, or when the feeding is prescribed by the stars, the child should be joined with the sound of a conch; the outing is said to occur in the fourth month. 4**

After taking the child and leaving the house, one should go to the temple of the Sun or another deity. Having worshiped the deity properly, one should have the priests recite blessings. Then, after circumambulating the temple, the child should be brought back to their own home. They should also visit the house of the maternal aunt and then be taken to the maternal uncle’s house. There, through the recitation of blessings, the child is honored with the wish for a long life. This procedure for the child's outing is written according to the school of **Jayanta** and should be adapted according to one’s own family traditions. 4 Next, the author describes the **Ear-Piercing** <note>original: "Karnavedha"</note> in the *Mandakranta* meter. It should be done under the "Friendly" stars <note>original: "Maitra," such as Anuradha</note>, Revati, Mrigashirsha, or Chitra; or the "Swift" stars <note>original: "Kshipra," such as Hasta, Pushya, and Abhijit</note>. It can also be done under "Fixed" or "Moving" stars. The "Fixed" stars are the three latter ones <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, Uttara Bhadrapada</note> and Rohini. The "Moving" stars are Shravana, Dhanishta, Shatabhisha, Punarvasu, and Swati. These timings are further qualified: they should be free from the influence of the wind and water elements <note>original: "vyanilambha," referring to specific astrological defects</note>. <margin>Di. 14</margin>

---

This means they should be free from the stars associated with the wind (Swati) and water (Shatabhisha), and also free from the star Rohini. The months of **Chaitra, Karttika, Phalguna, and Pausha** <note>roughly April, November, March, and January</note> are suitable. The ears of boys and girls should be pierced by a skilled practitioner according to the rules. For a boy, the right ear is pierced first; for a girl, the left ear is pierced first. Regarding the needle, **Brihaspati** says: "A golden needle should be used for a prince, a silver one for a Brahmin or a Vaishya, and an iron needle for a Shudra; it should be the length of the middle finger." The procedure for ear-piercing is described in the **Grihyaparishishta** <term>Grihyaparishishta: an architectural and ritual supplement to the domestic law codes</term>. It states that the ear-piercing should happen in the third or fifth year, during the morning. Facing the East, the practitioner consecrates the right ear with the mantra: "May we hear what is auspicious with our ears" <note>original: "Bhadram karnebhih," a famous Vedic prayer for well-being</note>. Then the left ear is addressed with the mantra "Seeing with the eyes" <note>original: "Vashyanti veda"</note>. After giving the boy sweets, 

**Under the friendly, swift, fixed, or moving stars, avoiding those of wind and water, in the months of Chaitra, Karttika, Phalguna, and Pausha, avoiding the days and stars of Saturn and Mars—**

Then, one should provide a feast for the Brahmins. However, one must avoid the stars and days ruled by the Sun, Saturn, and Mars <note>Sunday, Saturday, and Tuesday</note>, as well as their specific astrological divisions. Furthermore, one should avoid the "Empty" lunar days <term>Rikta: the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of the lunar fortnight, considered unlucky</term>, the full/new moon days <note>original: "Parva"</note>, and the eleventh and eighth days. Nighttime and the twilights must also be avoided. Crucially, the ceremony must be performed in an **odd-numbered year** of the child's life, avoiding even-numbered years. The strength of the Ascendant <note>Lagna</note> is determined as previously described. Here, the sage **Nrsimha** says: "The two ears should be pierced in an odd-numbered year for the child, during the bright fortnight, on an auspicious day of the week, in the months of Chaitra, Pausha, Margashirsha, or Phalguna. The eleventh, eighth, full/new moon, and 'Empty' days should be avoided to ensure a favorable outcome."

It specifies the necessary astrological conditions, such as favorable stars, months, and the requirement of performing the piercing in an odd-numbered year.</summary>

<margin>Su. Ma. 15</margin>
<margin>Ri.</margin>
Regarding the two ears: according to the words of Krishna, all rituals should be performed except during the <unclear>three-fold</unclear> period. With the auspicious stars of Shravana <note>represented by "Karna" in the text</note>, Dhanishta, Hast, Citra, Svati, Mrigashira, Revati, Anuradha, and Ashvini; and when Jupiter is in the ascendant. **Agastya** says: "One should not perform ear-piercing during the two twilights <note>sunrise and sunset</note>, nor at night, nor during the junctions of lunar mansions and lunar days <note>Tithi-sandhi</note>." **Brihaspati** adds: "If one pierces the ears during these times, it will result in a tearing of the ear." Days associated with Saturn, the Sun, and Mars should be avoided for ear-piercing. According to the *Sangraha* <note>a compendium of traditional rules</note>, the days of Jupiter, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon are worshipful and auspicious. A golden needle is best for piercing and brings good fortune; silver or steel needles are also auspicious, used according to one’s wealth. On a clean ground, in a place facing the east, the piercing should be done. For a male child, the right ear is pierced first; for a female, the left. One should pierce with a copper needle threaded with white silk. After the piercing, wash the site under a favorable star.

<insert>
->One should avoid the "empty" days <note>Rikta tithis: 4th, 9th, 14th</note>, the transition days <note>Parva</note>, and the days of the Lord of Vasu. One should not perform the rite at night or twilight. For boys and girls, a skilled person should pierce the right and left ears respectively according to the rules. 5<-
</insert>

<margin>15</margin>
On a day when the sun is blocked: **Devala** says, "The shadow of the sun should not enter the ear-hole while it is being pierced. If it does, the ancient merits of the child will vanish." 5. Next, the **First Feeding** <note>original: "Annaprashana"</note> is described. From birth, the first feeding of grain for a male child is prescribed in the sixth or eighth month. The wise state that for a female child, the feeding should occur in the fifth or seventh month. Here, the sages avoid the *Nanda* days, and the days of Hari <note>Vishnu</note>, Varuna, and the Night. *Nanda* refers to the 1st, 6th, and 11th lunar days. *Hari* refers to the 12th, *Varuna* to the 8th, and *Rajani* to the night. The *Rikta* days are the 4th, 9th, and 14th. Some teachers also avoid the birth star <note>Janma-nakshatra</note>. It is said: "Feeding on the birth star leads to the loss of prosperity." However, many observe the first feeding during the birth star for weddings and feedings, so it is determined based on other factors. Again, in which...

---

The term *Shatkamshahoh* refers to a collection of sixths, ninth-divisions <note>Navamsha</note>, and days. When the lunar *Hakkansha* <note>a technical astrological division</note> is not present, it is called *A-chandra*. If the group is full, there is no defect. This applies to the divisions of other houses as well. Again, in which stars? In the fixed, soft, mobile, and swift stars, which have been famous and previously mentioned in the Tantras. Again, in which signs? Excluding Aries, Scorpio, and Pisces. This means the ascendants of Aries, Scorpio, and Pisces are not auspicious for the first feeding. When the center <note>Kendra</note>, the trine <note>Trikona</note>, and the eighth and twelfth houses are pure—meaning they are devoid of malefic planets.

<insert>
->The feeding is prescribed in the sixth or eighth month for males, and in the fifth or seventh for females. The wise avoid the Nanda, Hari, Varuna, Night, and Rikta days, and some also avoid the birth star.<-
</insert>

The "centers" are the first, fourth, seventh, and tenth houses. The "trines" are the ninth and fifth. The "end" is the twelfth and "death" is the eighth house. For both males and females, these houses must be pure and free of malefic planets. A malefic planet here would cause harm. The Moon <note>Indu</note> should be full and positioned in the ascendant; a full moon in the ascendant is auspicious for the first feeding. Other strengths of the ascendant are as previously described. Here **Narada** says: "The first feeding of a child should be done in the sixth or eighth month for boys, and the fifth or seventh for girls. Avoiding the Rikta days, Nanda days, the 12th, and the 8th, other lunar days are excellent for the feeding." In the *Jyotir-vincharane*: "One should know that prosperity is lost on the birth star. However, in the feeding of children, performing it on the conception star <note>Adhana-nakshatra</note> destroys disease." Thus says **Vriddha-Narada**.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for childhood rituals (Samskaras), specifically focusing on the first feeding of solid food (Annaprashana) and the astrological conditions that can be ignored due to the time-sensitive nature of these early rites.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 16, Commentary 16</header>

Regarding the child’s first feeding: the aspects and planetary days of Jupiter <note>original: "Jiva"</note>, Mercury <note>original: "Saumya"</note>, Venus <note>original: "Sita"</note>, and the Moon <note>original: "Inu"</note> are favorable. For the feeding of the child, the Moon and the Sun are praised. **Argava** states that the zodiac signs of Taurus, Aquarius, Libra, Virgo, Leo, Cancer, and Capricorn are auspicious, while Aries, Gemini, and Scorpio are to be avoided. **Narada** says: "The ritual should be performed under movable, fixed, soft, or swift lunar mansions <note>Nakshatras</note>, provided it is not the eighth house <note>Naidhana</note>." In the ascendant of the Sun, Aquarius is mentioned. 6 Now, regarding the four considerations: in which sacraments the faults of Jupiter's and Venus's combustion <note>Maudhya - when a planet is too close to the sun to be seen</note> are absent; where they are a fault; the duration of a planet's "infancy" and "old age"; and the lordship of the branches—all this is explained in one section. From conception to the end of the first feeding—meaning rituals from **Garbhadhana** <note>conception</note> to **Annaprashana** <note>first solid food</note>—one should not abandon the timing even if Jupiter and Venus are in their "infant" or "aged" states <note>periods immediately after or before combustion</note> or are fully combust. Why is this? Because of the "constraint of time" <note>Kalasya rodhat</note>.

->The Moon's portions, along with the fixed, soft, movable, and swift lunar mansions; Scorpio and Pisces; with the Moon in the center <note>Kendra</note>, the trines <note>Trikona</note>, or the twelfth and seventh houses; these are pure and auspicious for the child. 6<-

Since the Shastras <note>sacred teachings</note> have fixed the specific time for rituals from conception to the first feeding, one cannot delay them. **Mandavya** says: "In the seven rituals—including daily travel, the aging of a house, and up to the first feeding—the combustion of Jupiter and Venus is not a fault for domestic auspiciousness." Regarding "Hari-Guru" <note>Jupiter in the sign of Leo</note>, the southern path of the sun <note>Dakshinayana</note>, and intercalary or diminished months <note>Adhikamasa and Kshayamasa</note>, one should not abandon the ritual because of the time constraint; this should be understood through wisdom. For instance, the birth ritual <note>Jatakarma</note> is prescribed exactly at the time of birth. Because of that time constraint, one does not consider whether a planet is combust. The first feeding is prescribed as an option in either the sixth or eighth month. If combustion occurs in one month, it can be done in the other. Since there is a two-month window, there is some flexibility.

---

Even in those two months, if there is a restriction such as Jupiter being in Leo, it should not be considered here. One should perform the daily and occasional rites that have no other timing, as per the tradition. The sequence of the sacraments is stated in the **Katyayana-gatikakarika**: "Conception <note>Garbhadhana</note>, producing a male child <note>Pumsavana</note>, parting the hair <note>Simantonnayana</note>, the birth rite <note>Jatakarma</note>, naming <note>Namakarana</note>, the first outing <note>Nishkramana</note>, and the first feeding <note>Annaprashana</note>. Then tonsure <note>Chuda</note>, initiation <note>Upanayana</note>, the four Vedic vows, the gift of a cow <note>Godana</note>, releasing the girdle, and marriage—these are the sixteen actions." By this sequence, one should understand the rituals from conception to feeding. Likewise, **Brihaspati** says: "In rituals tied to a specific month, the combustion of Jupiter or Venus causes no fault, for then the characteristics of the month are more powerful." **Yama** also states: "In the rituals for pregnancy, birth, feeding, and monthly ancestral rites <note>Shraddha</note>..."

->From conception to the end of the first feeding, one should not discard the timing due to the infancy, old age, or combustion of Jupiter and Venus, nor for Jupiter being in Leo, the southern solstice, or intercalary months, because of the constraint of time.<-

"...in the daily performance of the Sapindikarana <note>ancestral ritual</note>, one should avoid the intercalary month." **Bhrigu** says: "Rituals from the parting of the hair and birth to the first feeding should be done in order; there is no fault even if Jupiter is in the sign of Leo." In the **Katyayana Smriti**, it is said: "Rituals from conception to feeding should be performed even in an impure month <note>Malimlucha</note> up until the piercing of the ears; others are not allowed, says **Bhaskara**." He says that combustion and other faults must be avoided for rituals starting with the tonsure <note>Chuda/Chaula</note>. This means for tonsure and subsequent rites, one should discard the timing if Jupiter or Venus are combust. Now, the measurement of "infancy" and "old age" for planets is given. For Jupiter and Venus, the infancy and old age lasts for seven days. "Seven days of infancy and seven days of old age." Although there are many views on the duration of planetary infancy and old age, the middle path of seven days is accepted here. Now, for the lordship of the branch...

<vocab>ज्योतिष (Jyotisha): Astrology and astronomy, संस्कार (Samskara): Rites of passage or sacraments, गर्भाधान (Garbhadhana): The rite of conception, अन्नप्राशन (Annaprashana): The first feeding of solid food to an infant, गुरु (Guru): The planet Jupiter, sometimes representing the teacher, शुक्र (Shukra): The planet Venus, सिंहस्थ (Sinhastha): The period when Jupiter is in the zodiac sign of Leo, considered inauspicious for some rites, शास्त्र (Shastra): Traditional scripture or authoritative treatise</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the astrological timing for the first feeding of a child (Annaprashana) and explains that early childhood sacraments are so time-sensitive that certain planetary "faults," like the combustion of Jupiter and Venus, can be ignored. It lists the sixteen primary Hindu sacraments and clarifies that stricter astrological rules begin only after the child's first feeding, starting with the tonsure ritual.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of childhood sacraments (Samskaras), moving from the first feeding (Annaprashana) to the commencement of Vedic studies (Nigamarambha), the sacred thread ceremony (Upanayana), and the first ritual haircut (Chaudakarana/Chaula).</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>Commentary on Muhurta Martanda</header>

Now, he explains what must be avoided during "setting" <note>the astronomical combustion of a planet</note>. Regarding the combustion of the Lord of the Vedas <note>original: "Sharvesha," referring to Jupiter/Guru</note>: while he is combust, a noble person should not perform the sacred thread ceremony <term>Maunjibandha</term> or the commencement of Vedic studies <term>Nigamarambha</term>. If the sacred thread ceremony and other such rites are performed during this combustion, the boy may become a fool. One might ask: why would he become a fool? Because the Lord of the Vedas is the one who grants mastery over them. The sacred thread ceremony is performed specifically for the sake of studying the Vedas; therefore, if the planet is weak, foolishness results.

On this, **Yajnavalkya** says: "The teacher, having initiated the student with the 'Great Utterances' <note>the sacred syllables Bhur, Bhuvah, Svah</note>, should teach him the Vedas and instruct him in the rules of purity and conduct. For each Veda, the period of student-celibacy <term>Brahmacharya</term> is twelve years, or five years, or until the text is mastered. The ritual haircutting <note>Keshanta</note> should occur in the sixteenth year. For the twice-born <note>members of the upper three social classes</note>, the Veda alone is the highest means for the success of sacrifices, austerities, and auspicious works."

<insert>
This tonsure <term>Chaula</term> is desired; however, while Jupiter or Venus are in their "infant" or "aged" states, or during the combustion of the Lord of the Vedas, the noble person should not perform the initiation or the beginning of Vedic studies. 7
</insert>

Thus, in the **Katyayana Grihya Sutra**: "Having completed the Veda, he should bathe <note>the Samavartana ceremony</note>; or the student-celibacy may last for forty-eight years, or twelve years for each Veda as permitted by the teacher," and so on. 7

Now, the determination for the tonsure <term>Chaula</term> is explained in one verse. The tonsure <term>Chudakarana</term> is said to be performed in the five months starting with Magha <note>roughly January–February</note>, excluding the month of Chaitra <note>original: "Amadhu," the month of honey/spring</note>. As **Narada** says: "The tonsure is beneficial in the five months beginning with Magha, avoiding Chaitra <note>original: "Madhu"</note>." It should be done in the second, third, or fifth year, or according to one’s own family tradition <note>original: "Svachara"</note>.

The **Katyayana Grihya Sutra** states: "The ritual haircutting <term>Chudakarana</term> for a one-year-old, or in the third year, or as tradition dictates; the Keshanta rite for a sixteen-year-old, or as is auspicious for all." **Harihara Mishra** explains "of a one-year-old" <note>original: "Samvatsarikasya"</note> as referring to a child who has passed one year of age.

---

Thus says **Shadgurushishya**: "Some perform it in the first year, others in the fifth, but not in the second; or it may be performed along with the sacred thread ceremony <term>Upanayana</term>, according to the family custom <term>Kuladharma</term>."

If the mother is pregnant <note>original: "Sagarbha"</note>, this ritual should not be performed here. This means if the mother of the boy is pregnant during the second, third, or fifth year, the tonsure should not be done. However, it is understood that it *can* be done after the fifth year even if she is pregnant. On this, **Narada** says: "While the mother is pregnant, one should not perform the tonsure ritual. This applies before the fifth year; after the fifth year, it may be done even if she is pregnant."

He then states that there is no fault if it is performed together with the sacred thread ceremony: "Where it is performed together with the initiation <term>Upanyayana</term>, then this prohibition does not apply." That is, when the tonsure and initiation are done at the same time, this prohibition (regarding the mother’s pregnancy or the month of Chaitra) is lifted.

<insert>
The tonsure <term>Chaula</term> is declared in the five months starting with Magha, excluding Chaitra, in the appropriate second, third, or fifth year, or by family custom. If the mother is pregnant, this should not be done.
</insert>

The same author says: "If performed with the initiation, no fault is found." Regarding the prohibition during the mother's menstrual cycle, he says: "The man seeking auspiciousness should avoid the thread ceremony, marriage, and tonsure during the mother's period until she is purified."

This means the seeker of well-being should not perform these three—the tonsure, the sacred thread initiation <term>Vrata-maunji</term>, and marriage <term>Upayamana</term>—until the mother’s purification. Similarly, in the **Manusmriti**: "If the mother is menstruating during a wedding, a vow, or a tonsure, then no auspicious work should be done; it should be done after her purification."

If another auspicious date cannot be found, a "peace-making" ritual <term>Shanti</term> is prescribed by the authors of the Karikas: "If the mother's menstrual fault exists and no other time is available, one should worship the Goddess of Fortune <term>Shri</term> to remove the terrifying fear of 'Indra-slaying' <note>a metaphor for a great sin or spiritual obstacle</note>. One should worship a golden image of a lotus, weighing one Masha, according to the rites of the Shri Sukta."

<vocab>Chudakarana (ritual haircut), Vratabandha (sacred thread), Upanayana (initiation), Narada (sage/author), Yajnavalkya (sage/lawgiver), Katyayana (sage/author), Samvatsarika (yearly/annual), Manusmriti (Laws of Manu), Muhurta Martanda (the primary text being commented upon)</vocab>

Key restrictions include planetary combustion, the mother's pregnancy during the child's early years, and the mother's menstrual cycle, along with specific ritual remedies if these timings cannot be avoided.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the first ritual haircut (tonsure), known as Chaulakarana, following the discussion of childhood sacraments and Vedic study on the previous page. It details the astrological timing specific to different social classes and planetary positions.</meta>

<page-num>१८</page-num>
<header>Commentary on Muhurta Martanda</header>
<header>Tika (Commentary)</header>

<note>original: "payasa-hantva-abhisincya"</note> After sprinkling [the child] with milk and curds, one should perform the ritual for forgiveness. According to the *Smritikodanda*, purity is achieved when the ritual is performed with the proper prefixing of "Ga" and others; otherwise, it is not purified.

Regarding the specific timing for the different classes <term>varnas</term>, the days of the week, the lunar mansions <term>nakshatras</term>, and the strength of the rising sign <term>lagna-bala</term>, it is said: The first ritual haircut <term>Chaula-karma</term> should be performed for the social classes starting with the Brahmins on the days of the Sun, Mars, and Saturn respectively. **Ina** means the Sun; **Ara** means Mars; **Arki** means Saturn. Saturn is well-known. This means: for a Brahmin, it should be on Sunday; for a Kshatriya, on Tuesday; for a Vaishya, on Saturday; and for a Shudra, also on Saturday. It is mentioned that for a Shudra, there are twelve sacraments <term>samskaras</term>, but they are performed without Vedic mantras <note>original: "amantra-iti"</note>, excluding the study of the Vedas, vows, the sacred thread ceremony <term>upanayana</term>, and the great vows.

### If [the tonsure] is performed along with the sacred thread ceremony, then this restriction applies; for Vedic study, vows, and marriage, one seeking auspiciousness must wait for purity. ८

For all social classes, the tonsure can be done during the three specific lunar mansions and the three "Mercury" mansions <note>refers to specific groups of nakshatras</note>. It should be performed on Friday or Monday during the waxing half of the month <term>shukla-paksha</term>. On Monday during the waning half <term>krishna-paksha</term>, it is highly condemned because the Moon is weak <note>original: "kshinatvat"</note>. On this, **Narada** says: Avoid the "empty" lunar days <term>rikta-tithis</term> and the junctions of the month. **Brihaspati** says: Monday is good during the waxing phase but despised during the waning phase. Wednesday is said to be auspicious, unless Mercury is joined with malefic planets. Regarding the days of malefic planets: for Brahmins, the Sun is auspicious; for Kshatriyas, Mars; for Shudras, Saturn.

With which lunar mansions should this be done? With **Antya** <note>Revati</note>, **Aja** <note>Ashvini</note>, **Aditya** <note>Punarvasu and Pushya</note>, **Shakra** <note>Jyeshtha</note>, and **Indu** <note>Mrigashirsha</note>. Further, which others? With **Ina** <note>Hasta</note> and **Hari** <note>Shravana</note>, and the three mansions following each of them. Thus says **Narada**: From **Dasra** <note>Ashvini</note>...

---

...the lunar mansions of Jupiter, the Moon, and the "Flower" <note>Pushya</note> and Fire <note>Krittika</note> are auspicious for this. In the ritual of tonsure, one should use the three mansions starting from **Hasta** and the three starting from the mansion of **Vishnu** <note>Shravana</note>. 

Regarding the seventh house <term>jamitre</term> during tonsure: the Sun, Mars, Saturn, and Venus in the seventh house bring death. **Ina** is the Sun; **Ara** is Mars; **Arki** (the offspring of the Sun) is Saturn; and Venus is well-known. These four are givers of death. **Vasishtha** speaks of their specific results: the Sun or Saturn in the seventh house causes death; Venus or Saturn brings destruction; Saturn in the ninth house <term>bhagya-sthana</term> is also mentioned. Although a general result of death is stated for all four, there is no fault if they are in their own signs. 

Except for Venus <note>original: "gata-kavi"</note>, all planets in the eighth house are givers of death. Those who have "lost Venus" <note>meaning Venus is not in the eighth</note>, if they are in the eighth house, cause death. On this, **Brihaspati** says: All planets in the eighth house are not desired, except for Venus. Venus in the eighth house <term>nidhana</term> grants all types of wealth and prosperity to the child. 

Benefic planets <note>original: "sadgraha"</note>, when they are without the Moon <note>original: "vyabja"</note>, are desired and auspicious in the last house <note>the 12th house</note>. **Narada** states: For tonsure and the first feeding of solid food <term>annaprashana</term>, benefic planets in the 8th or 12th houses are praised. However, there is another statement: "except for the 8th and 12th houses," which refers to the Moon. Other strengths of the rising sign are as previously described. Avoid the night, the twilights, and the "empty" lunar days for the Moon and the Ascendant. **Narada’s** word is: the sixth lunar day, the full moon, the eighth day, the days of malefic planets, the night, the empty days, the sixth day, the twilight, and the birth mansion should be avoided by the wise. ९

Next, he speaks of the decision regarding shaving <term>kshaura</term> in the appropriate context...

<vocab>चौलकर्म (Chaulakarana - first ritual haircut), उपनयन (Upanayana - sacred thread ceremony), वेदोव्रत (Vedovrata - Vedic vows), स्मृतिकोदण्ड (Smritikodanda - a legal/ritual text), वसिष्ठ (Vasishtha - a legendary sage), नारद (Narada - a divine sage and author), संस्कार (Samskara - rite of passage), नक्षत्र (Nakshatra - lunar mansion), लग्नबल (Lagna-bala - strength of the ascendant sign)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the astrological requirements for the Chaulakarana (ritual tonsure), specifying the auspicious days of the week for each caste and the favorable positions of planets and lunar mansions. It highlights that while most planets in the 8th house are dangerous for the child, Venus in that position is uniquely beneficial.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions regarding shaving and hair-cutting rituals (Kshaurakarma). While the previous page focused on the timing for the first haircut (Chaulakarana) for children of different social classes, this page shifts to general prohibitions for adults and specific exceptions based on circumstances or royal service.</meta>

<page-num>१९</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda - 19</header>
<header>Commentary - 19</header>

<term>Bhuktah</term> means one who has just eaten; <term>Abhyaktah</term> means one who has applied oil to their body; <term>Upavasi</term> is well-known as one who is fasting. <term>Ishvarajano</term> refers to a servant of the king or a powerful person. <term>Yuvatih</term> refers to a woman of auspicious status who is in her youth. <term>Pragavayaskah</term> refers to the first stage of life, specifically <note>according to the text's logic</note> up to fifty years of age. <term>Yogi</term> refers to a royal yogi, as famously described in the Gita and other texts. <term>Yatra</term> refers to one about to set out on a journey or towards battle. <term>Yuddhonmukhah</term> means those facing war or ready for combat. <term>Kritadinavidhih</term> refers to those who have already completed their daily morning prayers and rituals <term>ahnikas</term>. Regarding the phrase "one whose father is alive" <note>original: "ko-jivan-pitrikah"</note>, for such a person, shaving <term>mundana</term> is prohibited, except when it is an absolute necessity <term>avashyakarya</term>, as will be explained in the second half of the verse.

**Shaving is prohibited when the Sun, Mars, or Saturn are in the rising sign, or when the Moon is in the eighth house. One should avoid the constellations of Mars and the Moon, the nights, the twilight hours, and the "empty" lunar days <term>rikta tithis</term>. ९. Shaving is also forbidden for those who have just eaten or applied oil, those fasting, royal servants, young women, those in the first half of life, kings, yogis, those about to travel or fight, and those who have finished their daily rituals. For a man whose father is alive, shaving is forbidden. Furthermore, after the hair-parting ritual <term>Simanta</term>, a husband must not shave, cut his nails, travel to distant lands, cut trees, or carry a corpse, unless it is an unavoidable duty. १०**

<margin>Now, following the discussion of solar prohibitions, the text addresses the restrictions for the husband of a pregnant woman. Regarding the phrase "After the Simanta ritual" <note>original: "Simantordhvam"</note>: After the <term>Simantonnayana</term> <note>a traditional pregnancy ritual where the husband parts the wife's hair</note> has been performed, the husband must not engage in shaving <term>naravaka-lavana</term> (cutting of hair/nails) except for mandatory duties. "Shaving" here specifically refers to the cutting of human hair. Other terms are easy to understand. The phrase "unless it is an unavoidable duty" means that there is no fault in performing these acts if they are required by necessity. For example, if one's father passes away, shaving is required; or if there is a national crisis, traveling to another country is permitted. For one who maintains the sacred fire <term>Agnyadhana</term>, the tradition of shaving every month <note>original: "masi masi"</note> is accepted.</margin>

---

According to some views, the shaving ritual for those who maintain the daily fire <term>Agnihotris</term> occurs every fortnight. Now, for others, the text describes the constellations <term>nakshatras</term> for shaving. It is said that for the first tonsure <term>Chaula</term>, the solar timing is preferred. The term <term>Sauram</term> refers to the solar influence. The text now explains the specific solar occasions: being in the region of the Sun <note>possibly a specific sacred geography</note>, being at the Ganges, or during the installation of the sacred fire <term>Agnyadhana</term>. In these cases, even if it might normally cause the "death of the father" <note>an astrological warning</note>, the command of a Brahmin or a King makes shaving desirable at all times. This means the usual rules for solar constellations do not apply in these cases. This is supported by the *Ratnamala*, which states that under the order of a king or a priest—

->**Solar timing is prescribed for the first tonsure; however, in the region of the Sun, at the Ganges, or during the installation of the fire, or by the command of a priest or king, shaving is always considered auspicious.**<-

As Sage Brihaspati says: "For men employed in royal service or those serving the king, there is no need to examine the astrological timing for the cutting of beard, hair, or nails." In the *Jyotir-nibandha*, it is noted that a royal yogi, a woman, or a son whose father is living should not perform shaving at holy pilgrimage sites. In another law book <term>Smriti</term>, it is said: "Bathing in the Indus, shaving, carrying a corpse, and traveling to foreign lands should not be done by a householder <term>grihapati</term> <unclear>while the father lives</unclear>." However, "Solar" (emergency or specific) rituals must be performed even if there is a general prohibition. Performing the funeral rites for parents is never a fault. At the Ganges, in the region of the Sun, or on the anniversary of a parent's death, or during the <term>Soma</term> sacrifice...

<summary>This page outlines the specific conditions under which hair-cutting and shaving are prohibited, such as during pregnancy rituals for the husband or while a father is still living, while providing exceptions for royal service and pilgrimage.</summary>
<keywords>shaving prohibitions, Simantonnayana, pregnancy rituals, royal service, Ganges, Agnyadhana, astrological timing, Muhurta</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the timing of life-cycle rituals (Samskaras) from folio 19. While the previous page focused on the restrictions for shaving and hair-cutting, this page transitions into the specific auspicious timings for the commencement of a child's education (Akṣarārambha) and the sacred thread ceremony (Vratabandha/Upanayana) for different social classes.</meta>

# Muhurta Martanda — Page 20
## Commentary — Page 20

During the six months of the Sun’s northern course <note>original: "sauraṃ," referring to the solar half of the year or Uttarayana</note>, these rituals are prescribed. However, while one’s wife is pregnant, one should not perform the "pilgrimage-shaving" <note>original: "tīrtha-sauraṃ," shaving the head at a holy site</note>. Following the ritual of "parting the hair" <note>the Simantonnayana, performed during pregnancy</note>, one must not carry a corpse or perform similar funerary duties. Before the age of fifty, one should not perform the solar-shaving unless it is for a specific, required ritual <term>naimittika</term>. After that age, however, one may shave according to their own desire. This concludes that section. 

Now, the author describes the appropriate time for the beginning of education <term>Akṣarārambha</term> and the initiation ceremony <term>Vrata</term>. 

Starting from the constellation of Anuradha <note>original: "Mitra"</note>: In the fifth year from birth, before the time for the initiation ritual, at the time prescribed for the beginning of learning, the "rite of the letters" <term>Akṣaravidhi</term>—meaning the first time a child is taught the alphabet—should be performed. It is auspicious when the Sun is in its northern path <term>Uttarayana</term>, but not when it is in the sign of Aquarius <note>original: "Ghaṭe," the Water-bearer</note>.

->**In the constellations of Anuradha, the three Hastas, Punarvasu, Jyeshtha, Revati, Shravana, and Ashvini; in the fifth year, before the initiation ritual; the rite of education is performed while the Sun is in the northern path, excluding Aquarius. (11)**<-

The commentary clarifies: "In the northern path <note>original: "saumya-saṃjñe ayane"</note>," meaning when the Sun moves toward the north. "Excluding Aquarius" means the sign of the Water-pot is avoided during this northern transit. According to the standard definition, the year is divided into two paths of six months each, starting with Capricorn and Cancer. 

The constellations mentioned are: 
<term>Mitra</term> refers to Anuradha. 
<term>Tryarka</term> refers to the three constellations associated with the Sun, namely Hasta and its neighbors <note>often referring to the group of Hasta, Chitra, and Svati in this context, or variations of the Sun's asterisms</note>. 
<term>Aditi</term> is Punarvasu. 
<term>Indra</term> is Jyeshtha. 
<term>Antima</term> is Revati. 
<term>Hari</term> is Shravana. 
<term>Turaga</term> is Ashvini. 

The rest has been explained previously. By saying "before the initiation" <note>original: "vratāt-prāk"</note>, it is implied that this should not be done after the sacred thread ceremony. As Sage Brihaspati stated: "A wise person should begin the study of letters before the second birth <note>the spiritual birth of initiation</note>." The sage Nrsimha also says: "The acceptance of letters is prescribed in the fifth year; one should avoid the month of Aquarius during the Sun's northern path." 11.

---

Next, the author describes the primary and secondary times for the initiation ritual <term>Vratabandha</term> for the Priestly, Warrior, and Merchant classes <note>original: "Brahma-Kshatra-Visha"</note>.

The ritual for a Brahmin <term>Vipra</term> should occur in the eighth year, either from conception or from birth. For a Warrior <term>Kshatriya</term>, it should be in the eleventh year <note>original: "shiva," a numerical code for 11</note>. For a Merchant <term>Vaishya</term>, it should be in the twelfth year <note>original: "ravi," a numerical code for 12</note>. 

In this matter, even if the planet Jupiter <term>Jiva</term> is in an unfavorable or inauspicious position for one's specific birth-sign, the ritual may still be performed. When is this allowed? During the month of Chaitra when the Sun is in the sign of Pisces <note>original: "mina-sthe ravau"</note>.

->**For the Brahmin and others, the initiation occurs in the eighth, eleventh, or twelfth year from conception or birth. Even if Jupiter is unfavorable, it may be done when the Sun is in Pisces during the month of Chaitra, due to the strength of the year itself.**<-

The "strength of the year" <note>original: "abdasya dāḍhyāt"</note> means that reaching the prescribed age provides enough spiritual potency to override minor planetary afflictions. This is supported by the *Sangraha* text: "If one has reached the eighth year, even if the Sun is in Pisces during Chaitra, the initiation <term>Upanayana</term> is auspicious." Even if Jupiter is in the eighth house from the birth moon, or in the sign of Leo, or in its sign of debilitation or an enemy's sign, the tying of the girdle <term>Maunjibandha</term> is declared auspicious in the month of Chaitra when the Sun is in Pisces. If the specific year is not utilized, even if all other planets are in good positions, the "year-strength" is lost, and the time becomes as useless as death itself.

Next, the author will discuss the results of performing the ritual during the "secondary" or late period...

<summary>This page outlines the astrological timing for a child's first education (Akṣarārambha), ideally in their fifth year, and the sacred thread ceremony (Vratabandha) for the three upper social classes. It emphasizes that reaching the correct age (8, 11, or 12) provides a "strength of the year" that can overcome certain inauspicious planetary positions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from folio 20 regarding the appropriate timing for the initiation ceremony (Upanayana) and the start of a child's education. It transitions from discussing the primary age for these rituals to the maximum age limits and the astrological requirements for a successful ceremony, specifically focusing on the "Strength of Jupiter" (Gurubala).</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Subodhamala</header>

Beyond the designated years <note>original: "sharad," literally "autumns," often used to mean years</note>, that age is considered the limit. Thus, the decision regarding the years is as follows: for a **Brahmin**, the eighth year is the primary time. Doubling that makes sixteen. Therefore, the period for a **Brahmin** is from the eighth year until the sixteenth year. For a **Kshatriya** <note>the warrior/governing class</note>, it is from the eleventh year until the twenty-second year. For a **Vaishya** <note>the merchant/agricultural class</note>, it is from the twelfth year until the twenty-fourth year. This is the progressive determination of the years. Beyond this, they are no longer eligible. As stated in the *Sangraha* <note>a compendium of traditional rules</note>: "For kings (Kshatriyas) 16, for the doers (Vaishyas) 22, for the twice-born (Brahmins) 24—these are the final limits; beyond this, they become 'fallen' <term>patitya</term>. For their purification, the **Vratyastoma** rite <note>a ritual to recover lost social or religious status</note> is prescribed." 

Likewise, according to the teachings of **Katyayana**: "Until the sixteenth year, the time for a **Brahmin** has not passed; until the twenty-second for a **Kshatriya**, and until the twenty-fourth for a **Vaishya**. Beyond that, they are considered to have 'dropped the Savitri' <note>referring to the Savitri or Gayatri mantra, the core of the initiation</note>."

-> **Beyond one's own primary year, the limit is determined by doubling those years in sequence. However, if a child shows a desire for learning, it is praised to begin as early as the fifth year. || 12** <-

What happens to them? One should not initiate them, nor teach them, nor perform sacrifices for them, nor have social dealings with them. When the time has passed, if three generations have "dropped the Savitri," their descendants have no right to the sacraments <term>samskaras</term> or to be taught. If they wish to be purified, they must perform the **Vratyastoma** rite, after which they may be socialized with—so say the scriptures. 

Now, the author speaks of the time for initiation even before the standard age if there is an eagerness for study: "from the fifth year." In the fifth, eighth, or ninth years, for the Brahmin, Kshatriya, and Vaishya respectively, the "desire for reading"—meaning the study of the Vedas and other sciences—is considered appropriate and praised by the sages. It is said in the *Sangraha*: "Spiritual radiance, vigor, knowledge, longevity, prosperity, fame, and strength; these are the fruits for one initiated for the sake of learning from the fifth or eighth year." || 13 ||

---

Now, the author describes the "Strength of Jupiter" <term>Gurubala</term> in a single verse. Regarding the "sign" <note>original: "rashe," referring to the birth sign or Janmarashi</note>: Relative to the birth sign, when Jupiter (Guru) is in the **sixth, third, or first** houses, he is to be honored and bestows auspiciousness in this "binding of the vow" <term>vratabandha</term> (initiation). Jupiter also stays in the **tenth** house. However, even if he is in a position to be honored, if he is in the **twelfth** (last), **fourth** (water), or **eighth** houses, he is not a giver of auspiciousness. This means he becomes inauspicious. 

But if he is "in his own house" <note>original: "nijagrihagah," Sagittarius or Pisces</note> or "in his exaltation" <note>original: "tungago," the sign where a planet is strongest</note>, then even if he is in these "bad houses," he is considered praiseworthy. His exaltation is **Cancer** <note>original: "karkata"</note>. As stated in the *Brihajjataka* <note>a classic text on Hindu astrology by Varahamihira</note>: "Aries, Taurus, Capricorn, Virgo, Cancer, Pisces, and Libra are the exaltations for the Sun and the other planets, respectively, at the 10th, 3rd, 28th, 15th, 5th, 27th, and 20th degrees."

-> **Jupiter is auspicious for the sign if in the 6th, 3rd, or 1st house. Though he may be honored elsewhere, he is not good in the 12th, 4th, or 8th, unless he is in his own house or exaltation.** <-
-> **Similarly, for the Girl's Sign <note>referring to marriage or female-specific rituals</note>, if Jupiter is in a weak position, or if it is an urgent time, he should be worshipped twice if in a good house, or thrice if in a particularly favorable house. 13** <-

If Jupiter is in his "sign of debilitation" <note>original: "swanishadi," the sign where a planet is weakest, which for Jupiter is Capricorn</note>, then the results for the various positions change. "Similarly for the Girl's Sign": relative to the sign of **Virgo** <note>representing the bride or a girl's horoscope</note>, the auspicious and inauspicious effects are the same as described for the boy. If one needs to find an auspicious time quickly for a groom who has many other good qualities, or if it is an urgent time, even if Jupiter is in the "bad" 10th, 4th, or 8th houses, one should perform "double worship" <term>dvirachya</term>. If he is in the 6th, 3rd, or 1st houses, as prescribed by the scriptures, he should be worshipped. If he is in a very favorable position, he should be "worshipped twice," and by that double worship, the timing becomes beautiful. After speaking generally, the author mentions a special case for the "house": if he is in a particularly excellent house, he should be "worshipped thrice" <term>trirachya</term>, and then it is beautiful. Here, Jupiter in **Taurus** <note>original: "vrisha"</note> or **Sagittarius** <note>original: "chapa"</note>...

<summary>This page outlines the age limits for the Upanayana (initiation) ceremony for the three social classes and the social consequences of missing these deadlines. It also introduces the astrological importance of Jupiter's position (Gurubala) in determining auspicious timing for rituals, including remedial worship to offset weak planetary positions.</summary>

It also identifies the planetary lords of the four Vedas.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 23 Di. 22</header>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 23</margin> <margin>Di. 22</margin>
**Jupiter** situated in **Cancer** or **Taurus** grants great auspiciousness for marriages, initiations, and other vows. Even if Jupiter is in a water-sign <note>Cancer, Scorpio, or Pisces</note>, he is desired... <note>The text here discusses technical placement calculations involving the 8th and 12th houses</note>. For a marriage, the purity of the girl's Jupiter is essential in the 1st, 4th, 5th, 7th, or 9th positions. Wise men say Jupiter is auspicious when high. Even if he is in the 12th, 4th, or 8th house, if he is worshipped with triple offerings <note>original: "triguṇārcanāt"</note>, he grants the desired results.

### Concerning the Obstruction of Jupiter (Vima-vedha)

->When Jupiter is situated in the 2nd, 5th, 11th, 7th, or 9th houses, he is auspicious. He is praised if he is not "pierced" <term>viddha</term> by other planets transiting through the 12th, 4th, 8th, 3rd, or 10th houses. Even if he is in an unfavorable position, he is not considered harmful if he is similarly obstructed. He is not auspicious in the 8th house.<-

->During the **Bright Half** of the lunar month, in the five months starting with **Magha** <note>approx. January–February</note>, on a day ruled by the **Sun** or other auspicious planets <note>Jupiter, Mercury, or Venus</note>, the initiation should take place in the first half of the day. One must avoid prohibited study days, "empty" dates, and the evening period. 14<-

<margin>22</margin>
One should consider the purity of the girl's Jupiter. Even in the 8th house, a marriage can be performed if triple-worship is conducted. Now, concerning the months and other details for initiation: "In the Bright Half..." In the **Shukla-paksha** <note>the waxing moon phase</note>, in the five months beginning with **Magha** (excluding **Jyeshtha**), on a day of the Sun or the auspicious planets—namely Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus—on such a day, in the first half of the day, the initiation <term>vratam</term> is auspicious. 

What must be avoided? One must avoid **prohibited study days** <term>anadhyāya</term>, **"empty" dates** <term>riktā tithayo</term> (the 4th, 9th, and 14th), the 7th <note>original: "muni"</note>, the 13th <note>original: "madana"</note>, and the evening period <term>pradoṣa</term> as described in the chapters on prohibited study. 14 

### The Asterisms for the Initiation Ceremony

->The binding of the **Maunji** <note>the sacred girdle of grass worn during initiation</note> is praised in all asterisms except for **Magha**, **Bharani**, **Jyeshtha**, **Vishakha**, and **Krittika**. This is done when the Lord of the Veda to be studied is strong. Jupiter, Venus, Mars, and Mercury are the Lords of the Vedas.<-

->If the benefics are in their own signs or signs of exaltation, and if the Lord of the Veda is in a cardinal or nodal house <term>kendra-koṇa</term>, the initiate will become a scholar of the Vedas, a possessor of gold and cattle, and a devotee of the gods and ancestors. 15<-

It is clearly stated that the five asterisms <term>nakshatras</term>—**Magha**, **Bharani**, **Jyeshtha**, **Vishakha**, and **Krittika**—should not be accepted by the wise for the initiation ceremony. The initiation should happen when the **Lord of the Veda** <term>vedeśe</term> is strong in its position. If one asks who the Lords of the Vedas are, the text says: **Jupiter**, **Venus**, **Mars**, and **Mercury** are respectively the presiding lords. **Jupiter** is the lord of the **Rig Veda**; **Venus** of the **Yajur Veda**; **Mars** of the **Sama Veda**; and **Mercury** of the **Atharva Veda**. 

Regarding the strength of the houses: if the benefics are in their own or exalted signs...

<summary>This page outlines the astrological requirements for the Upanayana (initiation) ceremony, focusing on the transit of Jupiter, the avoidance of "pierced" planetary positions (Vedha), and the selection of auspicious months and lunar days. It also assigns specific planetary rulers to each of the four Vedas to determine the best timing for a student's specialization.</summary>

<keywords>Upanayana, Jupiter, Vima-vedha, Rig Veda, Yajur Veda, Sama Veda, Atharva Veda, Nakshatras, Maujibandha, Muhurta</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical astrological requirements for the Vratabandha (sacred thread ceremony). It transitions from discussing the specific influences of planetary placements on the student’s character and health to the rules for Vidyarambha (the commencement of formal education), including the necessary deities to worship and the timing of the ritual.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. / Tika</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 23</margin>
<margin>Tika 23</margin>
If the planets are situated in their own exalted signs and divisions, and if the Lord of the Vedas <note>referring to the presiding planet of the specific Veda being studied</note> is also in his own exalted division and placed in the centers <term>kendras</term> <note>the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a horoscope</note> or trines <term>konas</term> <note>the 1st, 5th, or 9th houses</note>, the boy <gloss>2</gloss> will always be a devoted student <term>brahmachari</term> and a knower of the inner meaning of the Vedas. Not only will he be a scholar, but he will also be as wealthy as a "winged bird" <gloss>the term 'pakshagraha' implies a bird or a specific lunar cycle</gloss>. However, if Saturn is placed in the centers or trines, the boy will become a servant of the low-born. Therefore, Saturn should not be in the centers or trines. It is said in the *Sangraha*: "With the Sun and others in the center, one serves the king; with Mercury, one engages in trade; with exalted planets, one follows a high profession, studies the Vedas, and performs sacrifices; but with weak planets, one becomes a servant of the lowly." 15

### Concerning the Affliction of the Ascendant

The text now describes the "breaking" or affliction of the Ascendant <term>lagna</term>. The Moon and the malefic planets <note>such as Mars or Saturn</note> in the Ascendant, or the Moon, Venus, and the Lord of the Ascendant in the 6th house...

-><insert>If the Moon and malefic planets are in the Ascendant, or the Moon, Venus, and the Ascendant Lord are in the 6th house, or if Venus is in the 12th, or if all planets are in the 8th house, they become "great killers" <note>highly inauspicious</note>. However, some say the Sun in the Ascendant is excellent if he is the Lord of the Day. If the Moon is exalted, he is also excellent in the Ascendant.</insert><-

-><insert>At the time prescribed for the sacred thread ceremony <term>Maunji</term>, after the ceremony is complete, one should worship Ganesha and the Goddess of Speech <note>Saraswati</note> according to the rules and then begin one's studies. 16</insert><-

...in the 12th house <gloss>vyaya</gloss>. If the rest are in conjunction in the 8th house <note>the house of death/obstacles</note>, each one becomes a "great killer." Furthermore, in the absence of other options, the Sun <note>original: "Ina"</note> in the Ascendant is considered excellent. It is said in the *Maunji Patala*: "The powerful Sun in the Ascendant or the 10th house is the giver of all purity for everyone’s sacred thread ceremony." Some great sages say that because the Sun is the lord of all purification, his presence in the Ascendant is praised when there is an extreme lack of other favorable times. Regarding "the exalted Moon being excellent," some sages say that the Moon in his sign of exaltation in the Ascendant is best. This statement applies to the rising of the Moon...

---

...he may be cursed or suffer from a wasting disease <term>kshaya-rogi</term> if it is the other fortnight <note>the dark fortnight</note>. In the bright fortnight, or if the Moon is in his exalted sign, it is different. The meaning is this: if the initiation occurs with the Moon in the Ascendant during the dark fortnight <term>Krishna Paksha</term>, the initiate will be cursed. He will be a "wasting-disease sufferer," meaning people will falsely accuse him, saying "this sin was committed by him."

The fruit of the Moon in the Ascendant during the dark fortnight has been stated. The verse starting with "In the bright fortnight..." seems to refer to the "re-initiation" <term>punar-samskara</term>. Since the Moon's placement in the dark fortnight is discussed for the thread ceremony, and such a ceremony usually occurs during the day, it is possible that for one who requires a re-initiation due to a lapse in ritual duty, the ceremony might be performed at night to avoid further delay. However, for the first sacred thread ceremony, the authors of the scriptures have prescribed the morning <note>original: "purvanha"</note> and not the night <gloss>Because it is forbidden to perform the Maunji at night</gloss>. Therefore, this statement likely refers to the subject of re-initiation. The rules for re-initiation and the climbing of the sacrificial post are well-known in the legal scriptures <term>Dharma Shastras</term>. A similar verse in the *Ratnamala* states: "The Sun in the 3rd, 6th, 8th, or 11th houses, the Moon in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th, and Saturn, Rahu, and Mars in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th—with all others in the 11th, 2nd, 3rd, or in the centers and trines—is an auspicious time for the thread ceremony."

Following this, regarding the Sun in the 6th house occurring at a night-time Ascendant: Narada, however, has censured the Moon in the Ascendant. His words are: "Even if the Moon is in his own exalted sign, if he is in the Ascendant, he makes the initiate suffer from wasting diseases." Other strengths of the Ascendant remain as previously described.

### The Commencement of Education (Vidyarambha)

Now the time for beginning education is described with the phrase "The time prescribed for Maunji." After the thread ceremony, during the time prescribed for the ceremony, one should begin the study of the Vedas. The use of "after the Maunji" implies that one does not have the authority to study the Vedas *before* the thread ceremony. What should one do? Having worshipped Ganesha and the Goddess of Speech <note>Saraswati</note> in the manner prescribed by the rules. In what kind of "Maunji time"? One that is free from fixed, movable, or "lion" ascendants and the "Brahma" constellations...

<vocab>Maunji (sacred thread), Vratabandha (rite of initiation), Samskara (sacrament), Lagna (ascendant), Chandra (Moon), Surya (Sun), Jyotisha (astrology), Vidyarambha (start of education)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the astrological conditions for the sacred thread ceremony, warning that poor planetary placements (like Saturn in the centers) can lead to a life of servitude or illness. It also marks the transition to Vidyarambha, emphasizing that Vedic study must only begin after the ritual initiation and proper worship of Ganesha and Saraswati.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of planetary influences on a student's education, moving into the final rites of the student phase—the first shaving of the beard and the removal of the sacred girdle—before transitioning into the rites for those who do not undergo the thread ceremony, and finally beginning the chapter on marriage.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 25, Commentary-25</header>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 25</margin>
The collection of the best jewels <note>original: "ratna-nisṛṇāsu-uttarāṇām"</note> refers to the later constellations. The constellations Rohini and the three Uttaras <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note> are considered supreme. For the commencement of education <term>Vidyarambha</term>, one should use these primary constellations, excluding those meant for the sacred thread ceremony <term>Maunji</term>. This is to avoid dullness of intellect <note>original: "jāḍyam"</note> and to ensure the knowledge remains firm in one's person. Because of the excellent knowledge gained, it is heard through the tradition. 16. Now, in the half-verse regarding the ceremony of the first shaving <term>Keshanta</term> and the removal of the sacred girdle <term>Maunji-vimoksha</term>, it is said: "Keshanta..." As stated by the lawgiver Manu: "The Keshanta rite is ordained for a Brahmin in the sixteenth year, for a member of the warrior class <term>Rajanya</term> in the twenty-second year, and for a member of the merchant class <term>Vaishya</term> two years after that <note>in the twenty-fourth year</note>." Regarding this rite of Keshanta, the elders say it should be performed like the first haircut <term>Chaula</term> in the year designated by the number "king" <note>a code for 16, as there are traditionally 16 legendary kings</note>. All rules applicable to the tonsure ceremony <term>Chaula</term> are to be applied here. The removal of the sacred girdle <term>Maunji-vimoksha</term> is said to happen at the time prescribed for the end of the vows. For both Keshanta and the removal of the girdle, wise men need not examine the strength of Jupiter. Now, for the Shudras <note>the fourth social class</note>, who do not have a sacred thread ceremony, the rite of "binding the dagger" <term>Churikabandha</term> is described.

### The first shaving <term>Keshanta</term> should be performed like the first haircut <term>Chaula</term> in the sixteenth year. The removal of the sacred girdle occurs at the time prescribed for the end of vows. For these two, wise men need not observe the strength of Jupiter.

In the second half of the verse, it is said that since Shudras lack the sacred thread ceremony, the binding of the dagger should occur on the lunar days and constellations previously mentioned, provided Mars, Jupiter, and Venus are rising. One should avoid the day of Mars <note>Tuesday</note>. The term "Aara" refers to Mars, as noted in the *Amarakosha* <note>a famous Sanskrit thesaurus</note>. The Ascendant <term>Lagna</term> or the divisional sign <term>Navamsha</term> must not be that of Mars. This should be done in the months prescribed for marriage; the *Amarakosha* states that marriage <term>vivāha</term> and wedding <term>upayama</term> are synonymous. The "binding of the dagger" <term>Churikabandha</term> refers to tying a small knife at the waist. Thus, in the *Sangraha* it is said: "For Shudras and the sons of kings, when there is no sacred thread ceremony, there is the binding of the weapon." This should be done during the times described for the sacred thread ceremony, but never on a Tuesday. The sage Narada says: "I shall describe the binding of the dagger for kings before they take a hand in marriage. In the months prescribed for marriage, in the bright fortnight, when Jupiter and Venus have not set, for a prince, when the Moon and stars are strong..."

---

<margin>25</margin>
Regarding the binding of the sacred girdle, one should avoid the day of Venus <note>Friday</note>. The ritual should avoid the eighth house from the person's birth sign. The Ascendant should be free from the six planetary faults <note>original: "ṣaṭ śānti vivarjite"</note>. Benefic planets should be in the centers <term>kendras</term>, trines <term>trikonas</term>, or the 11th house, while malefic planets should be in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th houses. One should perform the binding of the dagger after worshipping the gods and ancestors. One should properly worship the dagger itself in the presence of the deities, and then at an auspicious moment, bind it to the waist with its proper characteristics. I shall now speak of those characteristics as told by Brahma in the past. One should measure the length of the dagger and its width. Dividing the total by eight <note>the 'Gaja' or elephant count</note>, the remaining units <term>angulas</term> provide the result. I shall tell the fruit of the remainder: if the remainder is 1 <term>Dhvaja</term>, one becomes wealthy; if 2 <term>Dhumra</term>, death; if 3 <term>Simha</term>, victory; if 4 <term>Shvana</term>, sickness; if 5 <term>Vrisha</term>, gain of wealth; if 6 <term>Khara</term>, distress regarding a woman; if 7 <term>Gaja</term>, great affection; if 0/8 <term>Dhvanksha</term>, destruction of wealth.

### Since Shudras lack the sacred thread ceremony, the binding of the dagger is prescribed on the mentioned lunar days and constellations, when Mars, Jupiter, and Venus are rising, on their respective days, and in the months fixed for marriage. 17.

Thus ends the Chapter on Sacraments <term>Samskara-prakaranam</term> in the *Muhurta Martanda*. The rules for things to be avoided, as mentioned throughout, should be observed. In this chapter, all verses except the one on ear-piercing were composed by the author himself. 17. Thus ends the commentary titled *Martanda Vallabha* (The Beloved of the Sun) on the Chapter on Sacraments, including the Rite of Conception and others, being the 7th chapter of the *Muhurta Martanda*. Now, desiring to speak on the Chapter on Marriage <term>Vivaha-prakaranam</term>, the author describes the consideration of the qualities of the bride and groom in twelve verses. From here on, all verses in this book are in the *Shardulavikridita* meter <note>a complex poetic meter of 19 syllables per line</note>. A groom should marry <note>original: "udahet"</note> a beautiful maiden <term>kanyā</term> who is pleasing and suitable in terms of lineage <term>gotra</term> and family. One must identify any flaws. One should know the lineage and investigate the family through five or seven generations as described in other texts, and then observe the following here—

<vocab>Keshanta, Maunji, Churikabandha, Samskara, Muhurta Martanda, Martanda Vallabha, Shudra, Vivaha-prakaranam, Shardulavikridita</vocab>

<meta>This page transitions from the final rites of the student stage (Keshanta and Maunji-vimoksha) into the comprehensive rules for marriage compatibility. It introduces the eight-fold system of matching (Ashtakuta), specifically detailing Varna (social class), Vashya (mutual attraction/dominance), and the beginning of Yoni (biological/sexual affinity).</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>Shu. Ma. 25 Right</header>

<margin>Shu. Ma. 25</margin> Now, the text explains the sequence of factors, each stronger than the last, starting with <term>Varna</term> (Social Class). The sequence is: 1. <term>Varna</term> (Class), 2. <term>Vashya</term> (Control/Attraction), 3. <term>Bha</term> (Constellation/Tara), 4. <term>Yoni</term> (Biological Affinity), 5. <term>Khechara</term> (Planetary Friendship), 6. <term>Gana</term> (Temperament), 7. <term>Kuta</term> (Zodiacal Relation), and 8. <term>Nadi</term> (Pulse/Vitality). Each subsequent factor carries more weight than the previous one. If the total points <note>original: "guna"</note> exceed eighteen, the match is considered auspicious. First, the characteristics of Varna are explained: starting from the sign of <term>Jhasha</term> (Pisces), the classes are known as Brahmin and so on. Specifically: Pisces is Brahmin; Aries is <term>Kshatriya</term> (Warrior); Taurus is <term>Vaishya</term> (Merchant); Gemini is <term>Shudra</term> (Laborer). Then the cycle repeats: Cancer is Brahmin, Leo is Warrior, and so on. In this matching of zodiac signs, it is auspicious if the groom’s class is higher than the bride's; otherwise, it is not. 1. Next, the <term>Vashya</term> (Control) of various signs is described: for the <term>Nru</term> (Human) signs, the water-dwelling signs are "edible" <note>vulnerable or subordinate</note>. For the human signs, all signs except the Lion (Leo) are controllable.

### Now Begins the Chapter on Marriage

> A groom should marry a beloved woman who is pure in body, lineage, and family, and endowed with good qualities. The factors of <term>Vashya</term> (Control), <term>Bha</term> (Constellation), <term>Yoni</term> (Biological Affinity), <term>Khechara</term> (Planetary Friendship), <term>Gana</term> (Temperament), <term>Kuta</term> (Zodiac Relation), and <term>Nadi</term> (Pulse) are progressively more powerful. If the total points exceed eighteen, it is auspicious. The <term>Varna</term> (Class) begins with the Brahmin and others; here, a groom of a superior class is considered auspicious. 1.

<margin>from the Lions</margin> <margin>25</margin> "Without the lions" means that all signs except Leo are controllable. For the Lion (Leo), the "distorted" one—the Scorpion (Scorpio)—is the one to be avoided for control. This means all signs except Scorpio are controllable by Leo. Furthermore, the Scorpion is not auspicious for the Lion as they are enemies. For the Human signs, the "non-humans" are not preferred. These include water-dwellers and the Lion; a match should not be made with these. Other relations should be understood from their natural roles as "eater and food." For example, the Cow (Taurus) is controllable by the Human. Mutual friendship is seen here, therefore their

---

union is auspicious. However, this same match is not auspicious for those of the <term>Mlechha</term> (foreign/outcast) community, because among them, the cow is considered food. Such things must be considered according to local customs. The nature of the zodiac signs is understood from the <term>Brihaj-Jataka</term> <note>a foundational 6th-century astrological text by Varahamihira</note>: Pisces is two fish; Aquarius is a man with a pot; Gemini is a couple, a man with a mace and a woman with a lute; Sagittarius is a man with a bow whose lower half is a horse; Capricorn has the face of a deer; Libra is a man with scales; Virgo is a maiden in a boat holding grain and fire; the rest are similar to their names and are all moving in their own spheres. Now, <term>Bha-Gana</term> (Constellation matching) is explained in one verse: The constellations of the bride and groom should be counted from each other. Both counts should be divided by nine. If the remainders are 3, 7, or 5, it is not good. Next, <term>Yoni</term> (Biological Affinity) is explained: starting from the constellation <term>Ashwini</term>, the biological affinities (Yonis) of the stars are as follows.

-> The water-dwellers are controllable; all except the Lion are controllable; the Scorpion is not auspicious for the Lion; the non-humans are not preferred for humans, except where there is a natural relationship of eater and food. <-
-> Count the constellations and the <term>Kuta</term> between them; if the remainder is 3, 7, or 5, it is not good; the <term>Yoni</term> begins with the Horse and the Elephant, which are far apart in nature. 2. <-

The Yonis (animal types) for the constellations are: the Horse for Ashwini; the Elephant for Bharani; the Ram for Krittika; the Serpent for Rohini; the Serpent for Mrigashira; the Dog for Ardra; the Cat for Punarvasu <note>specifically a male cat/lynx</note>; the Ram is well-known for Pushya; the Cat for Ashlesha; the Rat for Magha and Purva Phalguni. 2. The Cow for Uttara Phalguni; the Buffalo for Hasta; the Tiger for Chitra and Vishakha <note>though later systems differ slightly</note>; the Buffalo for Swati; the Tiger for Vishakha; the Horse and Deer for others...
