<meta>This page continues the discussion of Muhurta (auspicious timing), moving from the mathematical calculation of house junctions to the specific planetary and temporal conditions that must be avoided or sought for marriage and other rituals. It specifically addresses the "Yavana" (Greek-influenced) traditions and the importance of the strength of the Ascendant (Lagna) in overcoming astrological faults.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>Su-Bha 51 / Ti- 51</header>

**Tuesday:** This refers to the tradition of the <term>Yavanas</term> <note>originally referring to Ionians/Greeks, later meaning foreign or western astrological traditions</note>, which suggests avoiding this day for certain activities. This means one should avoid the "death" <note>a technical term for a highly inauspicious moment</note> during marriage, on the days of Venus <note>Friday</note>, Mercury <note>Wednesday</note>, and during the twilight hours. The text explains an exception to this: some say this "death" fault only occurs if the <term>Lagna</term> <note>the Ascendant or rising sign</note> is weak. If the Lagna is powerful, none of these obstacles cause any harm. Thus, in the text *Jyotishaprakasha*, it is said: "One should avoid sickness and theft at night, and the five-fold fire during the day. Death is condemned at both twilights, but at other times it is not." Furthermore, if the Lagna is exceptionally strong, even the influence of Saturn becomes fruitlless, as known from the *Balajataka*. In the *Laghujataka*, it is noted that a zodiac sign becomes strong when it is occupied or aspected by its own lord, or by Mercury or Jupiter, or even if aspected by other planets in certain conditions.

## Purification of the Houses of Mars and Fire, the Day of Saturn, and the Ascendant in Marriage, Venus, and Twilight (29)

From that position, in the third or eighth house, those who are not the lords—the two lords of the houses of death—or those who have entered the house of death: their <term>Navamsha</term> <note>the ninth divisional chart used for fine-tuning predictions</note> or the Lagna should be considered. If the lords of the birth house and the death house are in total harmony <note>original: "mitraikatanasya," meaning acting as one friend</note>, then the fault is neutralized. If there is no such friendship between them, the ritual should not be performed. This is the conclusion. The sage Vashistha says: "When the lord of the eighth house or the lord of the birth sign is in the Lagna, or if the eye of the Lord <note>referring to a specific planetary aspect</note> is upon it, one gains great energy." Furthermore, regarding birth:

## The eighth lord from the birth star or Ascendant should not be in the Lagna; if the lords are friends, it is praised. The Navamshas of Libra, Gemini, Virgo, and Sagittarius are auspicious, as is Mercury even when set, and the Moon when joined with benefics. Weakness, however, causes misery. (30)

One should avoid Mars or the lord of the eighth house (calculated from either the birth star or the birth Lagna) if they are placed in the Ascendant; their Navamshas should also be avoided. As stated in the *Chudamani*: "In all rituals, the eighth house should be abandoned if the Lagna lacks strength." However, some authorities say this is not a fault if the lord of the birth sign and the lord of the eighth house are mutual friends; in that case, the sages know the marriage or auspicious event can proceed. The fault of the fourth and twelfth houses is not mentioned here because the previous teachers held that the fourth and twelfth houses should be abandoned, but later opinions accept them if they possess good qualities. This will be explained further ahead. The "Desired Planets" <note>original: "Ishtakheta"</note> are five, and there are five "favorable lines" <note>Ishtarekha</note>. If the Lagna is diminished by these, it is like a divine sign. 

Regarding the purity of the <term>Ansha</term> <note>divisional portions of a sign</note>: the portions of <term>Dhata</term> <note>Libra</note>, <term>Yugma</term> <note>Gemini</note>, <term>Bhiru</term> <note>Virgo</note>, and <term>Dhanu</term> <note>Sagittarius</note> are considered excellent. He then provides a specific detail: "the portions of the wise." This refers to Mercury <note>the "wise" planet</note>; even when Mercury is "set" <note>combust or invisible due to proximity to the Sun</note>, its Navamsha does not cause a fault. Likewise, when the Moon is joined with a benefic planet, it removes the "death" fault for the bride and groom. However, if these planets are "deprived of Ojas" <note>lacking strength or vitality</note>, they spread misery and poverty. (30)

->6 5 6 7 7 7<-

<summary>This page details specific planetary prohibitions for marriage, such as avoiding the 8th house lord and Tuesday, while emphasizing that a strong Ascendant or planetary friendship can override these faults. It also identifies the Navamshas of Gemini, Virgo, Libra, and Sagittarius as particularly auspicious for timing rituals.</summary>

<keywords>Bhaumavara (Tuesday), Marriage Muhurta, Lagna strength, Navamsha, 8th House (Mrityu), Mercury (Budha), Moon (Chandra), Vashistha, Jyotishaprakasha, Astrological Friendship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Muhurta (choosing auspicious times), specifically focusing on the purification of the Navamsha (the ninth-part of a zodiac sign) and the calculation of the Hora (planetary hour) for marriage rituals.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Sun of Auspicious Moments</header>

Now he describes another distinction: **"Not Vargottama..."** One should not select the final <term>Navamsha</term> <note>the 1/9th division of a zodiac sign</note> of a <term>Lagna</term> <note>Ascendant</note> unless it is <term>Vargottama</term> <note>a planet occupying the same sign in both the main chart and the ninth-part divisional chart, which increases its strength</note>. That is, unless the Lagna and the Navamsha are the same. Likewise, one should not select the Navamshas of the lords of the "death" signs <note>the 8th house</note>. This applies specifically when the Moon is in Libra or Capricorn. The meaning is that one should not choose these when these three conditions (specific Moon signs and Navamshas) coincide. Among the four movable signs, only Libra and Capricorn are mentioned here because Aries and Cancer lack suitable <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>lunar mansions</note> for marriage. Furthermore, one should not select a Lagna that is not aspected by five planets. Now, he explains the method for determining the Lagna from the Navamsha lord: among the signs starting with Aries, the first Navamshas are described in order. For Aries, they begin with Aries; for Taurus, they begin with Capricorn; for Gemini...

> ->**One should not give the final Navamsha unless it is Vargottama. When the Moon is in Libra or Capricorn, do not give the movable sign. Nor should the Lagna be given if it is not aspected by five lords. For Aries and the following signs, the first Navamshas begin with Aries, Capricorn, Libra, and Cancer.**<-
> ->**The number of elapsed Navamshas is multiplied by ten and divided by three; the result in degrees and minutes is the Lagna. 31**<-

<margin>...beginning with Capricorn; for Gemini, beginning with Libra; for Cancer, beginning with Cancer...</margin>

...and so on. Again, for Leo, they start with Aries; for Virgo, Capricorn; for Libra, Libra; for Scorpio, Cancer. Again, for Sagittarius, Aries; for Capricorn, Capricorn; for Aquarius, Libra; for Pisces, Cancer. This is the rule for the Navamshas. To calculate the degrees: the number of "past" or elapsed Navamshas should be multiplied by ten and divided by three. The result is the degrees and minutes of the Lagna. Here is an example: The desired Lagna is Aries. The signs follow the Aries sequence. If the desired Navamsha is Virgo, then 5 Navamshas have elapsed. These 5 are multiplied by 10, equaling 50. Divided by 3, the result is 16 degrees and 40 minutes. Adding this to the desired sign...

---

Now he describes the purification of the rising and setting: **"The Lagna and its Navamsha..."** If the Lagna and its Navamsha are aspected by their own lords, they are considered "having a human voice" <note>auspicious or favorable</note>. Alternatively, if they are aspected by each other’s lords, they are also considered favorable. This describes the purification of the rising (Udaya). Now for the purification of the setting (Asta): from those two (Lagna and Navamsha), consider the seventh house and its Navamsha. If they are as described before—aspected by their lords—they are favorable for the couple. If not, they become "givers of death" <note>highly inauspicious</note> to the couple. Now, he explains the method for calculating the <term>Hora</term> <note>planetary hour</note> at a given time: **"Subtract..."** By subtracting the Sun's position from the Lagna, the lords of the Hora are found. He gives the sequence of their calculation.

> ->**If the Lagna and its Navamsha are aspected by their own lords, they are favorable; or if they are aspected by each other’s lords. The same applies to the seventh house and its Navamsha; if not, they bring death. Subtracting the Sun from the Lagna, the rulers are the Sun, Venus,**<-
> ->**Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars. These are the Hora lords starting from the lord of the day. In the signs of the cruel planets, the middle portion is not recommended. 32**<-

<margin>The sequence of the Hora lords is: Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</margin>

The lords are: the Sun (Ravi), Venus (Sita), Mercury (Jña), the Moon (Indu), Saturn (Arki), Jupiter (Jiva), and Mars (Asuṅ). A Hora occurring in a sign ruled by a "cruel" <note>malefic</note> or enemy planet is not recommended, or is at best mediocre. However, a Hora of a "gentle" <note>benefic</note> planet in a friendly sign is very auspicious. Here is an example: On a Thursday, the Sun is at 1s 27° 8' and the Lagna is at 4s 10° 0'. Subtracting the Sun from the Lagna gives 2s 14° 52'. Doubling this gives 4s 29° 44'. The number of completed Horas is 4, so the current Hora is the 5th. Counting from the lord of the day (Jupiter), the sequence (Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury) reveals the current hour is ruled by Mercury. 32.

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda, Page 53</header>

Now, he describes the method for knowing the supreme planet in the seventh house relative to the Ascendant and the Moon, and describes its effects. Regarding the Ascendant and the Moon: when a benefic planet is in the seventh house <note>original: *smarage*; the seventh house from the Ascendant is associated with the partner and marriage</note> or in an even sign, it is auspicious. If it is an auspicious planet, there is "Adhi" <note>mental distress or anxiety</note>; if it is an inauspicious planet, there is "Mrityu" <note>death or severe ill-fortune</note>. When does this happen? When the planet in the seventh house is subtracted from the Moon or the Ascendant. This means the Moon subtracted from its seventh house position or the Ascendant subtracted from its seventh house position. From that result, using the calculation of the time difference and multiplying by its own factor of two hundred, if the result is fifty-four, then "Adhimrityu" <note>the state of mental distress or death</note> exists; otherwise, it does not. An example is given for a previously calculated Ascendant: 4 signs, 10 degrees, 7 minutes. A planet in its seventh house is 10 signs, 11 degrees, 7 minutes. Subtracting the Ascendant from this gives 6 signs, 1 degree, 0 minutes. The calculation follows... resulting in 54. Now he explains the cancellation of this effect:

## **When benefic or malefic planets are in the seventh house from the Moon or Ascendant, mental distress or death occurs. Subtract the Moon or Ascendant from the planet in the seventh; if the result multiplied by two hundred yields fifty-four, it is so. But this is canceled if the planet is in its own division, or if the seventh is fully aspected, or if the Sun is in specific houses like the third, sixth, or eleventh, or if a benefic in the trines or angles aspects the Moon.** 
## **Thirty-three** <gloss>If the planet is in its own portion or sign, or if it is in its own Navamsha portion.</gloss>

If a planet fully aspects <note>original: *pashyet*; literally "sees"</note> the entire descendant <note>original: *astam*; the 7th house</note> or sign, then the negative "Jamitra" <note>a planetary blemish in the 7th house</note> does not occur. This is considered favorable and desired. "In its own division" means the planet is in its own sign or its own <term>Navamsha</term> <note>the 1/9th divisional chart</note>. Or, if it is in its sign of exaltation, or in its own portion, and it fully aspects the seventh house, then there are "six limbs" of cancellation. Alternatively, if the Sun <note>original: *Arkairavau*</note> is in the third, sixth, or eleventh house from the Ascendant, or...

---

...there are three ways it is canceled. Alternatively, if a benefic planet is in the "water" <note>4th house</note>, the "tenth" <note>10th house</note>, the "throne" <note>1st house/Ascendant</note>, or the "corners" <note>the 5th or 9th houses, also called trines</note>, and it looks upon the Moon <note>original: *ajam chandramam*</note> fully, then the blemish of the seventh house does not exist. These five types of cancellations, when combined with others, result in fourteen total ways to cancel the blemish. **Thirty-three.** Now he describes the characteristics of the aspects: the "void" <note>3rd and 10th houses</note>, the "corners" <note>5th and 9th houses</note>, and the "life-houses" <note>4th and 8th houses</note>. These three pairs are aspected with full sight by Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars respectively. Saturn <note>original: *Shani*</note> aspects the 3rd and 10th houses; Jupiter <note>original: *Guru*</note> aspects the 9th and 5th; Mars <note>original: *Bhu-putra*</note> aspects the 4th and 8th. These planets see these houses with a full "eye" <note>100% strength aspect</note>. Other planets see these same house-pairs with only a partial glance, increasing by quarters according to their position. Specifically, they see the 3rd and 10th with a one-quarter aspect...

## **Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars aspect the 3rd/10th, the trines, and the 4th/8th houses respectively with full sight. Others see them with increasing quarters of sight. All planets fully aspect the seventh sign. The Sign, the Hora, the Drekkana, the Navamsha, the Dvadashamsha, and the Trimshamsha are the divisions. A division belonging to a benefic is good; others say a friend's division is also good.**
## **Thirty-four** <gloss>The 9th and 5th houses are seen with two-quarters sight; the 4th and 8th with three-quarters sight.</gloss>

All planets aspect the seventh house <note>the house directly opposite them</note> with full sight. Now, wishing to explain the calculation of the <term>Shadvarga</term> <note>the six divisional charts used to assess a planet's true strength</note>, he first gives their names: *Bha* (the sign), *Hora* (half of a sign), *Drekkana* (one-third of a sign), *Navamsha* (one-ninth of a sign), *Dvadashamsha* (one-twelfth of a sign), and *Trimshamsha* (one-thirtieth of a sign). These are called the "divisions" (*vargas*) in sequence. A division produced by a benefic planet is called "good." Other teachers say a division belonging to a "friend" <note>a planet that is naturally or temporarily friendly to the planet in question</note> is also good. **Thirty-four.** Now he describes the method for calculating these: the lordship of the signs has been explained previously <note>original: *Bhe-shansha-buccha*; referring to earlier verses on sign and exaltation lords</note>. Now for the calculation of the *Hora*: in an odd sign...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical foundations of electional astrology, moving from the general seventh-house considerations to the specific calculations of the "Shadvarga"—the six divisional charts that reveal a planet's hidden strength. The transcription follows the traditional commentary format, explaining the condensed verses of the Muhurta Martanda.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Commentary on Muhurta Martanda</header>

In an odd sign <note>original: *rashi*; a zodiac sign</note>, the **Hours** <note>original: *hora*; a 15-degree division of a sign</note> of the Sun and the Moon occur in order: in the first fifteen degrees, it is the Sun's **Hour**, and in the second fifteen degrees, it is the Moon's **Hour**. Now, in an even sign, the **Hours** of the Moon and the Sun occur in order: in the first fifteen degrees, it is the Moon's **Hour**, and in the second fifteen degrees, it is the Sun's **Hour**. 

Next, he explains the calculation for the **Decan** <note>original: *drekkan*; a 10-degree division of a sign</note>. A **Decan** is of three types, each consisting of ten degrees. Its "lords" <note>original: *ishah*; planetary rulers</note> should be known as the rulers of the first, fifth, and ninth houses. This means: the lord of the first **Decan** is the ruler of the sign itself; the lord of the second **Decan** is the ruler of the fifth house from it; and the lord of the third **Decan** is the ruler of the ninth house from it. The **Ninth-part** <note>original: *navamsha*; a division of 3 degrees and 20 minutes</note> was already explained previously in the section beginning with "Peaceful lords" etc. 

Now, the calculation for the **Twelfth-part** <note>original: *dvadasamsa*; a 2.5-degree division</note>: one's own...

<insert>
It was said before: in an odd sign, the Hours belong to the Sun and Moon; in an even sign, to the Moon and Sun. The Decan ruler is the lord of the first, fifth, or ninth houses. The Ninth-part has been told. The Twelfth-part begins from the sign itself. For the Thirtieth-parts, the degrees for Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, Saturn, and Mars are five, seven, eight, five, and five in an odd sign; in an even sign, these are reversed. 35
</insert>

<gloss>This means: "from itself" refers to the Ascendant. The **Twelfth-part** should be understood as follows: the 30 degrees of a sign are divided by twelve. The result is 2 degrees and 30 minutes. This is the measure of a **Twelfth-part**. One should count these 2.5-degree increments from the sign where the Ascendant is located until the specific degree of the Ascendant is reached. The ruler of the sign where the count ends is the Lord of the **Twelfth-part**. 

Now, the calculation of the **Thirtieth-part** <note>original: *trimsamsa*; a 1-degree division</note>: "Shukra" is the well-known Venus; "Jna" is Mercury; "Ijya" is Jupiter; "Yama" is Saturn; and "Asrk" is Mars. For these five, the portions are "arrows" (5), "suni" (7), "earth" (8), "senses" (5), and "arrows" (5). <note>The author uses Sanskrit numerical code words: 'arrows' and 'senses' both represent the number 5</note></gloss>

---

...the "arrows" (5), "elephants" (8), and "wealth" (5) are the degrees. These five divisions of degrees belong to Venus and the others. This should be known for an even sign. This also should be calculated up to the specific degree of the Ascendant. Where the calculation stops, that planet is the Lord of the **Thirtieth-part**. In odd signs, the planets and degrees are said to be the same but in reverse order. Thus, the **Six Divisions** <note>original: *shadvarga*; the six primary ways to divide a sign to determine planetary strength</note> have been described. 35

Now, wishing to explain the calculation of the **Desired Time** <note>original: *ishtakala*; the specific moment for which a chart is cast</note>, he first explains the calculation of the Sun's position. "Between Transits" <note>original: *sankrantyantara*</note>: the interval of days between two solar transits. The group of days elapsed from the transit to the desired day is divided by the interval of days between transits. Whatever is obtained...

<insert>
The Sun's position is obtained from the days elapsed divided by the interval between transits. By adding the degrees and minutes to the previous sign, the Sun is found. Then, using the "precession of the equinoxes," the true position of the Sun and the Ascendant at sunrise are calculated. The time is determined precisely by the rising signs and the portions of the signs already passed. 36
Regarding the Ascendant and the Sun: the difference between them, combined with the rising times of the signs, reveals the time elapsed since sunrise. The Sun's position at the time of the Ascendant is refined by the rising and setting values... 37
</insert>

...the Sun, in signs, degrees, and minutes, is calculated. What kind of Sun? The Sun that has moved forward from Aries <note>original: *mesha*</note>, joined with the signs it has already traversed. This means the Sun is calculated for that specific moment. 

Here is an example: The days between the transits of Libra <note>original: *tula*</note> and Scorpio <note>original: *vrishchika*</note> are 29 days and 55 minutes. The desired number of days elapsed since the Libra transit is 5 days and 6 minutes. Dividing the elapsed days by the transit interval gives 0 signs, 7 degrees, 7 minutes, and 1 second. Adding this to the sign the Sun has already completed (6 signs for Libra) results in 6 signs, 7 degrees, 7 minutes, and 1 second. This is the Sun's position at that moment. To this, the "precession of the equinoxes" <note>original: *ayanamsa*; the correction for the Earth's axial tilt</note> must be added, as established by the rules of this text...

<summary>This page details the mathematical calculations for the Shadvarga (six divisional charts) and provides a step-by-step method for determining the Sun's precise longitude based on the time elapsed since the last solar transit.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical divisions of signs (Vargas) to the practical calculation of the "Desired Time" (Ishta-kala) and the ritual installation of the water clock (Ghati-yantra), concluding with the definition of the auspicious "Cow-dust" (Godhula) period.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Bhartanda 45</header>
<header>Commentary 45</header>

<margin>Muhurta Bhartanda 45</margin> <margin>Commentary 45</margin> Having determined the **Precession of the Equinoxes** <note>original: *ayanamsha*; the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</note>, one should add these degrees to the Sun’s position. To calculate the **Desired Time** <note>original: *ishtakala*; the specific time elapsed since sunrise</note> during the night, one must add six signs <note>original: *shadbha*; 180 degrees</note> to the Sun's position. The "remaining portion" of the Sun's current sign should be multiplied by the **Local Rising Time** <note>original: *nijodaya*; the time a specific zodiac sign takes to rise at a particular latitude</note>. This product is then divided by thirty to find the "Time to be Consumed" by the Sun. The **Local Rising Times** are to be understood starting from Libra and so on. 

What kind of time is this? It is the time calculated by adding the degrees already "consumed" by the **Rising Sign** <note>original: *lagna*; the ascendant</note>. This means: after adding the precession to the Desired Ascendant, one should multiply its consumed degrees by the local rising time and divide by thirty. The result is the "Time Consumed by the Ascendant." By adding this to the time consumed by the Sun, and further adding the rising times of the intervening signs between the Sun and the Ascendant, the **Clear Desired Time** <note>original: *sphuta ishtakala*</note> is obtained. If this total is divided by sixty, the result is in **Ghatikas** <note>original: *ghatika*; a unit of 24 minutes</note>. 36

Now, he describes a special rule for calculating the Desired Time: **"From the Ascendant and the Sun..."** The degrees between the Ascendant and the Sun, multiplied by their respective rising times and divided by thirty, yield the time. He adds a further distinction: **"If [the Ascendant] is less than the Sun..."** If the Ascendant's longitude is less than the Sun's, then that Desired Time occurs before sunrise; this too should be understood as being measured in **Ghatikas** by dividing by sixty. 

Since the known time cannot be calculated without instruments, he now describes the time for installing the primary instrument among them, the **Water Clock** <note>original: *ghati-yantra*; a copper bowl with a small hole that sinks in water at a measured rate</note>. **"In a basin..."** In a basin filled with water—meaning a vessel full of water—one should place the bowl, which is capable of immersion. The mention of a "basin" is to distinguish it from other types of vessels. Thus, in a clean vessel filled with water, one should place the water clock, which is calibrated to sixty **Palas** <note>original: *pala*; 24 seconds</note>.

---

When should this be done? At the moment of **Half-Sunrise** or **Half-Sunset**. What should one do first? After praising and praying. The prayer is mentioned in other texts: "This instrument, the primary form of all instruments, was created by Brahma for the benefit of couples to indicate auspicious times." Once the water clock is placed, he describes the results of its movement. If the bowl drifts toward the "Three Fires" <note>the South-East, South, and South-West</note> or toward the "Wind" <note>North-West</note>, it yields specific fruits. Drifting toward these four directions is considered unfavorable, while drifting toward other directions or remaining steady is auspicious. 

As stated in another text: "Whichever direction the prepared bowl moves in the water, it produces results starting from the East. Steady in the middle, it grants wealth. [Other directions may cause] a woman to become a widow, poverty-stricken, or diseased; or she may become beloved by her husband, a courtesan, or respected and endowed with wealth and children." After describing the results of its movement, he describes the results of its filling: **"Filling in the five fire [directions]..."** If the bowl becomes full and sinks in the five directions starting from the South-East, it is not auspicious. In other directions, it is auspicious. As it is said: "Filling in the North, North-East, or East, the water clock is auspicious. In the remaining directions, it grants widowhood to the bride." 37

Now he describes the **Cow-dust Ascendant**: **"Cow-dust..."** The **Cow-dust Ascendant** <note>original: *godhula lagna*; the time of sunset when returning cattle kick up dust</note> refers to the evening time. It is said to be auspicious for those born in the Brahmin caste and others, even if the five elements of the calendar <note>original: *panchanga*; tithi, vara, nakshatra, yoga, karana</note> are not entirely pure, though for marriages the purity of the five elements is usually required. He defines the duration of this period: the **Cow-dust** period lasts for half a **Ghatika** <note>12 minutes</note> before and after the Sun sets. This means fifteen **Palas** before and fifteen **Palas** after sunset. 

He then mentions what must be avoided: even in this **Cow-dust Ascendant**, one should avoid the sixth and eighth houses, and the "final" <note>the 12th house</note>. Furthermore, one must avoid a Lagna occupied by a planet, the presence of Mars, the eighth house, and the days of Jupiter or Saturn <note>Thursday and Saturday</note>. One must also avoid the **Great Omens** <note>original: *mahapata*; astronomical junctions considered inauspicious</note> and the transition of the Sun...

<summary>This page details the mathematical calculation of time using the Sun's position and local rising signs. It also provides ritual instructions for the Ghati-yantra (water clock) and defines the Godhula (Cow-dust) period as a powerful auspicious time, while listing specific planetary conditions to avoid.</summary>

<keywords>Ishtakala, Ghati-yantra, Godhula Lagna, Ayanamsha, water clock ritual, sunset calculation, electional astrology, Muhurta</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological calculations from page 45, specifically focusing on the auspicious and inauspicious time periods (Muhurtas) and moving into the technical specifications for building a wedding altar (Vedi). It refers to the "Muhurta Bhartanda" and its commentary.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

<margin>Muhurta Bhartanda 46</margin>
<margin>Commentary</margin>
This means that other defects arising from the sun's transition <note>original: *kranti-janyan*</note> should be avoided. Regarding this, it is said that "Jupiter and Saturn" are problematic. Due to the occurrence of these defects, the **Bell of Death** <note>original: *yama-ghanta*; a specific inauspicious time period</note> occurs on Jupiter's day <note>Thursday</note> for half a watch <note>original: *yamarddham*; approximately 1.5 hours</note> before sunset. On Saturday, before sunset, the period known as **Kulika** <note>original: *shvakuliki*; a malefic period associated with Saturn</note> is remembered. From the rising of the Sun, the Lords of the half-watches are the planets; starting from the Sun’s son <note>Saturn</note>, those used for auspicious works should be abandoned. This is the *Kulika*. If it occurs during the daytime, it is determined by the specific divisions of the day and the positions of the planets. By saying "this is Kulika," it is understood that on Saturdays, the *Kulika* occurs before sunset.

->The **Cow-dust period** <term>Godhula; the auspicious time at sunset when returning cattle kick up dust</term> occurs when the Sun is half-risen or set. For Brahmins, it is five parts; for others, it is seven parts. This period is free from defects, except for the eighth sign from the moon and certain planetary positions involving Jupiter and Saturn.<-

->Drinking the Sun's rays, it gives happiness to Brahmins in times of distress; it is said to be like a youth in its prime. 38<-

<margin>46</margin>
After determining the specific nature of *Kulika* starting from the Sun and so on, it is concluded that *Kulika* can also occur after this. For this reason, Thursdays and Saturdays are criticized for certain actions. Now, he describes the origin of the "Cow-dust" period for Brahmins and others in specific types of work. It provides happiness to Brahmins in times of great distress or crisis. When a youth is ready for marriage and a "pure ascendant" <note>original: *lagna-shuddhi*; a theoretically perfect astrological moment</note> cannot be found, this Cow-dust period may be used for the three higher classes <note>Brahmins, Kshatriyas, and Vaishyas</note>. Otherwise, it is not used. This is confirmed in the *Jyotir-nibandha* <note>a classical astrological compendium</note>: "When a calculated time-period is unavailable, then the Cow-dust period is remembered. The wise say it is for Shudras and never for the Twice-born <note>the three upper castes</note>." 1. However, if a pure ascendant is not available and the marriage must happen, then the Cow-dust period is auspicious for all classes. 38 <unclear>break markers</unclear>

---

Now he describes the characteristics of the **Wedding Altar** <note>original: *udvaha-vedi*</note>. It should be **sloping toward the east** <note>original: *prak-pravana*</note>, meaning the eastern side is lower. It should measure **four excellent hands** <note>original: *chatur-vara-kara*; a 'hand' or cubit is the length from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>. It should be **one hand high** <note>original: *hastocchrita*; about 18 inches high</note>. This is the measurement prescribed for the highest class, the Brahmins. That same altar for the "arm-born" <note>original: *dor-jadaih*; Kshatriyas or warriors</note> and others should be successively reduced by two, three, and four "finger-widths" <note>original: *angula*</note>. Thus, for a Kshatriya, it is two fingers less; for a Vaishya, three fingers less; and for a Shudra, it is four fingers less. He states where this should be made: **outside to the left** <note>original: *bahir-vamatah*</note> of the house. The measurement of a finger-width is described in the *Markandeya Purana* <note>an ancient scripture</note>: "An atom is the most subtle, followed by a mote of dust in a sunbeam, then the tip of a hair..."

->The altar slopes east, measures four cubits, and is one cubit high for the highest class. For the arm-born <note>Kshatriyas</note> and others, it is reduced by two, three, or four fingers. It is said to be placed outside to the left.<-

Next is the **barley-belly** <note>original: *yavodara*; a unit of measure based on the width of a grain of barley</note>. They say eight grains of barley placed side-by-side make one finger-width. Six finger-widths make a "foot" <note>original: *pada*</note>, which is used for achieving success. The sage Markandeya says the "hand" <note>cubit</note> is of two types. In other traditional texts <note>original: *agamantare*</note>, the measurement of a "measure-finger" <note>original: *matrangula*</note> is said to be the middle joint of the middle finger of the person performing the rite. Elsewhere it is said: whatever the length of a cow’s ear, divide it into ten parts, then into eleven; the eighth part is a finger-width. This is known as the "body-finger" <note>original: *dehalabdhyangula*</note>. The "great-measure finger" is what has been called the "measure-finger" here, used for measuring temples and other structures.

<summary>This page defines the Cow-dust (Godhula) period as an alternative auspicious time for weddings when a perfect astrological alignment is unavailable. It also provides precise architectural measurements for a wedding altar (Vedi) based on the social class of the practitioner and ancient units of length.</summary>
<keywords>Godhula, Cow-dust period, Kulika, Yama-ghanta, Vedi, Altar construction, Angula, Finger-width, Markandeya Purana, Hindu metrology, Wedding rituals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological timing for marriage-related rituals, transitioning from the construction of the wedding altar (Vedi) to the timing of preparatory rites (Anga) and the bride’s first entry into her new home (Vadhūpraveśa).</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. | Commentary</header>

One should perform various rituals such as the construction of the altar <note>original: *vedikā-ādīni*</note> and the preparation of palanquins, chariots, and the like. Regarding this, the groom takes the bride's hand because he is the primary performer of the fire sacrifice <term>Homa; a ritual involving offerings into a consecrated fire</term>. One might ask: "Does the bride not also have the role of performer in the offering of parched grain <note>original: *lājā-hutau*</note>?" There is no specific scriptural authority for her being the sole performer; however, it is true that in cases where there is room for interpretation, the bride’s participation is accepted. As stated in the *Devayajana-dīpikā*: "In a place of limited scope, one should rely on a mind that is humble." 

Now, the author describes which lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra; one of the 27 sectors of the sky the moon passes through</term> is suitable for the secondary parts <note>original: *aṅga*</note> of various rituals:

## ->Whatever secondary part is prescribed for a ritual, perform it in its designated lunar mansion. In these cases, do not consider the strength of the moon. One should not perform these within the 6th or 9th day from the wedding day. 39<-

The secondary part <gloss>aṅga</gloss> of a ritual <gloss>karman</gloss> should be performed in the lunar mansion mentioned for that specific part. In these subsidiary acts, the strength of the Moon <note>original: *indor-balaṃ*; usually a vital consideration in Muhurta</note> does not need to be examined. There is a specific exception: one should not perform these marriage-related preparations on the sixth or ninth day following the wedding. Examples of these "limbs" or secondary preparations for a wedding include: the husking and grinding of grain, the preparation of side dishes and sweets <note>original: *modaka*</note>, the setting up of the barley-grass, the wedding canopy <note>original: *maṇḍapa*</note>, the altar, and the preparation of saffron and pigments. 39

---

Now, following the proper sequence, the author discusses the bride’s entry into the groom's home <note>original: *Vadhū-praveśa*</note>. Regarding the phrase "From the timing": starting from the wedding day, within a period of sixteen days, the entry should occur on even-numbered days, or on the 7th, 5th, or 9th days. After sixteen days have passed, it should be done on odd-numbered days, in an odd-numbered month, or in an odd-numbered year. This is what the great teachers have said regarding the bride's entry.

The meaning is as follows: within the first sixteen days, it must be done on the even days or the specific days mentioned (5th, 7th, 9th). If sixteen days pass, then until the end of the first month, the bride’s entry should be performed on an odd-numbered day. If the first month passes, then until the end of the first year, 

## ->Within 16 days of the wedding, [the entry should be] on even days, or the 7th, 5th, or 9th. Afterward, it should be on odd days, months, and years. After five years, one may act according to their own wish.<-

it should be performed in an odd month; during that time, the rule regarding odd days no longer applies. Similarly, if the first year has passed, it should be done in an odd year up until the fifth year; during that time, the rule regarding the odd month no longer applies. After the fifth year, there is no longer a restriction on the year being odd. 

Now, the author describes which months and lunar fortnights are suitable for a delayed bride's entry: In the bright fortnights <note>original: *śukla-pakṣa*</note> of the months of **Vaishakha** (April/May), **Phalguna** (February/March), and **Margashirsha** (November/December). Regarding the lunar mansions: under the pairs of Shravana and Dhanishtha, or under Chitra, Ashvini, or Pushya <note>original: *ijya*</note>.

<page-num>४८</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 48</header>
<header>Commentary 48</header>

The three lunar mansions <term>Nakshatra; the 27 sectors of the sky the moon travels through</term> for marriage mentioned previously—namely Citrā, Aśvinī, and the famous Puṣya <note>original: *ījyarkṣe*</note>—are the ones used. There is a prohibition against entering an "old house" <note>original: *jīrṇa-gṛhe*</note>; therefore, the text specifies a "new house" <note>original: *navīna-mandire*</note>. It is said that a newly married girl should not be made to enter a house that is not new. Regarding the bride's entry <term>Vadhūpraveśa; the formal ceremony of the bride entering her new home</term>, the sage Narada states: "The bride's entry should be performed on the fifth, seventh, or ninth day, on an auspicious day of the week, under a favorable rising sign <term>Lagna; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</term>, and when the Moon is strong." According to the *Sangraha*, starting from the day of the wedding, the bride's entry is for the sake of prosperity; it can also occur on the sixth or eighth day. It should be done on even-numbered days up to the sixteenth day from the wedding. Beyond that, it is permitted on odd days, excluding the fifth.

<insert>
->In the months of Vaiśākha, Mārgāśīrṣa, Caitra, and Jyeṣṭha, on the lunar mansions of Citrā, Aśvinī, and Puṣya, the bride's entry into her own home should be done at night, while the sign is fixed and auspicious. 40<-
</insert>

Regarding the rules of the stars, the Great Narada <note>original: *Vṛddha-Nārada*</note> says: "If a woman enters the home in the same year and same month [of her marriage], it steals the lifespan of her husband; that woman meets with death." According to Bhāskara’s *Vyavahāra*, the marriage stars are praised for a night entry. When there is a steady rising sign <gloss>sthira-udaya</gloss> at an auspicious moment <term>Muhūrta; a measurement of time, roughly 48 minutes</term>, the bride’s entry is performed. The learned say that the "Adversity of Venus" <note>original: *Prati-Śukra*; an astrological phenomenon where Venus is in a position opposing travel</note> is not considered here. By choosing pure lunar mansions and other factors, it brings blessings to the couple and the mother-in-law. As mentioned, the entry should be on the fifth day.

In the months of Mārgāśīrṣa, Phāguṇa, Vaiśākha, and in the bright half of the lunar month <term>Śukla-pakṣa</term> on an auspicious day: with the stars Mūla, Puṣya, the "soft" stars <note>Mṛdu nakshatras</note>, Svātī, the "fixed" stars <note>Sthira nakshatras</note>, Aśvinī, Śravaṇa, or Dhaniṣṭhā, and with the "ancestor" star <note>Maghā</note>, a woman entering becomes a mother of sons. 40

---

Now, the text describes the prohibition of residing in the husband's house during the first month of Āṣāḍha and others: If, following the wedding, the bride dwells in the husband’s house during the first month of Āṣāḍha <note>original: *Prathama-āṣāḍha*</note>, she destroys her mother-in-law <note>original: *jananīṃ*</note>. The meaning of the following is easy to understand: 41

Next, the text describes the marriage of a "remarried woman" <term>Punarbhū; a woman who marries for a second time</term>. Among the Śūdra <note>The fourth social class in traditional Indian society</note> and the "outer" castes <note>original: *antyāḥ*</note> such as the washermen <note>original: *rajakādayaḥ*</note>, remarriage is prescribed. One must look at the marriage stars, the date <gloss>tithi</gloss>, the month, astrological obstructions <gloss>vedha</gloss>, and the positions of Jupiter and Venus to be clear. As stated in the *Jyotir-nibandha*: "One must consider the stars, the setting of planets, and factors like *vedha* even for Śūdras and for remarriage."

<insert>
If a girl dwells in her husband's home during the first month of Āṣāḍha after the wedding, she kills her mother-in-law. In the month of Jyeṣṭha, she kills herself or her husband's elder brother. In Pauṣa, she kills her father-in-law. In Caitra, she destroys her own father's home and her parents. However, there is no fear if they [the relatives] are not present. 41 For Śūdras and lower castes, remarriage is declared using marriage stars. One should look at the lunar date, month, and obstructions. The positions of Jupiter and Venus are calculated from the Sun's star. Groups of three stars: Death, Wealth, Progeny, Death, Sons, Progeny, Misfortune, Fortune, and Excellence. 18, 11, 25... with Abhijit, 42.
</insert>

Regarding the selection of the month and its purification: Now the text describes the purification of the "Six of Brahma" lunar mansions. From the Sun’s lunar mansion <note>original: *arka-bhāt*</note>, counting groups of three stars in order: the first three signify death <gloss>mṛti</gloss>; the second three signify wealth <gloss>dhana</gloss>; the third signify birth of children <gloss>suti</gloss>; the fourth signify death; the fifth signify sons; the sixth signify birth; the seventh signify misfortune; the eighth signify Lakshmi <note>Goddess of wealth/fortune</note>; and the ninth group of three signifies excellence.

Next, it describes the purification of the "Six of Rudra": Starting from the Sun, follow the count of the sages... [technical numbers: 18, 11, 4, 25]. These stars are counted from the Sun's star.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions regarding ritual procedures and the management of ritual impurity (Sutaka) during major life events. The previous page discussed the auspicious timing for a bride's entry into her new home; this page shifts to the rules for initiating rituals and the specific prohibitions that apply once a ceremony has begun.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 49 | Commentary 49</header>

...which means "by the fire of giving." In that context, if death <note>original: *atyayo mṛtyuḥ*</note> is calculated in the fourth Abhijit <term>Abhijit; an extra lunar mansion used in specific calculations</term>, it signifies death. In other cases, it is said to cause a "break" or failure of the ritual.

->**Now he explains the meanings of various scriptural injunctions**<-

A man who has fasted should give the Savitrika <term>Gayatri Mantra; the sacred solar initiation</term> to a student <note>original: *batava*</note> only after he has eaten. This is stated in the *Jyotirnibandha*: "One should give away a daughter in marriage or the Savitri mantra after eating. A fasting person should give it to a groom or a student who has bathed and arrived."

Regarding the beginning of a festival: if a birth or death <note>original: *jani-smṛtī*</note> occurs in one's lineage <note>original: *gotra*</note> after the ritual has started, some say it is acceptable to continue, while others say it is not. Here, "Vaja" means food <note>original: *anna*</note>. According to the *Anekarthadhvanimanjari* <note>a Sanskrit dictionary of homonyms</note>, the word *Vaja* has many meanings: "Vaja means food, strength, or nectar." The rest is easy to understand. It is said: "If impurity <term>Sutaka; ritual pollution from birth or death</term> occurs after a marriage, festival, or sacrifice has begun, the ritual acts may be completed."

**A man who has fasted should give the Savitrika [mantra] only after eating. If birth or death impurity occurs in the lineage after a ritual has started, some say the food may be eaten by others. From the start of the ritual—twenty-one days for a sacrifice, ten for a marriage, and three for tonsure or the thread ceremony—the days are considered heavenly.** 43

The giving of food and other acts are to be done by others. From the start of the ritual, the period for these four ceremonies is as follows: twenty-one days for a Sacrifice <term>Yajna</term>, ten days for a Marriage <term>Udvaha</term>, three days for a Tonsure ceremony <term>Chaula</term>, and three days for the Sacred Thread ceremony <term>Maujika</term>. During these days, the ritual is protected from impurity. As it is said: "Twenty-one days for a sacrifice, ten days for a marriage, and three days for the tonsure and initiation; during these, the Nandi Shraaddha <term>a preparatory rite to ancestors</term> is valid."

Regarding the definition of "the beginning" <note>original: *ārambha-lakṣaṇam*</note>: "The beginning of a sacrifice is the choosing of priests; for vows and fasts, it is the solemn resolution <note>original: *saṃkalpa*</note>; for marriage and similar rites, it is the Nandi Shraaddha; and for funeral rites, it is the preparation of the cooking area." 43.

---

The annual Shraaddha <term>ancestral offerings on the anniversary of death</term>, the study of the Vedas <note>original: *adhītiṃ*</note>, bathing in cold water, and crossing the boundaries or the Great River <note>the Indus or any major river; original: *sīmā-sindhu-atipātam*</note> are prohibited. "Sindhu" refers to a great river or the ocean. Family members belonging to the same lineage <note>original: *eka-kulajāḥ*</note> should not perform these acts until the dismantling of the ritual pavilion <term>Mandapa</term>. As long as the pavilion is not taken down, these restrictions remain. It is said: "The Shraaddha for the new moon, the Shraaddha for the deceased, bathing twice, wearing the sacred thread over the right shoulder <note>a sign of mourning</note>, and crossing borders should be avoided." Additionally: "One should not perform daily Shraaddha, Vedic study, or bathe with cold water."

**Family members should not perform ancestral rites for the deceased, Vedic study, cold water bathing, or crossing the border and the Great River until the pavilion is dismantled.**

When should the dismantling of the pavilion take place? The text says: "On the arrows and the mountains" <note>a numerical code where 'arrows' = 5 and 'mountains' = 7</note>. It should be dismantled on odd days, specifically the fifth or seventh day. It should be done on even days by the wise, but never on the sixth. It is said: "The dismantling of the pavilion should be done on even days by the learned; the sixth and other odd days are not desired, except for the fifth and seventh." Within the same year, two marriages or two house-constructions should not be performed for two biological brothers or for a brother and sister. This means they should not perform the same ritual.

<summary>This page details the specific timeframes during which a ritual is protected from birth or death impurity once it has begun, and lists the strict prohibitions family members must follow—such as avoiding Vedic study and travel—while the wedding pavilion is still standing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 49, transitioning from general rules about beginning rituals during impurity to specific regulations regarding the timing of life-cycle rites (Samskaras) for siblings and the limitations on performing ancestral rites following a celebration.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>

<margin>Muhurta Martanda 50</margin>
<margin>Commentary 50</margin>

This implies that there is no fault <note>original: "nadosha," meaning ritual error or sin</note> in the case of those born of two different mothers. The logical conclusion is that if siblings born of different mothers are to have their marriages performed in the same year, the ceremonies should be held in different houses. In this context, the right to perform the Shraddha <term>Shraddha; ancestral offerings made to deceased relatives</term> belongs to the parents alone, and not to the groom who is performing the marriage fire-ritual. 

As it has been stated: "One should not perform the same ritual for a son and a daughter born of the same mother in a single year; however, if the mothers are different, it is prescribed." The mention of "man and woman" <note>original: "purusha-striyau"</note> refers to the specific distinction between a son and a daughter; if there are many sons or many daughters, the plurality does not change the rule. Alternatively, it refers to the marriage of two sons, two daughters, or a son and a daughter. 

### ### The limit for dismantling the ritual pavilion: after marriage or house-warming for siblings, one should wait half a year or the remainder of the year. 54

Regarding the specific rules for the marriage of siblings: those who know the scriptures state that the marriage of two sisters or two brothers should not occur in the same year, though a marriage of a sister and a brother may be permitted. Here, the "year" should be understood according to the human calendar <note>original: "manuja-manena"</note>, spanning from the month of Chaitra <note>approx. March-April</note> to the end of Phalguna <note>approx. February-March</note>. The phrase "same ritual" refers to two tonsure ceremonies <term>Chuda; the first ritual haircut of a child</term>, two sacred thread initiations <term>Vratabandha; the initiation into Vedic study</term>, or two marriages. The term "born of the same" implies having the same mother. It is also said: "A house-warming and a marriage should never be performed in the same year." 54

---

Now he discusses the prohibitions: After the rites of marriage, initiation, or tonsure, there is a specific period of time during which certain acts are forbidden. In the case of marriage <note>original: "udvahe"</note>, this lasts for half a year <note>original: "abda-dala," literally 'half a year'</note>. This means for six months after a wedding, one should not perform certain rituals. In the case of tonsure <note>original: "chulakarmani"</note>, the period is a "fragment" of that <note>original: "tat-khandam"</note>, meaning three months. 

During these periods, one should not perform the libation of water with sesame seeds <term>Tarpana; a rite of pleasing the ancestors or gods</term> or the ancestral rite accompanied by funeral cakes <term>Pinda; balls of rice or flour offered to the deceased</term>. However, there are exceptions: one may perform these at the Great Ancestral Fortnight <term>Mahalaya; a 15-day period in the month of Bhadrapada dedicated to ancestors</term>, at the sacred city of Gaya, or on the specific anniversary of a parent's death <note>original: "pitrya"</note>. 

It is stated: "After marriage, initiation, or tonsure, one should not offer water or funeral cakes for half a year or three months respectively, except for the Mahalaya rites, rites at Gaya, or the death anniversary of one's mother or father. Even one who has recently married should perform the Pinda offering according to the rules on those specific occasions."

### ### For half a year after marriage and initiation, and for three months after tonsure, one should not perform ancestral offerings with sesame or funeral cakes, except for Mahalaya, Gaya, or the parents' death anniversary. If a defect is found in the groom or if he dies before the Seven Steps, the girl may be given to another, provided she has not been "pierced." 55

Regarding the "Seven Steps" <term>Saptapadi; the most important rite of a Hindu wedding where the couple takes seven steps together, finalizing the marriage</term>: if, before this rite is completed, a major defect is discovered—such as the groom being blind or mute <note>original: "andha-mukadike"</note>—or if the groom dies, the girl may be given to another man. Furthermore, even if a girl was married by force <note>original: "balad-vivahita"</note>, she may be given to another, provided her "womb is not pierced" <note>original: "viddha-yoni," a term referring to the consummation of the marriage or loss of virginity</note>. 

As Yajnavalkya <note>a famous ancient legal scholar</note> stated: "If a great defect is recognized before the Seven Steps rite, or if death occurs, she should be given to another; there is no fault." Another text states: "If a girl was taken by force and even if the mantras were recited, she should be legally given to another; she is to be treated exactly as a maiden." What are these great defects? <note>The text breaks off here, preparing to list the defects in the next section.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the forbidden timings for performing multiple family rituals in one year and the restrictions on ancestral offerings following a wedding or initiation. It also establishes the legal right to remarry a bride if the groom dies or a major defect is found before the marriage is finalized by the Seven Steps rite.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the suitability of marriage partners, moving from the timing of rituals for siblings to the physical and character defects of grooms, and the astrological configurations that define a "Poison Girl" (Visha-kanyaka).</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>Su-Ma- 51</header>
<header>Tika- 51</header>

...the defects are stated: one who is blind, deaf, devoid of ritual action, an enemy of the bow <note>original: "chapasyari," a metaphor for one who is impotent or lacks virility</note>, a eunuch, one living far away, an outcaste, a leper, or one suffering from a long-term illness—such a groom is not suitable. Other defects are also mentioned: one who is a fool, impoverished, living in a distant land, or a Shudra <term>Shudra; the fourth social class in traditional Indian society</term> who desires liberation, or one who is more than three times the age of the girl—to such men, a girl should not be given. 

The characteristics of a man versus an impotent man <note>original: "purusha-napumsaka lakshanam"</note> are as follows: he whose semen is clear, whose urine is non-foamy, is a man; by the opposite signs, he is known as a "bundle" <note>original: "bandhakah," referring to one who is sterile or impotent</note>. Without examining the man, there is a fault. If one gives a daughter to a man of no merit without examination, that man destroys his own lineage and family. **45** 

Next, for those without wives:

<insert>
**For those without wives, a wife made of Kusha grass, gold, silver, or copper should be fashioned by Brahmins and the other classes respectively. For the sake of religious duty, wise men fashion a wife of gold. If born on the days of Saturn, Mars, or the Sun, under the constellations of Shatabhisha, Krittika, or Ashlesha, and on a Bhadra lunar day—this is a "Poison Girl." If the benefics are in the house of an enemy or in the ascendant... 46**

**One malefic planet in the house of an enemy or in the ascendant—this also creates a Poison Girl. If Saturn is in the ascendant, the Sun is in the fifth house, and Mars is in the ninth house, this is another fearful combination; she who is born then is a Poison Girl. Just as poison causes death to those who consume it, this girl is to be avoided. 47**
</insert>

<margin>51</margin>

The method is explained: "For those without wives" <note>original: "astrikaikh," referring to widowers or those whose wives are absent</note>—this refers to Brahmins, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, and Shudras. In order, they should use a wife made of **Kusha grass, gold, silver,** and copper. This "wife for the sake of religious duty" <term>Dharma-artha bharya; a ritual substitute for a wife required to perform Vedic sacrifices</term> means: a Brahmin uses a wife made of Kusha grass, a Kshatriya one of gold, a Vaishya one of silver, and a Shudra one of copper. What kind of men should do this? "Wise men," meaning those dedicated to their respective duties. Alternatively, all classes may use a wife made of gold, for without a wife, the religious goals of a householder <term>Grihastha; the householder stage of life</term> cannot be accomplished. 

---

As it is said: "For those without wives, for the sake of religious duty, a wife of Kusha, gold, silver, or copper should be made by Brahmins and the others; or a golden one by all." 

Next, the definition of a **Poison Girl** <term>Visha-kanyaka; an astrological configuration believed to bring misfortune to a husband</term> is given: "Manda" refers to the days of Saturn, Mars, or the Sun. If the day is one of these three, and the constellation <term>Nakshatra; lunar mansion</term> is Shatabhisha, Krittika, or Ashlesha, and the lunar day <term>Tithi</term> is a "Bhadra" day (the 2nd, 7th, or 12th of the lunar fortnight)—if these coincide, the yoga <note>astrological combination</note> is formed. A girl born under this combination is a Poison Girl. 

Another combination is mentioned: If the "benefics" <note>planets like Jupiter or Venus</note> are in the house of an enemy or in the ascendant (1st house). **46** 

Another combination is stated: "One malefic" <note>planets like Saturn or Mars</note>—if one malefic is in an enemy’s house and in the ascendant. These two combinations also result in a Poison Girl. If Saturn is in the ascendant, the Sun is in the 5th house, and Mars is in the 9th house, this is another combination. A girl born of this is "Poison." Just as poison leads to death, the result here is indicated as such; therefore, the meaning is that she should be avoided. 

Next, the results of being born in constellations like **Mula** are given: A girl born in the **first three quarters** <term>Pada; a quarter of a constellation</term> of Mula kills <note>meaning causes the death of</note> her father-in-law. A girl born in the **last three quarters** of Ashlesha kills the mother-in-law <note>the wife of the father-in-law</note>. A girl born in the **fourth quarter** of Jyeshtha kills her husband's elder brother <note>original: "pati-purvajam"</note>. A girl born in the **fourth quarter** of Vishakha kills her husband's younger brother <note>original: "devaram"</note>. By implication, those born in other quarters are auspicious. **47** 

Thus, girls born in these specific quarters of constellations do not just affect the husband but also the father-in-law and others. As Garga <note>an ancient sage and astronomer</note> says: "One born in Mula kills the father-in-law; one born in Ashlesha kills the mother-in-law; one born in Jyeshtha kills the husband's elder brother; one born in Vishakha kills the younger brother." Similarly, in the collection of Kashyapa <note>another foundational sage</note>: "Those born in the last quarter of Mula kill the mother-in-law, and those born in the first quarter of Ashlesha..." 

<summary>This page lists physical and social disqualifications for a groom and explains the ritual use of effigy wives for widowers. It also details the "Poison Girl" (Visha-kanyaka) astrological omens and the specific negative impacts certain birth constellations have on a husband's family members.</summary>
