<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on astrological prohibitions for marriage and other life-cycle rituals, specifically focusing on the lunar mansions of Mula and Jyeshtha, and the timing of ceremonies for siblings.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>Su-Ma-</header>
<sig>Hi-</sig>
<meta>two folios shown in spread, catchword '52' on right margin of both</meta>

The fault arises for those born in the constellations of Mula and Jyeshtha <note>Mula and Jyeshtha are specific lunar mansions (Nakshatras) in Hindu astrology often associated with potential misfortune for specific relatives</note>. However, there is an exception: "the fault does not exist if they are absent." This means if those specific relatives, such as the father-in-law and others, are already deceased, then there is no fault in marrying a person born under Mula or other such stars. 

Some teachers, however, say that the results for a man only manifest after his sacred thread ceremony <term>Maunjibandhana; the traditional initiation rite or "upanayana" where a young man receives a sacred thread</term>. As stated by the sage Narada: "The fault arising from the 'Serpent of Mula' <note>original: "mula-vyala-bhava-dosho," referring to the intense astrological influence of the Mula constellation</note> is cited regarding marriage only after the thread ceremony has been performed for men."

### Thus, there is no fault if the relatives are absent; some say the result follows the thread ceremony; some say the result of the first quarter of the constellation is like Mula.

"Not at all," say some. Even in the first quarters of certain constellations, some teachers claim the result will be like that of Mula. Thus, it was said by Narada: "A girl born in the junction of the fivefold knots <note>original: "pancha-gandodbhava," referring to Gandanta, the spiritually turbulent junctions between fire and water signs in the zodiac</note> is said by some to be a destroyer of her father-in-law."

Next, the author explains what should and should not be done within a period of three seasons <note>Ritu-traya: literally "three seasons," equating to six months</note>. "No two marriages": for those born of the same womb (siblings)—whether two sons, two daughters, or a son and a daughter—a marriage ceremony should not be performed within the span of three seasons (six months). It must not be done within six months. 

As Narada stated: "If the marriage of two children from the same parent occurs within six months, without doubt, one of them will become a widow within three years." Regarding the phrase "no marriage [and other rites]": after a marriage, the vow of the thread ceremony should not be performed within three seasons. As Narada said: "After a son's marriage, a daughter's marriage should not occur within three seasons; nor should their thread ceremony or marriage follow another auspicious event." 

### No two marriages for siblings; no vow after a marriage; no marriage after a man's marriage; no tonsure during auspicious rites; not during the "Three Voids." 48

"No marriage after a man's marriage": after the marriage of a son, the marriage of a daughter should not be performed within three seasons. Thus says the sage Katyayana: "A daughter’s marriage follows a son’s marriage only after three seasons." 

Regarding "no tonsure during auspicious rites": within three seasons of a marriage, the rite of tonsure <term>Mundana; the ritual first shaving of a child's head, also known as Chaula</term> or the thread ceremony should not be performed. Here, Katyayana says: "Within the family, one should not perform tonsure within three seasons of a previous tonsure." 

Regarding "not during the Three Voids": in these cases, the "Three Voids" should be avoided. The "Three Voids" refers to the combination of three seasons (six months) or the performance of three major auspicious rites <note>original: "mangala-trayam"</note>. One should not perform three auspicious rites in a row [within that timeframe], according to the teachings. **48**

<summary>This page outlines astrological exceptions for marriages involving the Mula and Jyeshtha constellations and details the "Six Month Rule," which prohibits multiple major life-cycle rituals (marriages, thread ceremonies, or tonsures) for siblings within a single six-month period.</summary>
<keywords>Mula Nakshatra, Jyeshtha Nakshatra, Narada, Katyayana, Marriage (Vivaha), Thread Ceremony (Maunjibandhana), Tonsure (Mundana), Ritu-traya, Sibling rituals</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the timing of life-cycle rituals (Samskaras). It moves from the specific influence of constellations to the rules regarding the sequence and grouping of "major" and "minor" ceremonies for siblings and twins.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 53</header>
<header>Ti. 53</header>
<meta>image quality: excellent, marginalia: present</meta>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 53</margin>
"Not a minor ritual after a major one" <note>original: "no jyeshthallaghu"</note>—this means that after a major auspicious ceremony such as a marriage, a minor ritual should not be performed, as stated in the *Bahihshala*. However, this does not apply to a "major auspicious" ritual following another. The definitions of major and minor rituals are found in the *Sarasamuccaya*: "The wise state that the first tonsure <term>Chuda; the ceremonial first haircut of a child</term>, the first shaving of the beard <term>Keshanta</term>, the parting of the hair for a pregnant woman <term>Simanta</term>, marriage, and the sacred thread initiation <term>Upanayana</term> are 'Major Rituals' <term>Guru-mangalam</term>. Anything other than these is a 'Minor Ritual' <term>Laghu-mangalam</term>." Therefore, a minor ritual should not be performed afterward. Why? Because these are "blocked by time" <note>original: "kala-ruddha," referring to periods where the astrological or ritual purity is already occupied by a significant event</note>. If a ritual like conception <term>Garbhadhana</term> or the ritual to ensure a male child <term>Pumsavana</term> is already constrained by timing, it is an exception. Thus, the sage Katyayana says: "Before the mother-sacrifices <term>Matri-yajna; rituals honoring the mother goddesses</term> and after a major ritual, one should not perform a minor ritual for three seasons <note>a "ritu" is two months, so three seasons is six months</note>, unless it is an occasional necessity."

<margin>Ti.</margin>
> **One should not perform a minor ritual after a major one, except for those rituals blocked by specific timing. Katyayana says this applies to tonsure and the first haircut, but not to the sacred thread ceremony. These may be done for both siblings if the year changes, or even in times of crisis.**
> **One should perform the previously mentioned auspicious ritual if the years are different, or if the days are separated by the four Vedas <note>meaning a gap of four days</note>, or separated by at least one day. 49. Even on the same day, if there is a difference in the public shelter <term>Janashraya; a pavilion or ritual canopy</term>, the wise say it may be done due to necessity. But for twins, what is forbidden for others is prescribed: their rituals should be done together. One should not give two daughters to two brothers on the same day, nor should two sisters marry two men, unless they are a twin pair. 50.**

<margin>53</margin>
If it is a "Guru's word" or an obligatory ritual <term>Naimittika</term>, then there is no fault in the differing performers of minor and major rituals. The sage Katyayana speaks thus: regarding the ritual of tonsure <term>Chaulm</term> and the sacred thread <term>Maunji</term>, he says "both should be done." His statement specifies that while marriage is a major ritual, tonsure and hair-cutting are specifically what should be avoided afterward. However, the sacred thread ceremony <note>Maunji</note> can be performed. 

Now, the text describes what to do in a "crisis" <term>Sankata; such as war, famine, or geographic displacement</term>. If there is a "difference in the year" <note>meaning the ritual falls into a new lunar or solar year</note>, one may proceed with the auspicious ceremony. As stated in the *Samhita Saravali*: "In the months of Phalguna or Chaitra, if the year changes, one may perform a son's marriage or initiation without waiting for the passing of three seasons." Alternatively, it can be done if there is a "gap of the Vedas," meaning four days have passed. As noted in the *Smritisaravali*: "After a daughter's wedding, one should wait four days before performing another daughter's wedding." Or, it may be done if "separated by a day," meaning a one-day interval. It is said: "A man should not perform a marriage for those born of the same womb on the exact same day, nor a tonsure upon a tonsure." 

---

Alternatively, even on the same day, if the rituals are "separated by a river or a mountain" <note>indicating a change in jurisdiction or location</note>, the auspicious ceremony may be performed. In the *Jyotirvivarana*: "If two brothers marry on the same day, destruction follows; but some say if separated by a river on the same day, it is auspicious in a crisis." Sharngadhara also says: "Separation by a river or a distinct mountain range removes the obstruction." 49. "Even on the same day," if there is a "difference in the shelter," meaning a different pavilion <term>Mandapa</term>. *Amarakosha* <note>a famous Sanskrit lexicon</note> defines *Janashraya* as a pavilion for women. The *Jyotirvivarana* states: "The timing is not forbidden in a great crisis or war for those born of the same womb if they use different pavilions on the same day." 

Now, the text decides between these options: "This here" <note>original: "ihedam"</note> means that regarding marriage and other rituals, the wise should apply these rules—such as the change of year—according to the degree of the crisis. 

Finally, the text gives a special rule for twins: "Not for twins" <note>original: "no yamayoh"</note> refers to the previous prohibitions. For twins, the auspicious ritual *should* be done in the same year, on the same day, and in the same pavilion. This is what the virtuous say. It is stated: "In one year, on one day, and in one pavilion, the auspicious ceremony must be performed for twins, whether they be two girls or two boys." Because the logic is equal for them, if there is a girl and a boy among the twins, one should finish the girl's ritual first and then perform the boy's ritual.

<summary>This page outlines the timing restrictions for Hindu life-cycle rituals, distinguishing between major rituals like marriage and minor ones like tonsure. It provides exceptions for siblings based on time gaps or location changes and mandates that twins should have their ceremonies performed together.</summary>
<keywords>Samskara, Muhurta, marriage rules, twins, Katyayana, tonsure, sacred thread, ritual timing, crisis exceptions</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions regarding the timing and conditions for marriages, moving from the sequence of rituals for siblings to prohibitions on specific family exchanges and the impact of a death in the family on wedding scheduling.</meta>

<page-num>54, 55</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda, Commentary</header>

Regarding the performance of simultaneous marriages: next, the text explains the prohibition of reciprocal or exchange marriages. **"One should not..."** <note>original: "naikasya..."</note> — this means a single father should not give two daughters to the same man. Likewise, two brothers born of the same womb should not marry two sisters. Furthermore, one should not perform a "mutual marriage" <term>Pratyudvaha; a reciprocal exchange where a brother and sister from one family marry a sister and brother from another</term>. However, in times of great distress or necessity, the text says one "may perform it." Therefore, the rule "one should not..." applies when there is no lack of resources; if other brides or grooms are unavailable, it may be done. As the sage Narada states: "A reciprocal marriage should not be performed; two daughters should not be given to one man; two men born of the same parents should not marry two daughters of another single set of parents," and so on.

### The determination of Adversity
Next, the text explains the "Determination of Adversity" <term>Pratikula-nirnaya; the period of ritual impurity or inauspiciousness following a death</term>. Even if a marriage has been decided upon, wise men say it should not be conducted if there is an "outflowing of blood" <note>original: "asrik-kranti"; a poetic term for death</note> of the parents.

### Even if the marriage is settled, the wise say it should not be performed if there is a death among the parents, the bride, the son, a brother, or an unmarried sister. The same applies to the death of a paternal uncle.

The "outflowing of life-breaths" <note>original: "prananam utkranti"</note> of these relatives creates a state of adversity. Which parents? This refers to the mother and father of both the groom and the bride. In the case of a second marriage or subsequent marriages, the death of a previous wife must also be considered. Similarly, if a son, a biological brother, an unmarried sister <term>Anudhita Swasri</term>, or a paternal uncle <term>Pitri-vya; the father's brother</term> passes away, the wedding must be postponed. As the sage Shaunaka says: "The father, mother, paternal uncle, and biological brother of both the groom and the bride—if these face adversity, it provides an obstacle to the ceremony." Furthermore:

---

The father, grandfather, mother, grandmother, paternal uncle's wife, son, brother, and unmarried sister—the death of any of these is considered "Adversity" <note>original: "Pratikulam"</note> by the wise. Some also include other obstacles. Now, the text discusses the timing for others:

### Others suggest that even if there is a death in the lineage, the marriage may be performed after a delay of half a year, a quarter year, or half of that, provided a "Ganesha Peace Ritual" is performed. 51

Other wise authorities say that even if there is a death in one’s own family line, the marriage may eventually proceed. As the sage Bhrigu states: "If a death occurs in either family after the oral promise of marriage <term>Vagdana; formal betrothal</term>, the wedding should not be performed immediately due to the defect of 'loss of kin'."

In times of necessity, the rule for the "end of the loss" is as follows: If the father, mother, wife, son, or another close relative <term>Kulaja; born of the same lineage</term> passes away, the wedding may proceed after specific intervals if a Peace Ritual for Ganesha <term>Heramba Shanti</term> is performed. 

The intervals are interpreted as follows:
- If the groom's or bride's **father** dies: wait one year <note>original: "abda"</note>.
- If the **mother** dies: wait half a year (6 months).
- If a **wife** dies (for a man seeking a second marriage): wait a quarter of a year (3 months).
- If a **son** dies: wait half of that (1.5 months or 45 days).
- If **another** relative of the lineage dies: wait half of that again (approximately 23 days).

Regarding brothers, the rule is the same as for a son—one should wait 1.5 months. As stated in the *Smriti Saravali*: "One year for the father, half of that for the mother, three months for the wife, and half of that for the brother or son."

<summary>This page outlines prohibitions on reciprocal "exchange" marriages and defines the mandatory waiting periods (Adversity) for a wedding following the death of various family members.</summary>
<keywords>marriage laws, Pratyudvaha, Pratikula, mourning periods, Heramba Shanti, family lineage, Hindu ritual law</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Determination of Adversity" (Pratikula-nirnaya). Having previously established that a parent's death halts wedding preparations, it now explores the specific duration of this ritual impurity and the emergency measures—such as the Shanti and Kushmanda rituals—that allow a family to proceed with a marriage under dire circumstances or specific exceptions.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda 55 / Commentary 55</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 55</margin>
<margin>टी. ५५</margin>

For close blood relatives <term>Sapindas; those sharing the same funeral cake</term>, the period of ritual impurity <term>Ashaucha</term> is said to last a month. In such a case, another legal text states that a marriage ceremony <term>Lagna</term> is permitted only after performing a pacification ritual <term>Shantikam</term>. When a crisis arises, the yogi Yajnavalkya <note>A famous ancient sage and legal authority</note> has prescribed a pacification ritual for Lord Ganesha; after performing that, one may proceed with auspicious acts. According to the text *Jyotir-prakasha*, one who performs these acts without the pacification ritual during a terrible period of prohibition will face "interruption of satisfaction" <note>original: "tripti-bhangam"; referring to the spiritual failure of the rite</note> at every step. Even in adverse circumstances, a marriage may be performed after a month has passed, provided one performs the pacification and repeats the betrothal <term>Vagdana</term>. This specifically applies to a "mature maiden" <term>Praudha; an older bride whose marriage is urgent</term> following the death of her parents.

The text further describes situations where the "adversity" rule does not apply. In times of fear—such as famine or danger from the state—or when a great deal of time has passed, or if there is uncertainty regarding the death of the parents, the "adversity" does not affect the other sons. As it is said:

<centered>**When there is doubt regarding the death of parents during a time of crisis, there is no "adversity" for the sons, nor for those who are detached or far away.**</centered>

According to the *Jyotir-sagara*, during famine, national collapse, or when the lives of parents are in danger, even for a mature maiden, no other "favorable" time may be available. Here, the author notes a distinction: there is no "adversity" caused by those who are long-term invalids <note>original: "amayi"</note>, those who have renounced their homes <note>original: "virakta"</note>, or those who have gone to a distant land. The dictionary *Amarakosha* defines "distant" <note>original: "arara"</note> as being far away. Legal tradition confirms: "Adversity does not exist for one afflicted by long-term illness, one residing in a distant land, or one living as an indifferent ascetic."

Next, the author discusses other scriptural points. Regarding the "fourth person" <note>original: "aturya"; meaning the fourth generation from the common ancestor</note>, if the funeral rites for a deceased man have not been completed due to some reason, one should not perform the "Ancestral Invocation" <term>Nandi-mukha; a ritual honoring ancestors before a wedding</term>. This applies to members of the same paternal clan <term>Sagotra</term>. As the commentator Medhatithi states: "One should not perform an auspicious rite until the funeral rites are finished, up to the fourth generation; from the fifth generation onwards, it is auspicious."

Furthermore, one should not perform a marriage for someone suffering from a fever. As the sage Garga says: "One should not perform an auspicious ceremony for someone whose fever is increasing. Only after the defect has left the body and they are healthy should they practice their religious duties." This refers to the onset of a terrible fever in the body of a family member. According to the physician Vagbhata, synonyms for fever include "the union of sins," "disease-transformation," and "misery." Garga confirms that if someone in the house has a severe fever, the wise determine that auspicious works should not be performed.

> **If the funeral rites for the fourth generation are incomplete, do not perform the Nandi-mukha ritual; nor should one perform a marriage for one suffering from fever. This is the certainty. (52)**

Next, the text describes what to do in a "great crisis" <term>Mahat-sankata</term>. If a ritual—such as a sacrifice, wedding, tonsure, or initiation—has not yet begun and a period of birth-impurity <term>Sutaka</term> occurs, one should perform the "Kushmanda Ghee Offering" <term>Kushmanda-ghrita-homa</term>. This involves an offering of clarified butter while reciting the three Vedic verses beginning with "Deva hedanam..." <note>A Vedic prayer for the removal of sins</note>. By doing this, one may conduct the ceremony even within the ten days of birth-impurity. This is based on the legal tradition: "When a crisis and birth-impurity arrive together, one should offer the Kushmanda sacrifice, give a milch cow, and may then proceed with the tonsure, initiation, wedding, or consecration."

<summary>This page discusses how to handle wedding scheduling when a family member dies or falls ill. It outlines exceptions for relatives who are distant or ascetic and provides "emergency" rituals like the Kushmanda-homa to bypass impurity periods during a crisis.</summary>

<keywords>ritual impurity (Ashaucha), adversity (Pratikula), marriage rituals, Shanti ritual, Kushmanda-homa, Nandi-mukha, Yajnavalkya, Garga, medical ethics in ritual</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on astrological timing (muhurta), specifically addressing the prohibitions and exceptions regarding Jupiter’s transit through the zodiac sign of Leo (Simha-stha Guru). The previous page established that certain life events, like weddings, are halted by family deaths; here, the text shifts to planetary obstacles and the specific geographical and astronomical conditions that allow these rituals to proceed.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda</header>
<meta>Commentary</meta>

<margin>Su. Ma. 56</margin>

In the interval, to pacify obstacles that might arise before the start of a rite, one should perform an oblation <term>homa; a fire ritual</term> with the Kushmanda mantras <note>Vedic verses used for purification and penance</note> using clarified butter, and should donate a cow. By consuming the five products of the cow <term>panchagavya; a purifying mixture of milk, curd, ghee, urine, and dung</term>, one who is ritually impure due to a birth or death in the family becomes purified. 

Next, the text describes the prohibition of auspicious activities when Jupiter is in Leo. **When Jupiter is in Leo** means when the planet Jupiter <term>Guru/Ijyya</term> resides in the zodiac sign of Leo. During this time, auspicious acts—excluding those that are daily necessities—are not beneficial. This has been explained in detail previously. Furthermore, the *Kalanirnaya* <note>A classic text on time and law</note> states: "One should not perform pacification rites, nourishment rites, journeys, consecrations, or weddings; all auspicious ceremonies are forbidden when Jupiter is in Leo." The sage Mandavya also says: "One should not perform public works like digging wells, or sacraments like the first hair-cutting <term>chaula</term>, or the crowning of kings, or other auspicious deeds when Jupiter has entered Leo."

Next, the text explains how to proceed during a crisis <term>sankata</term>. **Above the Leo-portion** <note>original: "hari-lavordhvam"</note> means that in certain crisis situations—such as danger from one's country or the era—even while Jupiter is in Leo, it is acceptable to proceed if Jupiter has passed the specific degree <term>navamsha; a one-ninth division of a sign</term> associated with Leo.

<margin>Comm. 56</margin>

-> If ritual impurity occurs during the previously mentioned time, the wise man should perform the Kushmanda oblation with ghee and then may carry out the birth-related purification rites. <-

After passing the Leo-portion, auspicious work is beneficial if one is South of the Gautami <note>The Godavari River</note> or North of the Jahnavi <note>The Ganges River</note>. The *Jyotir-nibandha* states: "When Jupiter is in Leo, for those born on the south bank of the Godavari who fear the expiration of the marriageable age, a marriage may be performed after Jupiter passes the Leo-degree; so says the Yavana <note>referring to Western or Greek-influenced astrological authorities</note>." Likewise, the sage Garga says: "On the northern bank of the Bhagirathi <note>The Ganges</note> and the southern bank of the Godavari, rites such as vows and weddings are not tainted by Jupiter being in Leo." By implication, one should not perform auspicious acts even in a crisis if they are *between* these two rivers—North of the Godavari and South of the Ganges. As Vashistha says: "On the northern bank of the Godavari and the southern bank of the Ganges, one must not perform weddings and the like when Jupiter is in Leo, according to the traditional teachings." 

Now, the text describes how to proceed in an extreme crisis. **In Aries...** When there is fear of weapons or the dangers of travel <note>original: "astra-bhityadishu"</note>, and one is between the Godavari and Ganges, one may perform auspicious acts even while Jupiter is in Leo, provided that the Sun is in Aries <term>Mesha</term>.

---

When there is fear of weapons or theft, and one is situated between the Godavari and the Ganges, and Jupiter is in Leo but has passed the Leo-degree, one should perform the auspicious act when the Sun is in Aries. The mention of the Sun in Aries specifically refers to the subject of marriage in the context of "fear of weapons." As Shaunaka says: "If the Sun is in Aries and the Counselor of the King of Gods <note>Jupiter</note> is in Leo, the woman whose marriage is performed then will be virtuous and endowed with all wealth." Regarding the sacred thread ceremony <term>vratabandha; Mounji-bandha</term>, it was previously stated that it should not be done even when the Sun is in Aries if Jupiter is in Leo, unless it is between the Sun and the "blinking" <note>a technical calculation of solar movement</note>. In the *Maunji-patala*, it is said: "If Jupiter is in Leo in the eighth house from the birth sign, or in its sign of debilitation or an enemy's sign, the sacred thread ceremony is declared auspicious if the Sun is in Pisces or Aries during the month of Chaitra." Alternatively, the Sun in Aries is a general exception for all auspicious things. As Shaunaka says:

### Verse 53: When Jupiter is in Leo, auspicious work is not beneficial, unless it has passed the Leo-degree, or one is south of the Godavari or north of the Ganges. It is occasionally beneficial when the Sun is in Aries, specifically in times of fear of weapons. 53

If the Thousand-Rayed One <note>The Sun</note> is in Aries while the Teacher of the Gods <note>Jupiter</note> is in Leo, Narada and Parashara say one may perform auspicious acts. The phrase "fear of weapons and so on" refers to extreme crises where no other option exists, such as the suspected imminent death of a father. 

Next, the text determines when to use the "Name Sign" <term>Nama-rashi; the zodiac sign based on one's given name</term> versus the "Birth Sign" <term>Janma-rashi; based on the moon's position at birth</term>. The name sign is primary for: entering a famous country, village, or house; during a fever or illness; for daily business dealings; in famous gambling matches; and for great gifts <term>dana</term> like the "Weight of a Man" <note>Tulapurusha; donating one's weight in gold</note>. In these cases, the strength of the Moon should be checked from the Name Sign. This is done to align the mind and the mantra. For matters of service to a king or meeting a king, or when checking the *Kakini* <note>a small unit of measurement/calculation in astrology</note> and the *Vargas* <note>divisional charts</note>, the Name Sign is used.

<vocab>Simhajye (Jupiter in Leo), Simha-rashi (Leo), Guru (Jupiter), Godavari (Gautami river), Bhagirathi (Ganges river), Udvaha (Marriage), Vratabandha (Sacred thread ceremony), Mesha-rashi (Aries), Astra-bhiti (Fear of weapons/war), Muhurta (Auspicious timing), Jyotisha (Astrology)</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the astrological prohibitions of Jupiter transiting Leo, outlining geographical exceptions (South of the Godavari or North of the Ganges) and situational exceptions (Sun in Aries during a crisis) for performing marriages and other rites. It also distinguishes between when to use a person's birth-sign versus their name-sign for daily and legal affairs.</summary>
<keywords>Jupiter in Leo, Godavari River, Ganges River, Marriage Timing, Aries Sun, Name Sign vs Birth Sign, Emergency Rituals, Vedic Astrology</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions of the Muhurta Martanda commentary. Having previously discussed planetary prohibitions like Jupiter in Leo, the author now addresses the practical application of astrology: when to prioritize a person’s birth data versus the astrological properties of their given name.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>Mu. Ma. 57 / Commentary</header>

In contexts such as determining the strength of the Moon for a battle or a transit, or for the compatibility matching <term>melaka; the process of checking astrological suitability between two people</term> of a woman marrying for the second time <note>original: "punarbhu"</note>, the Name Sign <term>nama rashi; the zodiac sign associated with the first letter of a person's name</term> should be observed. In other matters, such as marriage or travel, the Birth Star <term>janma-nakshatra; the lunar mansion the moon was in at the time of birth</term> is primary. 

As stated in the *Jyotir-nibandha*: "In matters of country, village, house, war, service, or business, the Name Sign is primary; one should not worry about the Birth Sign." Regarding the purification of groups <note>original: "varga-shuddhi"</note> in the *Kakini* system: "In giving, gambling, the onset of fever, the selection of a mantra, and the marriage of a widow, the Name Sign is primary. But in marriage, all auspicious ceremonies, travel, and the transit of planets through houses <note>original: "griha-gochara"</note>, the Birth Sign is primary; one should not worry about the Name Sign." 

Now, the text addresses a common problem: if the Birth Star is known for one partner in a couple but not for the other, how should their compatibility be evaluated? The author explains:

->**In matters of land, village, house, fever, business, and gambling; in giving and receiving, in service, in group dynamics, in battle, and in the remarriage of a widow—use the Name Star.**<-
->**For other matters, the Birth Star is primary for the man and woman. If the Birth Star of one is unknown, one should determine their compatibility through the Name Star. || 54 ||**<-

If the Birth Star is known for one person but not the other, then for the one whose birth data is known, use the Birth Star to check purification and other factors. For the other, use the Name Star to check compatibility factors like class <term>varna</term> and dominance <term>vashya</term> <note>These are two of the eight traditional "kutas" or points used to score a couple's compatibility</note>. Or, if the birth signs of both are unknown, everything should be determined from the Name Stars. 

As the sage Sharngadhara says: "In marriage, matching, the ascendant, and the strength of the planets, one should consider everything from the Name Star if the birth details are not known." || 54 ||

---

Next, to facilitate compatibility matching, the text explains how to identify the corresponding lunar mansion <term>nakshatra</term> from the name:

The letters beginning a name are used. From the first letter of the name, one uses the *Avakahada* system <note>A traditional phonetic grid that assigns specific sounds to specific quarters of the lunar mansions</note>. 

---

Verses are cited to explain this grid, such as those beginning with "a-va-ka-ha-da." From the first letter of the name <note>the *chudamani* or "crest-jewel" name given at birth</note>, the Star is known. For example, if the name starts with the sound "Chu," "Che," "Cho," or "La," it corresponds to the first quarter of the Ashwini star, and so on. 

As stated in the *Deepika*: "For those whose birth is not known, the name is investigated." Some scholars suggest that when searching for the star from a name, the "Nadi" <note>the "pulse" or physiological temperament in astrology</note> should be viewed starting from the star Krittika <note>original: "agnibha"</note>. However, this view is generally rejected. The preferred method is to view the Nadi starting from Ashwini according to the rule of "triplets."

Now, the author addresses what to do if the name begins with a compound consonant <note>original: "samyogakshara"</note>:

->**The star is known from the first letter of the name. In the "Avakahada" system, if the letters are compound, use the very first one; similarly, "Va" and "Ba" are treated as equal, as are "Sha," "Shaa," and "Sa."**<-

Regarding compound letters: if a name starts with a conjunct consonant, such as "Pradyumna," one must use the very first sound—in this case, "P." In names like "Priyavada," use "P"; in "Dvapada," use "D." This follows the rules of phonetic science <note>original: "svara-shastra"</note> where the initial sound of a compound name is the key. 

Furthermore, if a specific letter is missing from the *Avakahada* grid, the author provides substitutions. In this grid, the letters "Va" and "Ba" are considered identical. Similarly, if a name like "Shivadasa" starts with "Shi" (using the palatal *sh*), and that specific sound is not in the grid, one should use the dental "S" sound as its equivalent to find the corresponding star.

<vocab>जन्मर्क्ष (Janmarksha): The birth star or nakshatra.</vocab>
<vocab>मेलक (Melaka): The process of astrological compatibility matching.</vocab>
<vocab>नक्षत्र (Nakshatra): A lunar mansion; the 27 divisions of the sky.</vocab>
<vocab>अवकहडा (Avakahada): A phonetic system linking sounds to astrological signs.</vocab>
<vocab>वर्णवश्य (Varnavashya): Two of the eight factors (Kutas) used to calculate marital compatibility.</vocab>
<vocab>मुहूर्तमार्तण्ड (Muhurta Martanda): "The Sun of Electional Astrology," the title of the primary text.</vocab>

<summary>This page defines when to use a person's Birth Star versus their Name Star for astrological purposes, prioritizing the birth data for marriages but using the name for business and daily affairs. It also provides phonetic rules for identifying a person's astrological sign when their birth date is unknown, including how to handle compound consonants and similar-sounding letters.</summary>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 58</margin>
<margin>Commentary</margin>

This is the intended meaning: in the same way, one should understand that there is no difference between the letters/sounds used in various scriptures. For instance, in the Science of Sound <term>Swarashastra; the study of the esoteric and astrological significance of phonetics</term>, certain letters like 'kha', 'pa', 'sha' are known to be interchangeable. Furthermore, there is the question of names. At the beginning of a name, certain letters exist... <note>original: "nṛddhanaṃ vititha" - this passage discusses the phonetic correspondences used to calculate astrological compatibility</note>. Thus, the Avakahada Chart <term>avakahada-chakra; a grid used to match the first syllable of a name to a specific lunar mansion and zodiac sign</term> as described in the Science of Sound is established. 

Now, a question arises: if a person has many names, which name should be used to determine these calculations? The text says: "The name that is most commonly called." When a person has many names, the one used for the calculation should be the name by which they are most frequently addressed. As the Science of Sound states: "If a man has many names, however many they may be, one should take the name that is most commonly used in daily life."

### Wise teachers have declared that the birth star—if it is pure and free of defects—is auspicious for the marriage of a man, the binding of a royal fillet, the building of a house, entering a village, the coronation of a king, agriculture, the sacred thread ceremony, the first feeding of grain, and the wearing of jewelry. 55

Regarding the "autumnal" <note>original: "shāradaiḥ"</note> rituals: Now the author describes the specific activities where the Birth Star <term>janma-nakshatra; the lunar mansion the moon occupied at the moment of birth</term> is considered favorable. In the marriage of a man, the birth star is said to be "pure" or faultless. It is also favored for "binding the fillet" <term>pattabandha; a coronation ritual or the tying of a decorative head-band</term>, building or entering a house, entering a village, the coronation of a king, agriculture, the sacred thread ceremony <term>mauñjī; the upanayana initiation for students</term>, the first feeding of solid food to an infant, and the first time a child wears jewelry. 

The text *Jyotir-prakasha* confirms this: "The birth star is auspicious for coronations, wearing jewelry, city and house-building, assuming authority, the sacred thread ceremony, and the marriage of a man." In the *Rajamartanda*, it says: "In the spring, in the eighth month or eighth year from conception, one should give the girdle <note>the sacred thread</note>... even in the birth month or on the birth-day."

---

<page-num>59</page-num>
<margin>59</margin>
The sage Narada says: "The birth star is auspicious for coronations, the tonsure ceremony <term>caula; the first ritual haircut</term>, the first feeding of grain, or the initiation ceremony, but it is inauspicious for other types of work." 55. 

**Thus ends the Chapter on Marriage in the Martandavallabha commentary on the Muhurta Martanda.**

Now, the Kindling of the Sacred Fire <term>Agnyadhana; the ritual of establishing the domestic sacrificial fires for a householder</term> is discussed in a single verse. Some wise teachers say the Kindling of the Fire should occur at the "time of the wife" <note>meaning at the time of marriage</note>, occurring immediately after the Fourth-Day rituals of the wedding. Other teachers say it should occur at the "time of inheritance" <note>original: "dāyādya-kāle"</note>, meaning when the family wealth is divided. As the revealed scripture <term>Shruti; the Vedas</term> says: "The Kindling of the Fire is ordained at the time of inheritance." Some say it is not specifically tied to the time of inheritance. In the *Katyayana-ratna*, it is stated: "The establishing of the domestic fire occurs at the time of marriage; according to some, at the time of inheritance." The commentators have arranged it thus: for an eldest brother, it is at marriage; for younger brothers, it is at the time of inheritance.

### The Kindling of the Fire is said by some to occur at the time of marriage, and by others at the time of inheritance. It should be done under the stars Vishakha, Krittika, the fixed stars, Jyeshtha, Pushya, Mrigashira, or Revati, during an auspicious solar or lunar sign.

In the legal codes <term>Smriti</term>, great fault is seen in a married man living without the sacred fire. Indeed: "A man who has taken a wife should not remain even for a moment without the sacred fire. If a twice-born man <term>dvija; a member of the three upper social classes</term> remains so, he becomes an outcast and fallen. He would be living off the food of others and his life would be of little merit." He who does not serve the fire after taking a wife is considered as one who has committed adultery, even if he has only accepted his own wife. Just as one performs the twilight prayers <term>sandhya</term> and honors the Vedas in their proper place, so too is the worship of the fire necessary for the status of the household. Therefore, all authorities like Katyayana emphasize that it should mostly be done at the time of marriage. 

The auspicious lunar mansions for Kindling the Fire are: Vishakha, Krittika, the "fixed" stars <term>dhruva; these are the three Uttaras and Rohini</term>, Jyeshtha, Pushya, Mrigashira, and Revati.

...by those [planets]. Of which two? Of the ascendant and the solar degree. Those which are free from the "water-born" <note>original: "aja" - here referring to specific zodiac signs</note>. Which two are those? The ascendant and its divisional portion. The "water-born" signs are the latter half of Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces, and Cancer—these are the watery signs. The Moon is also water-born. The meaning is: [the ritual should be done] in an ascendant or divisional portion that is devoid of these. Thus, it is said in the *Brihan-shata* <term>an ancient astrological treatise</term>: "If the ascendant or its division falls in Cancer, Capricorn, Aquarius, or Pisces, the fire is destroyed, even if the ritual is performed by one beloved to the white lily (the Moon)." By which planets? By Jupiter, the Moon, the Sun, and Mars. When they are in the son (5th), the 3rd, 6th, 11th, 10th, the teacher (9th), or the centers (1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th). Specifically: 5, 3, 6, 11, 10, 9, 1, 4, 7, 10. In what state are they? When they have not set <note>original: "anastangataih" - referring to heliacal setting, where a planet becomes invisible due to proximity to the Sun</note>, meaning their light is not obscured by the Sun's rays. Furthermore—

->**When Jupiter, Moon, Sun, and Mars are in the 5th, 3rd, 6th, 11th, 10th, 9th, or the Centers (1st, 4th, 7th, 10th), and are not set, but are in their signs of exaltation or their own divisions, and other planets are in the "upachaya" (improving) houses, and there is purity of the two [the second house and the ascendant]—excluding Mercury—[the ritual is successful].**<-
<gloss>With Mercury: 1</gloss>

...in their own exaltations or divisions. Each has its own sign of exaltation... <note>original: "svochchani"</note>. This means the sign where the planet is strongest, or its own sign, or a friend's sign. Other planets should be in the "upachaya" houses (the 3rd, 6th, and 11th). When this is the case, and there is purity—meaning freedom from cruel planets—in the second house and the first house (the ascendant), the rest is as stated before. It is said that the Moon's strength is paramount. So it is in the *Brihan-shata*: "If the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Moon are in the centers, the 3rd, the improving houses, or the 9th/5th, and the rest are in improving houses, then the establishment of the fire is said to be auspicious. If the Moon is in the 8th house, there is fear; if Mars is in the 8th, the wife is affected; if the lords of the 9th or 1st are in the house of death (8th), the performer of the ritual will suffer disease. One should not take the fire when Mars, Jupiter, or the Moon are in their signs of debilitation, are defeated in planetary war, are in an enemy's house, or are set."

...or if they have set. Thus, in the *Samhita-sara*: "One should perform the consecration of the fire when the Moon is in Cancer, Leo, or Virgo, in the ascendant or the 2nd house, and no malefic planets are present." This concludes the section on the <insert>Consecration of the Fire in the Martandavallabha [commentary].</insert>

Now, wishing to explain the section on Houses, he first discusses the "Consideration of the Village" (Gramavichara) based on the name-star <term>namarksha; the zodiac sign associated with the first letter of a person's name</term>. From the name-sign, a village located in the 2nd, 3rd, 7th, 4th, 5th, 9th, 10th, or 11th house is auspicious. Any other position is inauspicious. As stated in the *Jyotish-sagara*: "If the village is in the same sign or the 7th, enmity is destroyed; in the 3rd or 6th, it is good; in the 4th, 8th, or 12th, there is disease; in the remaining houses, there is happiness." Having thus explained the auspicious and inauspicious nature of the village, he now states the rule regarding the directions within the village. "In the corners..."

->**A village in the 2nd, 5th, 9th, 10th, or 11th house from the name-sign is auspicious. Otherwise, it is not. Living in the corners of that [village] is for the "last-born" castes; for others, it brings great defects.**<-

In the corners of one's village, it is auspicious for the "last-born" <term>antyaja; groups historically outside the four-fold varna system</term>, such as washermen and leatherworkers, to reside. The washerman, the leatherworker, the basket-maker, the fisherman, the Meda, the Bhilla—these seven are known as the <term>Antyajas</term>. For these groups, living in the village corners is auspicious. But for others (the four varnas), living in those corners brings great defects <note>original: "alam" - meaning "sufficiently" or "exceedingly"</note>, meaning it will be inauspicious. Thus, in the *Jyotish-nibandha*: "Know that there is a defect for those residing in the corners of houses, cities, or villages, except for those of the Chandala or other last-born castes, for whom it brings auspiciousness." Having thus spoken of the auspicious and inauspicious directions for the castes, he now explains the directions for residence based on the zodiac signs. Starting from the direction of Yama (South), the signs Virgo, Cancer, Sagittarius, and others reside in the eight directions in order...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the science of dwelling (Vastu Shastra). While the previous page focused on the astrological timing for establishing ritual fires, this page transitions into the spatial organization of a village or house site. It uses a system of zodiac signs (Rashis) and phonetic groups (Vargas) to determine the most auspicious locations for residents.</meta>

<margin>
Sūtra Mālā 60
</margin>

Others have described it in the middle; this is what is being said: Having imagined the desired village divided into nine parts, one should live in the southern part [dedicated to] **Virgo**. <note>original: "kanya"</note> This means a house should not be built in the southern part of the Virgo sign. Similarly, **Cancer** <note>original: "karkata"</note> resides in the Southwest; **Sagittarius** <note>original: "dhanu"</note> in the West; **Libra** <note>original: "tula"</note> in the Northwest; **Aries** <note>original: "ajo mesha"</note> in the North; **Aquarius** <note>original: "ghata kumbha"</note> in the Northeast; **Scorpio** <note>original: "kaupi vrishchika"</note> in the East; and **Pisces** <note>original: "andajo mina"</note> in the Southeast. Others—**Taurus, Gemini, Leo, and Capricorn**—reside in the middle portion.

Having thus stated the rule for directions based on the zodiac signs, he now states the rule for directions based on the phonetic groups (*vargas*). <term>varga: the Sanskrit alphabet is divided into eight groups based on the point of articulation; in astrology, these groups are used to match a person's name to a location</term> 

Starting from the direction of Indra <note>the East</note>, the eight groups—**a, ka, cha, ta, ta, pa, ya, and sha**—are well known. These should be vigorous or powerful, as connected with the following verse:

<insert>
Virgo, Cancer, Sagittarius, Libra, Capricorn, Aquarius, Scorpio, and Pisces reside from the South; in the middle, others reside. The groups starting from the direction of Indra [East] shall be vigorous. 1
</insert>

The sixteen-letter group starting with **a, ā, i, ī, u, ū...** is powerful in the East. The five-letter group starting with **ka** is in the Southeast. The **cha** group is in the South. The **ta** <note>retroflex T</note> group is in the Southwest. The **ta** <note>dental T</note> group is in the West. The **pa** group is in the Northwest. The **ya** group is in the North. The **sha** group is powerful in the Northeast. 

The meaning is this: after knowing one's phonetic group from the first letter of one's own name, one should build a house in that specific location. This is because it is well known in the world that living beings are powerful in their own place. 

What are the characteristics of these groups? They are "those whose enemies are the fifth from themselves." <note>This refers to a traditional system where the fifth group in the sequence is considered hostile</note> For example, the fifth from the **a**-group is the **ta**-group; therefore, that is its enemy. The fifth from the **ka**-group is the **pa**-group; therefore, that is its enemy. In this way, the enemies of all the phonetic groups should be known. Hostility is indeed determined by the "lord." <note>referring to the planetary or symbolic ruler of the group</note> It is said in the *Bhupāla Vallabha* <term>a classic text on architecture and kingship</term>: The groups are symbolized by the **Eagle, Cat, Lion, Dog, Snake, Rat, Elephant, and Deer**, starting from the East.

<margin>
Commentary 60
</margin>

---

...the enemy is the fifth from one's own group. Therefore, the meaning is that one should not build a house in the direction of one's enemy group. By implication, building elsewhere is mediocre, but proximity to two [favorable] groups is good. Proximity to the enemy direction should be understood as inauspicious and should be avoided. Thus, it is established that one should reside after observing the friendship between one's own phonetic group and the phonetic group of the village. 

Between the system based on zodiac signs and the system based on phonetic groups, the system based on zodiac signs is more powerful. Thus it is said in the *Jyotish Sāgara* <term>The Ocean of Astrology</term>: "Some say that for the phonetic groups, a house is auspicious in its own place." 

Now, he speaks of the *Kākinikā* <term>a mathematical point system used to determine the 'wealth' or 'strength' of a site</term> calculated for these groups:

<insert>
...the five enemy groups. One's own group is doubled, added to the village group, and divided by eight. The remainder is the Kākinikā. Where this is higher, that place gives wealth.
</insert>

One's own group [number] should be doubled. Then it should be joined with the other [the village's group number]. The sum is divided by eight <note>original: "gajaih," literally "by elephants," a poetic term for the number eight</note>. The remainders are the *Kākinikās*. Whichever of these is higher, that will be the giver of wealth. 

Having thus stated the rules for directions for villages and so on, he now speaks of the examination of the site for the construction of cities, villages, fields, and so forth. Since it is said, "One should first examine the field by its smell, color, taste, and slope," he describes the color, taste, and smell of the earth through the classification of the four castes:

**White, red, yellow-brown, and black** are the colors of the ground for the castes, starting from the **Brahmins**. <note>The text assigns soil types to social classes: white for priests, red for warriors, etc.</note> From the perspective of the Brahmins, these colors are auspicious. White—

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for site selection (Bhumi-pariksha) in Vastu Shastra. The previous page established the directional layout of the village; this page moves to examining the physical characteristics of the soil—color, taste, and smell—and the slope of the land to determine its suitability for different social classes.</meta>

<margin>
M. M. 61
</margin>

<margin>
Di. 61
</margin>

Earth that is white is for the Priest <term>Brahmana: the clerical or scholarly class</term>, red is for the Warrior <term>Kshatriya: the ruling or military class</term>, yellow is for the Merchant <term>Vaishya: the agricultural or trading class</term>, and black is for the Laborer <term>Shudra: the artisan or service class</term>. These are considered beautiful or auspicious. This implies that for mixed classes, mixed colors are auspicious. As Vasistha says: "White earth is praised for the leaders of the twice-born <note>original: "dvijendranam", referring to Brahmanas</note>, red for the rulers of the earth, yellow for the merchants, and black for the laborers, provided the soil is unmixed." Having described the colors of the classes, he now states the tastes: "Sweet" <note>original: "svadu"</note> means honey-like earth is auspicious for the Priest; pungent like pepper for the Warrior; bitter like Neem <note>original: "nimba", a bitter medicinal plant</note> for the Merchant; and astringent like Myrobalan fruit <note>original: "haritaki"</note> for the Laborer. This implies mixed tastes for mixed classes. As Narada says: "Sweet, pungent, bitter, and astringent are the tastes in order." Having said this, he states the smells: White-smelling or the scent of clarified butter <note>original: "ghrita"</note> is for the Priest; the smell of blood or a red scent for the Warrior; the smell of grain or food for the Merchant; and the smell of wine or liquor <note>original: "madira"</note> for the Laborer. For mixed classes, a mixed scent is auspicious.

->**White, red, yellow, and black earth; sweet, pungent, bitter, and astringent tastes; the smells of ghee, blood, food, and liquor are auspicious, starting from the Priest. 2**<-

As it is said in the *Karika* <note>a concise metrical work on technical subjects</note>: "The scents of ghee, blood, food, and liquor are auspicious in order." Through various signs, one should distinguish the tastes and smells. As Narada states: "The scents of honey, parched grain, and meat follow the order; sweet, pungent, bitter, and astringent are the tastes in order." The smell of "pera" <note>unclear, likely a regional aromatic or specific resin</note> is an astringent scent; the rest is easy to understand. Thus the *Karika* says: "The scents of ghee, blood, food, and liquor are auspicious in order. The tastes are sweet, astringent, and pungent in the respective order." 2

---

Having thus described the color and other qualities of the earth, he now describes the "overflow" or slope of the ground according to social class. "Slope" <note>original: "plava"</note> means the downward inclination toward a direction. In a place where the ground slopes toward the North <note>original: "saumya", the direction of the moon</note> or the East, the Priest and other classes should build their houses. Having stated the slope according to class, he states a special rule for the Priest: In "all fires" <note>original: "agnyakhila", possibly referring to the ritual fire directions</note>, the Priest may build a house on land that slopes in any direction. As Bhrigu says: "A slope toward the North or East is desired for Priests and others; a Priest may live anywhere if he follows the circumambulation <note>original: "pradakshina", a clockwise movement</note>, while others should follow their assigned colors and directions." Now he states an alternative view: "Where" means in a place where the heart is joyful and steady, all classes may build their houses. The meaning is: house construction should be done on land where the inner self feels at peace. As it is said in the—

->**One should build on land sloping toward the North or East, starting from the Priest; for others, there is no fixed rule where the mind finds stability, there all may build their home.**<-

In the sacred scriptures <note>original: "sushastra"</note>, it is said that where satisfaction of the mind is born on the earth, there a house should be made by all; this is the opinion of Garga and others. Now, to determine the knowledge of "hidden flaws" <term>Shalya: hidden obstacles like bones, charcoal, or debris buried underground that cause bad luck</term>, he says "with Yama" <note>referring to the South, or a specific calculation</note>. Here, when a questioner asks "What should be done here?" and the like, if the first letter of the first word uttered belongs to a specific phonetic group <note>original: "varga"</note>, a wise man should predict a hidden flaw in the direction associated with that group. This is the method: divide the house-site using North-South lines into nine parts. Using the eight phonetic groups—**a, ka, cha, ta, ta, pa, ya, and sha**—one should point out the hidden flaw in the directions starting from the East. If the first letter is in the 'A' group, one should predict a hidden flaw in the East.

<summary>This page details how to evaluate a building site based on the soil's color, taste, and smell, assigning specific qualities to each of the four social classes. It also explains that while specific slopes are preferred, the ultimate criterion for a good home is the mental peace of the inhabitant, and introduces a phonetic method for locating hidden underground obstacles.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Vastu Shastra (architectural science), specifically focusing on the divination of hidden underground obstacles known as Shalya and the physical testing of soil suitability. It follows the previous page’s discussion on soil color and scent.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda / Commentary</header>

In the directions starting from the Southeast and South, one should indicate hidden obstacles using the eight groups of letters. If the questioner’s first letter is from the groups beginning with *Ha*, *Pa*, or *Ya*, the expert should declare that a hidden obstacle exists in the center of the site. This means that if the initial letter of the question belongs to any of these three, the obstacle is located in the middle region. As stated in the *Jyotirnibandha* <note>A classic compendium of Hindu astrology and rituals</note>: "By taking the first letter of the words spoken by one who has just remembered their chosen deity, the presence or absence of hidden obstacles is thoroughly investigated." The letters of the alphabet—*A, Ka, Ca, Ta, Ta, Pa, Ya, Sha*—correspond to the directions starting from the East and ending in the center. Residing in a house where such obstacles remain leads to destruction. This is further clarified: If a question is asked from the Eastern direction, a human bone <term>Shalya: literally an 'arrow' or 'thorn,' here referring to bones, charcoal, or debris that pollutes the site</term> is found at a depth of one and a half cubits <note>original: "sarddha-hasta", a traditional measurement from elbow to fingertip</note>, which results in death. If the question comes from the Southeast, a donkey's bone is at two cubits, and there will be punishment from the authorities.

->**The wise person should declare the location of a hidden obstacle based on the first letter of the first word spoken by the questioner. If it is from the middle letter groups, the obstacle is in the center. (3)**<-

If the question is from the South, a human bone from the waist down is present; this causes death or chronic illness in the house. If from the Southwest, at a depth of one and a half cubits, a dog’s bone is found; there, the householder will not be able to produce children. If the question comes from the West, the bone of a child is present at one and a half cubits; this obstacle remains in the house forever. If from the Northwest, charcoal is found at a depth of four cubits, which causes the loss of friends and the occurrence of nightmares. If from the North, a Brahmin's bone <note>A bone belonging to a member of the priestly class</note> is found below the waist; if this is a hidden treasure, it brings wealth like that of Kubera <note>The god of wealth</note>. If from the Northeast, a cow’s bone is at one and a half cubits; this causes the loss of wealth for the householder. If the letters *Ha*, *Pa*, or *Ya* appear for the center, there is a human skull, ash, iron, or even feces.

---

Having thus described the hidden obstacles, he now describes the method for knowing the auspicious or inauspicious nature of the site's physical ground. "Pit" <note>original: "shvabhra"</note> means one should dig a hole one cubit deep. Here, at the beginning of the night <note>original: "nishasye", at sunset</note>, one should fill it completely with water. If, in the morning, the pit is still full of water, then that site is excellent and auspicious; it will bring prosperity. If it is empty of water, it is mediocre. If the ground is cracked or parched, it is inauspicious and will cause various types of loss. As Narada says: "Having done this at the start of the night, fill it with water; if water is seen in the morning, it brings growth."

->**One should dig a pit one cubit deep here and fill it completely with water at nightfall. If the ground remains moist or watery, it is good; if dry, it is mediocre; if cracked, it is bad.**<-

The ground should be level and free of mud or sores <note>referring to pits or uneven hollows</note>. Having thus described the signs of auspicious and inauspicious sites, he now describes the purification of the earth <term>Bhumishodhana: the ritual and physical cleansing of a construction site</term>. Having identified the site through the methods mentioned, one should dig the ground intended for the house until the water table is reached. This "extra" ground of the house should be dug until water is found. This deep digging is for the purpose of ensuring the purity of the section or for the sake of building the foundation walls. Alternatively, one should dig down to the various strata <note>original: "stara"</note> of the earth's interior until a solid layer is reached...

<summary>This page details divination techniques to find hidden bones or debris (Shalya) based on phonetics and describes a practical soil-porosity test involving filling a pit with water overnight to determine site quality.</summary>
<keywords>Vastu Shastra, Shalya, soil testing, Bhumishodhana, divination, architectural foundations, construction rituals</keywords>

Having identified potential underground obstacles through divination on the previous page, the text now moves to the physical excavation process, the depth required for a stable foundation, and the astronomical methods for determining the cardinal directions (East and North) relative to the location of Ujjayini.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda 63</header>
<header>Ri</header>
<header>63</header>

This means one should dig to the depth of a man <term>Purusha: A unit of measurement based on a man with his arms raised, roughly 1.8 to 2 meters</term>. This should be understood according to one's physical and financial capability. The measure of a "man" is defined as five *ratnis* <note>original: "pañca-ratni"; a *ratni* is a traditional cubit measured from the elbow to the closed fist</note>. Likewise, the Sulba Sutras <note>ancient texts on geometry and altar construction</note> state that a *purusha* is five *ratnis*, or one hundred and twenty *angulas* <term>Angula: "finger-width," the smallest standard unit of measurement</term>. Thus, these terms are synonyms for the "man-measure." What should be done first? Having bowed down and paid respects to the earth, one should dig. Afterward, one should fill the hole with stones that are "like the heads of monkeys" <note>original: "kapi-shira-tulya"; referring to large, rounded river stones or cobbles used for a solid foundation</note>. Mandavya <note>a sage associated with architectural wisdom</note> also says: "One should dig until water is reached, or until the end of the soft soil, or to the depth of a man."

-> A wise man should dig the ground for the house until water is reached, or to the depth of a man. Then he should fill it with stones the size of monkey-heads. 4 <-

<margin>Firmly</margin> Having purified the field, one should begin the construction of the house, ensuring no hidden obstacles remain to hinder the growth of wealth. As stated in the Matsya Purana: "One should fill the hole with stones the size of monkey-heads. 
<margin>Having leveled the excavation there, one should then establish the direction of the East.</margin> <gloss>The result of what is found while digging the field is stated in the Karika (instructional verses):</gloss> <gloss>If a stone is found while digging the field...</gloss>
<margin>...</margin> ...it leads to wealth and long life. If bricks are found, it brings an arrival of money. If skulls, charcoal, or hair are found, the owner will be afflicted by disease." 5 <note>The symbols "छ" indicate the end of a section.</note>

***

Having thus performed the purification of the site, the author now explains the method for "finding the directions" (*Dik-sadhana*). From the location of Ujjayini <note>Modern Ujjain, the historic prime meridian of Indian astronomy</note>, in the southern direction, the East is to be determined from the midpoint between *Tvashtra* and *Anila*. Here, *Tvashtra* refers to the star Chitra <note>Spica</note> and *Anila* refers to the star Swati <note>Arcturus</note>. The meaning is that the East should be calculated from the center point between these two. The practical application is as follows: if possible, at night, sitting and facing the East, one should observe the star Chitra through a viewing tube (*nalika*) when it has risen to the height of a "pair" <note>likely two units of measure above the horizon</note>. Dropping a plumb line from the tip of the tube to the ground, one marks the spot. In the same way, using the same eye and tube, one observes the star Swati and drops a plumb line to mark that spot on the ground. After marking both the Chitra and Swati points, one should connect them with a cord (*rajju*) and find the exact center.

-> The East is to be determined from the midpoint of Chitra and Swati south of the Ujjayini site; north of it, it is found from the rising of Krittika. North is determined by the Pole Star. If the directions are confused, death occurs. <-

In that center, one should again extend the cord and fix a gnomon <term>Shanku: a wooden peg or pillar used as a sundial or alignment tool</term> in the middle mark. From there, one extends the cord to the western mark; this is the East-West axis. Having described the method for the south of Ujjayini, the author explains the method for the north: In the north, the East is determined from the rising of *Krittika* <note>The Pleiades star cluster</note>. As before, one observes Krittika through the tube, marks the ground using plumb lines from both ends of the tube, and draws the East-West line across those points. Then, the method for finding the North is given: "From the head of the Pole Star" (*Dhruva*), the North direction is to be determined. This method is common to all locations. Finally, the author warns of the fault of "directional confusion": "In the case of directional confusion, death occurs." This is clear. As stated in the divine—

<vocab>Purusha-pramana (man-measure), Pancha-ratni (five cubits), Kapi-shira (monkey-head stone), Kshetra-samshodhana (site purification), Matsya Purana, Prachi (East), Ujjayini, Chitra (Spica), Swati (Arcturus), Krittika (Pleiades), Dik-mudha (directionally confused)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the physical preparation of a building site, including the required depth of the foundation (the "man-measure") and the ritual filling with stones. It also provides astronomical instructions for orienting the building toward the East using the stars Chitra, Swati, and Krittika relative to the latitude of Ujjain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for house construction. While the previous page focused on the physical act of digging and filling the foundation, this page shifts to the precise astronomical timing and the geometric determination of the cardinal directions, ensuring the house is perfectly aligned with the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

<margin>Mu. Bha. 63</margin>
<margin>Ti. 63</margin>

In the work titled *The Lamp of Sacrifice* <note>original: "Yajanadipika"</note>, it is stated that for those living in the southern regions, the East is located between the constellations Chitra and Swati. For those living in the northern regions, the East is known by the constellation Krittika <note>the Pleiades</note>, relative to the meridian of Ujjayini <note>the historic "Greenwich" or prime meridian of ancient Indian astronomy</note>. Thus says the scripture: "The space between Krittika, Shravana, Pushya, Chitra, and Swati—this is the form of the Eastern direction." The measure of a *Yuga* <term>Yuga: here referring to a specific measuring rod or yoke</term> is eighty-six *angulas* <term>Angula: "finger-width," a standard unit of measurement</term>. As the scripture says: "Regarding the plumb line, the measure is seven units; the height is one hundred and eighty-eight, the lower part is one hundred and four, and the *yuga* is eighty-six." According to the *Clear Calculation* <note>original: "Sphutakarana"</note>, the line of the pole and the plumb line determine the North and South; from there, one should know the East and West using the "Fish" method <note>a geometric technique where two overlapping circles create a fish-shape (vesica piscis) to find a perfect perpendicular line</note>. The sage Vriddha-Narada says: "In temples, homes, icons, doors, and especially sacrificial pits, a confusion of directions leads to the destruction of the family."

## A house should be built in the months of Madhava, Phalguna, Shravana, and Margashirsha, under the soft, wind, water, sun, priest, wealth, and fixed stars, while the Ascendant is in a fixed sign and the central and eighth houses are auspicious. 5

This is why the text says "one should move according to the directions." Now the author explains in which months a house should be built. He says "A house..." etc. In the months starting with Madhava, under the stars starting with the "soft" ones, one should build. "Madhava" refers to the month of Vaishakha <note>April–May</note>. Pausha <note>December–January</note> and Phalguna <note>February–March</note> are well-known. "Nabhas" is Shravana <note>July–August</note>. "Marga" is Margashirsha <note>November–December</note>. 

The constellations are defined as follows:
- **Soft stars:** Chitra, Anuradha, Revati, and Mrigashirsha.
- **Wind star:** Swati.
- **Water star:** Shatabhisha <note>ruled by Varuna, the god of waters</note>.
- **Sun star:** Hasta.
- **Priest star:** Pushya <note>associated with Brihaspati, the priest of the gods</note>.
- **Wealth star:** Dhanishtha <note>ruled by the Vasus, deities of abundance</note>.
- **Fixed stars:** Rohini and the three Uttaras <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>.

The construction should happen in a **Fixed sign** <note>Sthira Lagna; signs like Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, or Aquarius, which symbolize stability</note>. The condition is that the central houses (1st, 4th, 7th, 10th) and the 8th house must be favorable and free from the influence of the three malefic planets. Some versions read "in a non-movable sign." The rest is as previously stated.

---

Now the author describes specific conditions regarding other constellations, starting with "Not when the Moon is at the back or front." This means construction should not be performed during the "Back-Front" phase of the Moon. 

The seven constellations starting from Krittika are placed in the East, the seven from Magha in the South, and so on, within the "Seven-Spear Diagram" <term>Shalaka Chakra: a grid used to track the Sun and Moon's positions relative to the building site</term>. If the Moon’s constellation is in the same direction the house faces, the Moon is considered "in front." If it is opposite, it is "behind." A house should not be built when the Moon is in either position. 

As the sage Bhrigu says: "In the diagram known as the Seven Spears, one should place Krittika and the rest. If a cruel planet or the Moon is in front of or behind the site, it is not auspicious." 

Furthermore, Brahma-Shambhu states: "Gain of wealth, exile, long life, or fear of thieves—these are the results, in order, for the builder when the Moon is to the South, North, or Back." This is clearly explained in the *Nṛkti-kanitha*.

## Construction should not be done when the Moon is in front or behind, nor on "Empty" lunar days, nor on the days or divisions of the Sun and Mars. Even under northern stars, one should not act in confusion when the Moon is in Aquarius or Pisces. 6

The days and divisions of the Sun <note>Sunday</note> and Mars <note>Tuesday</note> should be avoided. It is said that when the Moon is in Aquarius or Pisces, one should not act in "confusion" <note>referring to the *Panchaka* period, a time of five constellations considered risky for starting construction</note>; however, other activities may be performed. 

As Mandavya says: "During the five-star period <note>Panchaka</note>, one should not raise the pillars, but digging the field, laying the foundation stone, and building the surrounding walls may be done." 

Now he explains the "Bull of the Site" <note>Vrishabha Vastu</note> calculation: Starting from the Sun's constellation, count to the fourth, fifteenth, and twenty-third constellations. From these points, the next four, four, and five constellations respectively are considered harmful for house construction or for first entering the house. This is also stated in the *Vyavahara Sara*.

<summary>This page outlines the auspicious months and constellations for building a house, warns against specific "inauspicious" lunar positions using the Seven-Spear Diagram, and explains geometric methods for finding the East.</summary>
<keywords>Vastu Shastra, orientation, Ujjayini, cardinal directions, Nakshatras, Shalaka Chakra, auspicious timing, house construction, Hindu astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for architectural alignment from page 63. Having established the cardinal directions through astronomical observation, the text now focuses on "Aya"—mathematical formulas used to determine the auspicious orientation and "spirit" of a building based on its purpose and the owner's zodiac sign.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The Sun of Auspicious Moments</header>
<header>Commentary</header>
<meta>Two folios shown together</meta>

In mechanical rooms, armories, and chariot sheds, the **Lion** orientation is best. In the treasury, the **Elephant**. In places where grain and water are kept, the **Bull** is auspicious; likewise, the **Bull** is favorable in the main hall. In the central part of the site, the Elephant, Lion, and Bull are all favorable. Thus, having described the means of calculating the *Aya* <term>Aya: a series of eight architectural archetypes (Flag, Smoke, Lion, Dog, Bull, Donkey, Elephant, and Camel) used to determine the prosperity of a structure</term>, we now describe their "faces" or orientations. The **Flag** orientation faces all directions and is suitable for doors in any quarter. The **Elephant** orientation faces East and South; this is easy to understand. For a storehouse, the **Bull** orientation is used. Its "face" is the East; this is clear. The **Lion** faces away from the West <note>original: "vivaruna-mukha"</note>. "Varuna" refers to the Western quarter; therefore, the Lion faces all directions except the West.

->The Bull faces East; the Lion faces any direction except the West. These are praised. The Smoke orientation is for the fire-keeper; the Dog for the outcast; the Donkey for the bird-keeper; and the Camel for the harlot. 8<-

This means that whichever direction an *Aya* faces, the house of that type should have its main entrance in that direction. Having discussed the house orientation based on the *Aya*, the text mentions their general auspiciousness in passing. "These are praised" refers to the four orientations (Flag, Lion, Bull, Elephant) which are beneficial for everyone. By implication, the other four are considered mediocre or unfavorable. As the sage Garga says: "Fame, sorrow, victory, enmity, wealth, poverty, happiness, and disease—these are the sequential results of the house doors starting from the Flag orientation." Now, the text provides exceptions for specific cases: for a house belonging to a fire-tender, the Smoke orientation is praised; for an outcast, the Dog; for a bird-keeper, the Camel; and for a prostitute’s house, the Donkey orientation is favorable. 

---

Having explained the house orientation according to the *Aya* calculations, the text now explains it according to the zodiac signs of the owners. For those born under **Pisces** <note>original: "Antya," the last sign</note>, **Scorpio**, and **Cancer**, a house facing the East is beneficial. For **Libra** <note>original: "Juka"</note>, **Aquarius** <note>original: "Upantya," the penultimate sign</note>, and **Taurus**, a house facing the West is recommended. For **Virgo** <note>original: "Abala," the maiden</note>, **Gemini**, and **Capricorn**, a house facing the South is favorable. For **Aries** <note>original: "Ayameha," the first sign</note>, **Leo**, and **Sagittarius**, a house facing the North is best.

->They say: An Eastward face is for Pisces, Scorpio, and Cancer; a Westward face for Libra, Aquarius, and Taurus; a Southward face for Virgo, Gemini, and Capricorn is beneficial. A Northward<-
->face is for the houses of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. In these, having made the openings for wood and brick, it should be fashioned with windows like "cow-eyes." 9<-

<margin>They say a home is beneficial [based on one's sign].</margin>

Having thus described the fourfold orientation of the house, the text now provides a final decision on the matter. "In these, having made the openings..." refers to creating the architectural features—the wood and brickwork for the entrance—according to the chosen direction. The house should then be adorned with *gavakshas* <term>Gavaksha: "cow-eye" windows, a classic motif in Indian architecture involving circular or arched openings</term>. As stated in the *Vastu Shastra* <note>The Science of Architecture</note>: "The door is proclaimed to be of four types; having understood the transition of the site, one should make it beautiful and comfortable with windows and other features." The term "face" <note>original: "asyam"</note> refers to the main entrance or the specific corner where the house opens.

<summary>This page outlines the auspicious orientations for buildings based on two systems: the symbolic "Aya" archetypes (Lion, Bull, etc.) and the owner's zodiac sign. It provides specific directions for different social classes and architectural features like "cow-eye" windows.</summary>
