<meta>This page continues the technical architectural guidelines of the *Muhurta Martanda*. Following the discussion on house orientations (Aya), the text now shifts to "Vedha" (obstructions or "piercings") that can afflict a house entrance and the mathematical formulas used to calculate the house's astrological properties, such as its Nakshatra (lunar mansion) and its financial "spirit" (income versus expenditure).</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Mu-Ma- 67 Di 67</header>

<margin>Mu-Ma- 67</margin>
Having thus decided the orientation of the house entrance, the author now describes the defects arising from obstructions such as corners and paths, as well as the exceptions to these rules. A "corner" refers to an intermediate direction. A "path" is a road. A "machine" refers to a rotating device like a potter's wheel. A "well" is self-explanatory. "Mud" refers to the persistent sludge created by bathing and other activities. A "tree" is any large plant. A "pillar" is a standard post. A "deity" refers to a fixed idol or temple. If the entrance is "pierced" <term>Vedha: a metaphysical obstruction where an external object "pierces" the energy of the doorway</term> by these, it is not considered auspicious. Now, he states the exception: if the distance is more than twice the height of the house, there is no defect. This means that if the distance between the house and the obstruction (corner, etc.) is greater than twice the height of the building, the "piercing" does not occur. If the distance is less, the defect remains. This is certain, as stated in other traditional scriptures. Other texts mention paths, trees, corners, wells, machines, corals, and weapons as sources of distress.

## If the entrance is faced by a corner, a rotating machine, a well, mud, a tree, a pillar, or a deity, there is an obstruction. However, if the distance is more than twice the height of the house, no defect exists. Multiply the house-area by eight and divide by twenty-seven; the remainder is the house's constellation. If the remainder is divided by eight, it represents the expenditure. A house is inauspicious if the income is low and the expenditure is high. 10

<gloss>Katyayana's verse also says: if the distance is more than twice the height, the well, tree, pillar, path, or mud do not cause a defect.</gloss>
In the commentary on Katyayana’s rules, it is also said: "With wells, trees, pillars, paths, and mud, if they are situated far enough away, the door is said to be 'without weapon' (safe), provided they are not excessively tall." 

Next, the author explains how to calculate the house's constellation and its expenditure. Multiply the "house-fruit" (the area of the ground plan) by eight. Divide this sum by twenty-seven. The remainder indicates the house's constellation (*Nakshatra*). To find the expenditure (*Vyaya*), take that same product (area multiplied by eight) and divide it by eight. <note>Note: In Vastu calculations, the remainder indicates which of the eight "Aya" types or financial categories the house falls into.</note> Having described income and expenditure, he now qualifies them: a house where the income is small and the expenditure is great is "bad" or inauspicious. Conversely, a house where income exceeds expenditure is auspicious. 10.

---

Next, the author explains how to determine the zodiac sign (*Rashi*) once the constellation is known. 
In Aries, one should recognize the three constellations beginning with Ashwini <note>Ashwini, Bharani, and one-quarter of Krittika</note>. In Leo, the three beginning with Magha. In Sagittarius, the three beginning with Mula. In the other signs, two constellations should be understood for each. As Garga says: "The three starting with Ashwini are in Aries; the three starting with Magha are said to be in Leo; the three starting with Mula are in Sagittarius; and in the remaining signs, they are placed in pairs." 

Then, he discusses the "fitting" or compatibility between the house and its owner. The compatibility of the house and the master should be joined together as is done in marriage calculations <term>Ghatita: the process of matching astrological points for compatibility</term>. However, there is a specific difference regarding the "pulse" (*Nadi*). The pulse should be calculated differently than in marriage. The implication is that a "single pulse" <note>Eka-nadi: a condition usually avoided in marriage as it suggests medical or energetic incompatibility</note> is not considered a defect here. As Elder Garga says: "For masters, merchants, friends, and in the movement through countries, villages, and cities—"

## In Aries, the three starting with Ashwini; in Leo, the three starting with Magha; in Sagittarius, the three starting with Mula; in the others, two constellations each. The compatibility of the house and its lord is as before, but the pulse is judged differently. The numbers for the house's directions, starting from the door, are one, doubled successively. The sum of the hall's digits determines the stability of the entrance and the house clearly. 11

<gloss>A single pulse is auspicious for servants, but contrary for others.</gloss>
Thus, in the *Jyotish Sagara* (Ocean of Astrology), it is said: "For the servant and the served, and for the house and its master, and for two mutual friends, a single pulse is actually praised." 

Next, to calculate the "portion" (*Ansha*), he describes the numerical values of the directions. Starting from the direction of the house entrance and proceeding clockwise through the four directions, the numbers are one, doubled successively (1, 2, 4, 8). This means: in the direction of the door, the value is 1; in the second direction, 2; in the third, 4; and in the fourth, 8. The sum of these digits associated with the halls determines the specific result for the building.

<summary>This page outlines the "Vedha" (obstructions) to a house's entrance, such as trees or wells, and provides mathematical formulas to calculate a house's Nakshatra and financial viability. It also details the astrological compatibility between a house and its owner, specifically noting that "Nadi" (pulse) rules differ from marriage rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on house dimensions from page 67. <page-num>68</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the *Muhurta Martanda*</header>

The specific combination (*Yoga*) must be performed using the sixteen names prescribed by the scriptures, such as *Dhruva*, *Sumukha*, and others, which clearly manifest when the names are correctly applied. These sixteen names are as follows: **1. Dhruva** <note>original: "Dhruva" — Fixed/Eternal</note>, **2. Dhananjaya** <note>Winner of Wealth</note>, **3. Nandana** <note>Delighting</note>, **4. Khara** <note>Sharp/Hard</note>, **5. Kanta** <note>Beloved</note>, **6. Manorama** <note>Mind-pleasing</note>, **7. Sumukha** <note>Fair-faced</note>, **8. Durmukha** <note>Ugly-faced</note>, **9. Krura** <note>Cruel</note>, **10. Supaksha** <note>Having good sides</note>, **11. Dhanada** <note>Bestower of Wealth</note>, **12. Kshaya** <note>Diminishment</note>, **13. Akranda** <note>Crying out</note>, **14. Vipula** <note>Extensive</note>, **15. Vijaya** <note>Victory</note>, and **16. [Siddha]**. <note>The 16th name is implied by the total count.</note>

Regarding the number of syllables in these names: from the sixth onwards, setting a limit... the first, second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth, tenth, and thirteenth houses consist of two syllables <term>Dvivarna: names consisting of two phonetic units</term>. The last of the sixth group and the seventh house consist of four syllables.

<insert>
Up to the sixth, including the tenth and thirteenth, these are two-syllabled; the others have three. The last of the sixth and the seventh houses indeed have four syllables. Thus there are sixteen houses. Add the house-area result to the expenditure, then add the syllables of the name; divide the total by three <note>original: "vahnibhih" — by the three fires, a code for the number 3</note> to find the *Anshaka* <term>Anshaka: a fractional division used to determine the astrological quality of the building</term>. The second division is not auspicious.
</insert>

<margin>In these houses, there are four syllables where specified.</margin>

There are sixteen such houses. According to the dictionary *Amarakosha*, the terms for "house" include *Geha*, *Nikayya*, *Nilaya*, and *Alaya*. Now, the author explains the method for calculating the *Anshaka* (divisions): Take the result of the house-area calculation and add it to the "expenditure" (*Vyaya*) value previously derived. Add to this the number of syllables in the house's name. Divide the sum by three. The remainder determines the *Anshaka*. In other texts, these are named after deities like Indra, Yama, and Raja. Among these, the second division (*Pastya*) is not auspicious, meaning that the first and third divisions are the ones that grant auspicious results. According to *Amarakosha*, synonyms for "house" include *Nishanta*, *Pastya*, *Sadana*, *Bhavana*, *Agara*, and *Mandira*. 12.

---

<page-num>69</page-num>

Now, the author describes the method for evaluating the qualities (*Guna*): as long as the house's many qualities manifest in the heart (mind) through measurements made by "hands" <term>Hasta: a traditional unit of length roughly equal to 18 inches or a cubit</term>, they need not be further analyzed. However, if the many qualities do not manifest through cubit-measurements alone, then one should perform the calculation by adding or subtracting "fingers" <term>Angula: a smaller unit of measurement, roughly 3/4 of an inch</term> to the cubits. 

Next, the author points to other methods and authorities: **Shri Gopipati** <note>also known as Gopinatha</note>, **Menga** <note>Menganatha the Astrologer</note>, and **Hmalugiri** <note>Himalayagiri the Astrologer</note>. To eliminate the fatigue of calculation, these leading astrologers have fixed the results in their own treatises. In the context of building a house, the owner should have the site evaluated after knowing these results. Regarding this, **Gopiraja** says to the disciple **Vishnu**: multiply the house dimensions by 19 and divide by 27...

<insert>
As long as the quality of the house shines in the heart, it need not be analyzed by cubits alone; if not, add or subtract finger-breadths. Or, knowing the results fixed in the treatises of Shri Gopipati, Menga, and Hmalugiri to avoid labor, one should evaluate the site. 13.
</insert>

The results follow a sequence based on the remainder: 27, 8, 216, 8, 2, 21, 13, 5, 24, 16, 8, 27, 19, 11, 3, 22, 14, 6, 25, 17, 9, 1, 20, 12, 4, 23, 17, 7, 26, 18, 10. The meaning of this is as follows: Gopiraja addresses his student named Vishnu, saying: "O Vishnu, multiply by 19 and divide by 27; from the remainder, the results such as 'Serpent' (8), 'Killing' (Hana), and 'Nature' (Prakrti) shall be known..."

<vocab>Muhurta Martanda, Tika, Sanskrit, Astrology, Vastu, Grahana, Sloka, Gopiraja, Vishnu</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the naming conventions for houses based on syllable counts and provides mathematical formulas for calculating "Anshaka" (astrological divisions) and evaluating building sites using units of cubits and fingers. It cites historical authorities like Gopiraja and Gopinatha to simplify these complex architectural calculations for the reader.</summary>
<keywords>Vastu Shastra, architectural syllables, house naming, Anshaka calculation, cubits and fingers, Gopiraja, astrological auspiciousness</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical and astronomical calculations for determining the auspicious dimensions (Aya) and lunar mansions (Nakshatras) of a building, transitioning into the practical physical measurement of the site using cords and markers.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda</header>
<header>Commentary</header>
<header>69</header>

...from the one, it is "one-less." That which is "one-less" is combined with the **Income** <note>original: "Aya" — the formulaic calculation of spatial orientation and prosperity</note> to become the "one-less income." When the **Income** and the **Cow** <note>original: "Gau" — here a symbolic number, likely 9</note> are multiplied and the "one-less income" is added to the product of the length and width, and the result is divided by **twenty-seven** <note>the number of lunar mansions or Nakshatras</note>, the remainder (starting from one up to twenty-seven) indicates the fruit of the land's area. What kind of fruit? It is **with the Income** <note>Sayan-ayana</note>, meaning it includes the calculation of the movement. This result pertains to the **Sleeping Constellation** <note>original: "Swayana-Nakshatra" — the star under which the house "sleeps" or is situated</note>. 

Alternatively, the arrangement is as follows: The result produced by multiplying the area by **216** <note>a standard multiplier in these formulas</note> and adding the **Income** should be divided. When the desired area is added to the result, it becomes the fruit of the "Sleeping Constellation." This is how the arrangement should be explained.

Furthermore, the formula of the astrologer **Ganganatha Daivajna** states:
"Multiply the desired area by the **Asti** <note>16</note> and **Asti** <note>16</note>, add the income, and subtract the desired area. After dividing by the **Aga-Shiva** <note>27</note>, the resulting constellation is the Income-star of the area."
The meaning is: Multiply the desired area by sixteen and sixteen (256). Place this result separately. The **Income** that is determined is subtracted from the desired area. If the subtraction is not possible, add **27** and then subtract. In the formula where the result is divided by the "Aga-Shiva" (27), the remainder indicates the **Nakshatra** (star). When the Income and the Star are combined, it is called the **Income-Star** <note>Ayabha</note> of the area. Here, if the division by three leaves no remainder, one should continue the process until a remainder is obtained.

Similarly, the formula of the astrologer **Smalugi Daivajna** says:
"Subtract one, multiply by the **Arrows-Moons** <note>152</note>, add **17**, then subtract one from the 'Sleeping Star' and multiply by the **Elephant-Mountains** <note>81</note>. Add these together and multiply by the **Nabhi** <note>216</note>. The remainder will be the area of the field."
This will be the fruit of the desired "Sleeping Constellation." The width and extent are explained clearly in this verse.

---

For such considerations, one should calculate the desired **area** <term>Kshetraphala: the total square footage of the building site</term> according to the available space. 

Now, the text describes the formula for **Measuring the Site** <note>original: "Sthala-sadhana"</note> and the characteristics of the "twisting" or marking:
One should take a **measuring cord** <term>Sutra: a ritual and practical cord used for layout</term> that is not old or worn, and make it double the length of the building's dimension with two loops. From one end of the cord, one should mark the points. "Double the length" <note>Dvimayamamitam</note> means it is equal to twice the length. A "double loop" <note>Dvipasham</note> is a cord that has two loops at its ends. After making such a cord, mark it starting from the end. This is what is meant: Make the cord twice the length of the previously determined dimension of the Nakshatra, put loops at both ends, and then mark it. Where should it be marked? It should be marked at a distance of one **quarter** <note>Anghri: literally a 'foot' or 1/4th part</note> of the length.

-> Make a cord twice the length with two loops; mark it at one-quarter of the length and at the half-width point. These two marks are called the **Puller** and the **Corner**. <-

Furthermore, mark the **half-width** <note>Vistriti-dala</note>. One should mark the measurement at the end of the length and the other at the quarter. This means: the length of the field is divided by four; that result is one **quarter** <note>Anghri</note>. Leaving three quarters from one end, make a mark. At the half-width of the field, make another mark. Those two marks are known as the **Puller** <note>Karsha</note> and the **Corner** <note>Kona</note>. The mark made at the three-quarter point is called the **Puller**, and the mark made at the half-width is called the **Corner**. 

Having thus explained the "twisting" of the cord, the text describes the method for establishing the site: "The two loops..." The two loops are placed over the pegs at the boundary of the field...

<vocab>क्षेत्रफल (kshetraphala): land area/result, आयाम (āyāma): length, विस्तृति (vistṛti): width/breadth, सूत्र (sūtra): measuring cord, नक्षत्र (nakshatra): lunar mansion, द्विपाश (dvipāsha): two-looped cord, मु. मा. (Muhurta Martanda): the primary text, टी. (Tika): commentary</vocab>

<summary>This page provides complex mathematical formulas from historical astrologers to determine the 'Income' and 'Star' of a building based on its area. It also initiates the practical surveying process, detailing how to use a double-length cord (Sutra) with specific markers called 'Karsha' and 'Kona' to layout the house site.</summary>

<keywords>Vastu Shastra, Ganganatha Daivajna, Area Calculation, House Construction, Sacred Geometry, Surveying, Measuring Cord, Nakshatra, Aya calculation</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preparing a building site, moving from mathematical calculations of auspiciousness to the physical act of "stringing the site" (measuring boundaries with cords) and the ritual visualization of the Vastu Purusha, the spirit of the dwelling, who is said to reside within the foundations.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Commentary</header>
<meta>Mu-Ma- 70</meta>

One should pull the first mark; this is how the logic follows. Having placed two **pegs** <note>original: "shanku" — wooden stakes used as gnomons or boundary markers</note> at the two endpoints of the field, one should pull the first mark. This is what is being said: in the middle part of the field, above the established directional line <note>the east-west axis determined by shadows</note>, place a **cord** <note>original: "sutra"</note> of the desired length. Having placed the loops of the cord on those two fixed pegs, one should choose a point for pulling and pull it toward one direction. One should pull it in such a way that the cord segments going to both pegs are equal. The **corner peg** <note>original: "kona-shanku"</note> is then placed where that corner mark falls.

**Having placed the two loops on the pegs at the limits of the field, one should pull the first [mark]; this is the corner peg. By swapping the ends of the cord, two other [pegs] are placed. 14**

At that spot on the earth, one should drive in the pegs according to the ritual method. This is what is meant: pull that same cord-mark toward the other direction and fix a corner peg there. Thus, having explained the method for establishing two corners, he explains the method for the other two corners by saying "by swapping." By swapping the two ends of the cord—that is, by holding them in the opposite positions—two other corner pegs should be driven in. This is what is said: after crossing the ends of the cord and pulling as before, one should drive in the pegs at the two remaining corners. 14 <note>This describes a geometric method for creating a perfect rectangle or square using a cord of fixed length, similar to the ancient Sulba Sutras.</note> 6 6 6 6 6

---

Thus, having explained the preparation of the field, he explains the "laying of the cord" by saying "from the Southeast." Starting from the Southeast <note>original: "Agneya" — the direction of the fire god, Agni</note>, one should firmly lay the cord in a clockwise direction. In the middle of the field, one should meditate on the **Vastu Purusha** <note>the "spirit of the site," visualized as a man lying facedown or on his side within the earth</note> as being situated on his left side, with his head positioned according to the rules. Now, one might ask, how should one meditate on this person? He is "side-lying." Just as the short and middle parts are filled in the first [half], so too should it be in the other. The West should be filled repeatedly with heavy stones, while the rest is filled with light ones; this is the rule. What is his nature? He consists of "eight-Ashvin" parts. "Eight-Ashvin" means parts numbering eighteen <note>original: "Ashta-ashvin" — Ashvins are the twins (2), and 8, often interpreted as 18 in this context</note>. The meaning is this: having imagined the measurement of the field as the body of the Vastu Purusha, one should then divide it into twenty parts. Thus, imagining the "eight-Ashvin" (eighteen) parts, and leaving aside seventeen parts measured in finger-breadths from the tail, the head and the navel...

**Starting from the Southeast, one should firmly lay the cord in a clockwise direction. Meditating on the Person lying on his left side in the middle, leaving seventeen parts from the tail to the head, one should fill the navel and the left side with stones. 15**

<margin>From the navel; near the navel.</margin> On the left side, one should fill the space equal to the part mentioned with stones. And thus, **Brahma-Shambhu** says the "laying of the cord" is the laying of the foundation stone or the raising of the pillar. Likewise, **Kashyapa** says it should be done between the East and South. **Narada** says: In the months beginning with Nabhasya <note>approximately August/September</note>, in groups of three months each, his head lies in directions starting with the East; he lies on his left side and rotates clockwise. Thus it is said in the **Shilpa Shastra** <note>the traditional science of sculpture and architecture</note>: "One who desires long life and offspring should perform the digging on the head, the tail, the left belly, or the back of the Vastu; the excavation in the left belly is auspicious." Having determined the dimensions of the building field, in its left belly, one should perform the [ritual].

<term>kshetra</term> — the sacred or physical field/plot.
<term>shanku</term> — a peg or gnomon used for measurement.
<term>rajju</term> — a measuring cord.
<term>vastu purusha</term> — the personification of the site's energy and geometry.
<term>shulba sutra</term> — ancient texts detailing geometric construction.
<term>agneya</term> — the Southeast direction.
<term>dakshina</term> — the South or the "right-hand" path.
<term>nyasa</term> — the ritual placement or consecration of an object.

It explains how to divide the site into parts to determine where the foundation stones and "navel" of the house should be placed for maximum prosperity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for laying out a construction site according to Vastu Shastra (traditional Indian architecture). While the previous page focused on using cords to mark the corners, this page describes the creation of the central ritual peg (shanku) and the specific physical movements required of the owner based on their social class.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma.</header>
<header>Commentary</header>
<meta>Translator's note: "Su.Ma" likely refers to the Svarodaya Martanda, a text on divination and architecture.</meta>

<margin>Su. Ma. 71</margin>

...one should fill with arrows <note>original: "shara" — used here as a unit of measurement or metaphorical filler</note>, and so Lalla <note>a famous 8th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note> says: "One should discard two parts of the ten sections of the head and seventeen parts... one should recognize the **navel** <note>the geometric center of the site</note> in the middle, and there one should establish the **peg** <note>original: "shanku" — a ritual wooden stake or gnomon</note>." Where the head of the unstable ground-spirit <note>the Vastu Purusha</note> lies, one should multiply by the cubits <note>original: "kara" — a measurement from elbow to fingertip</note>, the length or breadth of the men... 15. Now he describes the placement of the peg: "At the proper time..." Here, in the area of the "belly" of the site, the peg—represented as a man—must be placed at an auspicious moment. Regarding the question of its size, he gives the proportions: From the **Brahmin** <note>priestly class</note> downward, the sizes are established according to the classes. **24**, **20**, **18**, and **12** are the numbers of digits <note>original: "angula" — roughly the width of a finger</note> for the peg to be installed. This is what is said: A Brahmin should install a peg of 24 digits; a **Kshatriya** <note>warrior/ruling class</note> one of 20 digits; a **Vaishya** <note>merchant/landowner class</note> one of eighteen;

<margin>71</margin>

-> At the auspicious time, one should install the peg made from prescribed trees: 24, 20, 18, and 12 digits in length, divided into three parts: square, octagonal, and circular. <-

and a **Shudra** <note>laborer class</note> should install a peg measuring 12 digits. What are its characteristics? It is "born from a prescribed tree"—a tree mentioned in the sacred scriptures. Furthermore, it should be "divided into three parts: square, octagonal, and circular." This means: having divided the peg of the appropriate length into three sections, the first part <note>the base</note> should be **square**, the second part <note>the middle</note> should be **octagonal** <note>original: "ashrashra"</note>, and the third part <note>the top</note> should be **circular** <note>original: "anastra"</note> and smooth. As it is said elsewhere: "The peg measurements for Brahmins and the others should be 24, 20, and 18 digits respectively, adorned with gold and gems. It should be made from Khadira, Arjuna, or Shala wood, or perhaps from Sandalwood, Palasha, Red Shala, Nimba, Karanja, Kutaja, Bamboo, or Bilva trees. The peg should be divided into three...

---

...parts: the first square, the next octagonal, and the third part circular and soft. Having prepared it with these characteristics on an auspicious day..." Now he describes the drawing of the lines: "With gold..." A Brahmin or member of the other classes should draw a line like a cord using **gold** <note>original: "agnija" — literally "fire-born"</note>. "Like a cord" means one should proceed clockwise around the house site, starting from the Southeast <note>the direction of Agni, the Fire God</note>. This is what is meant: A Brahmin, after touching his **head** or performing a specialized finger-gesture, should draw the line clockwise from the Southeast. Similarly, a Kshatriya should draw the line after touching his **chest**; a Vaishya after touching his **two thighs**; and a Shudra after touching his **two feet**. As it is said: "Having dropped the cord, one should draw with the middle finger, the index finger, or the thumb of the left hand, using gold, silver, or pearls..."

-> Touching the head, chest, thighs, or feet, the Brahmin and others should draw the line like a cord with gold, and likewise place the stones for the walls. 16 <-

And so says Bhrigu <note>a legendary sage</note>: "The Brahmin at the head, the King <note>Kshatriya</note> at the chest, the Vaishya at the thighs, and the Shudra at the feet—having touched these, they should draw the line clockwise from the Fire-direction <note>Southeast</note> at the start of building the house." "From the Fire" means starting from the Southeast corner. In the *Karika* <note>a collection of technical verses</note> it is said: "One should draw the line with the thumb and index finger using a cord, or with gold, gems, silver, pearls, or grain-fruits and flowers. **Never** use a weapon, nor iron, nor ash, nor pieces of wood, nor should it be done counter-clockwise or crookedly, as these show faults. Drawing with a **weapon** leads to death by a weapon..." and so on. Similarly, in the *Brihad-Vastu-Paddhati* <note>The Great Manual of Architecture</note>: "Drawing with a weapon causes death by weapons; with iron, imprisonment; with ash, fear of fire; with grass, fear of thieves; with wood, fear of the government; and drawing crookedly or with the feet causes fear of enemies. Using Kusha grass, misshapen tools, leather, or bone causes ruin to the builder and enmity...

<summary>This page details the ritual construction of the central peg (shanku), specifying its wood types, its three-part geometry (square, octagonal, and round), and its length based on the owner's social class. It also describes the ritual drawing of the site's boundary lines using precious materials like gold while warning against using iron or weapons, which are believed to bring misfortune.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical architectural instructions from page 71. While the previous page focused on the ritual peg (shanku) and the social status of the owner, this page details the ritual laying of the foundation stones, the mathematical positioning of doorways, and the ceremonial installation of the first pillar.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

<margin>Su. Ma. 72</margin>
<margin>Comm. 72</margin>

...it should be indicated as seven-fold and clockwise. Now, he describes the **laying of the foundation stone** (<term>shilānyāsa</term> <note>the ritual of placing the first stones that will support the structure</note>): The stones of the wall should be treated in the same manner. This means they should be established in a clockwise direction starting from the Southeast corner. As **Varāhamihira** <note>a famous 6th-century Indian polymath and astronomer</note> says: "Having performed the worship and the laying of the foundation stone in the Southeast corner, the remaining stones and the pillars should likewise be installed in a clockwise sequence." (16)

Next, in the context of the wall, he describes the **doorway** (<term>dvāra</term>): Regarding the length, if the length of the house is divided into nine parts, then starting from the East and proceeding in order through the directions, the doorway should be placed at the third, sixth, fourth, or fifth section from the left side. The sages say that this is the rule for the **doorway**. This is the meaning of the numbers: three, six, four, and five. To explain: having divided the length of the house into nine sections, if the house faces East:

## For the East and other directions, the door is placed at the third, sixth, fourth, or fifth section; it is stated that in all directions, the doorway should be well-positioned starting from the left, based on the division of the length.

<margin>from the left side, two parts</margin>
...having left aside two parts, the door should be made in the third part. In a house facing South, in the same way, one should leave aside five parts and place the door in the sixth. In a house facing West, one should leave aside four parts and place the door in the fifth part. In a house facing North, the door should be placed in the fifth part itself. In all directions, the door should be placed in the fourth section <note>original: "abdhi-lavake" — 'abdhi' or 'ocean' is a traditional Sanskrit code for the number 4</note> according to the characteristic signs of the day, or from the twelve well-situated divisions. As **Varāhamihira** says: "Divide the length into nine parts; the doorway on the left is auspicious. It should be in the fourth part for one direction, the sixth for the South, the fifth for the West, and similarly for the North." And as **Chyavana** <note>an ancient sage credited with architectural and medical knowledge</note> says: "In a doorway placed in a section greater or lesser than the measured portion, there is a loss of wealth and life."

Next, he describes the **installation of the pillar** (<term>stambha-pratiṣṭhā</term>): "In the direction of Shambhu or Agni..." In the direction of Agni—that is, the Southeast—at an auspicious time previously mentioned, the pillar should be established.

---

<column-break/>

Here it is also said that the same rules apply. What are the characteristics of the pillar? It must be **worshipped**—honored with garlands and sandalwood. What else? It must **possess a canopy on its head** <note>original: "shirash-chhatra-bhāk"</note>. An umbrella or canopy is placed on top of the pillar for fear of birds like crows landing on it. As **Varāhamihira** says: "The pillar should be raised while adorned with a canopy and garlands, and treated with incense and ointments; the doorway should be prepared with the same effort." And further—

## The worshipped pillar should be established in the direction of Shambhu or Agni at an auspicious time, possessing a canopy on its head; the wise know the house to be equal to five cubits and sixteen parts in width. (17)

And **Utpala** <note>a 10th-century commentator on Varāhamihira</note> says: "If crows, vultures, or other birds, or snakes and such, sit upon the top of the pillar, it is not auspicious for the builder. Therefore, the wise should place a canopy or a fruitful branch on top of the pillar, or perhaps throw a cloth or a gemstone there." In the **Gujarat region** (<term>Gurjara-desha</term> <note>an ancient region in Western India</note>), some observe the "Time-Serpent" <note>original: "kala-sarpa" — a geomantic or astrological concept where the energy of the land is thought to move like a serpent</note> and perform the first pillar installation in a "void" or neutral direction.

<summary>This page provides specific mathematical and ritual instructions for laying foundation stones, positioning doorways based on a nine-fold division of a wall's length, and the ceremonial installation of the first pillar, including protections against bad omens like birds.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical architectural instructions from page 72. While the previous page focused on the placement of doorways, this page details the specific rituals for the first pillar, the selection of timber based on species and ecological history, and the mathematical determination of a house's height.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>Su-Ma, Ti-</header>

That is incorrect, because that observation pertains to the pillars erected for decoration in the corners of the **wedding altar** (<term>vivāhavedyā</term> <note>a raised platform used for marriage rituals</note>). Regarding that matter, it is only a partial view and not widely accepted, much like the movement of the **Kālasarpa** (<term>Time-Serpent</term> <note>a symbolic astrological serpent whose position changes according to the seasons and zodiac</note>). The serpent moves from the Northeast, leaving the corners; in the various directions of the sacrifice, first the front, then the middle, and then the tail are considered. During the transit of the Sun through Taurus and other signs, if one digs at the serpent's hood, it brings death to the groom; in the middle, it brings death to the bride’s kin; at the tail, it is auspicious. One should fix the pillar in a vacant spot. In the three signs beginning with Aries, the installation of the **Southeast pillar** (<term>āgneya-stambha</term>) should be done; in Leo and others, in the Northeast; in Scorpio and others, in the Northwest; and in Aquarius and others, in the Southwest. This should be observed in the house just as at a wedding. Thus, leaving aside the mouth, middle, and tail of the serpent according to the Sun's position, one should install the pillar.

Furthermore, the installation of the house pillar is stated by many authors to be in the **Southeast** corner. **Brahmaśambhu** says that the measurement of the foundation cords, the walls, the laying of the foundation stone, and the raising of the pillar should be done in the area between the East and the South. **Kaśyapa** says the same. Similarly, **Śārṅgadhara** states: "In palaces, mansions, and all other houses, the first pillar should always be established in the Southeast, and never in the Northwest." **Varāhamihira** <note>the 6th-century polymath</note> also teaches: "Having performed the worship in the Southeast corner, one should lay the foundation stone; the remaining pillars should then be installed in a clockwise direction." It is also mentioned in the **Kātyāyana Gṛhya Kārikā**: "The householder should raise each pillar with these mantras, placing the stones in the corners starting from the Southeast." In the **Harihara commentary** on the domestic rites, the same is said regarding the rows of rooms; enough of this elaboration. This can also be understood from the *Vāstuśāstra Ratnākara*.

According to the science of architecture (<term>Vāstuśāstra</term>), the following trees are recommended for timber: Teak (<term>śāka</term>), Terminalia (<term>arjuna</term>), Sandalwood (<term>candana</term>), Ashoka, Jujube (<term>badarī</term>), Madhuca, and Kadamba.

---

**Nārada** states: "Trees such as the Shami, Neem, Bael, and Date-palm are auspicious for the house." Trees that are burnt, thorny, or species like the Banyan (<term>vaṭa</term>), Sacred Fig (<term>aśvattha</term>), Kapittha, Agastya, Sigru, and Tamarind are condemned and should always be avoided in house construction. **Varāha** also says: Trees from cemeteries (<term>pitṛvana</term>), roads, temple grounds, termite mounds, gardens, or hermitages should be avoided. Also to be rejected are those growing near sacred trees (<term>caitya</term>) or river confluences, those nourished by dirty water, those entwined by creepers, or those struck by lightning or high winds. Trees where elephants have leaned or those containing beehives or bird nests are inauspicious.

Trees with glossy leaves, flowers, and fruits, or those like Sandalwood, are auspicious for the **Brahmins** <note>the priestly class</note>. For the **Kshatriyas** <note>the warrior/regal class</note>, the Ashvattha, Khadira, and Bilva trees bring prosperity. For the **Vaishyas** <note>the merchant/landowner class</note>, the Jivaka, Khadira, and Sindura trees give good results. For the **Shudras** <note>the laboring class</note>, the Tinduka, Kesara, and Arjuna trees are best. **Vyāsa** also warns: "One should never use timber from another person’s house for one's own; the builder who does so will not live long in that residence." In the *Samarāṅgaṇa*, it is said that bricks, stones, and earth are fine even if old, but wise men say the timber for a house must be new. **Śārṅgadhara** adds: "For a new house, new wood is best; for a repair, old wood is praised. New wood in an old house is excellent, but old wood in a new house is never auspicious."

The **prayer for cutting a tree** (<term>vṛkṣachedana-prārthanā</term>) is stated in the Tantra as follows:
> "Whatever spirits reside here, having accepted this offering according to the ritual, may they seek residence elsewhere; may they forgive us, and may prosperity be unto them." <note>original: "yānīha bhūtāni vasatitāni bali-gṛhītvā... kṣamantu tāny aghanam astu tebhya iti"</note>

Now, regarding the pillar, he describes the **height of the house** (<term>gṛhoccatā</term>): One should know the height of the house is equal to the width plus one-sixteenth of that width. As stated in the *Ratnakośa*: "The height of the house should be the width plus one-sixteenth, totaling four cubits." Others say: "The height should be neither too high nor too low; the walls should be plastered according to one's taste." (17)

<summary>This page details the ritual and practical requirements for building a house, focusing on the Southeast corner for the first pillar, the selection of auspicious wood species based on social class, and the specific trees and ecological conditions to avoid. It also provides the mathematical formula for determining a house's height relative to its width.</summary>
<keywords>Vastu Shastra, Stambha (pillar), timber selection, house height, Varahamihira, Shilanyasa (foundation stone), tree spirits, construction rituals</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical architectural instructions from the previous page. While page 73 dealt with the ritual installation of the first pillar and the movement of the symbolic "Time-Serpent," this page shifts focus to the internal layout of the house and the specific dimensions that require complex astrological calculations.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>Su. Bha. 74 / T. 74</header>

Now, regarding the main house, he describes which small rooms should be placed in which directions, starting with the bath. "Bathing" is self-evident; "Fire" refers to the kitchen; "Sleeping" refers to the bedroom; "Clothing" is well-known; "Eating" refers to the dining area; "Cattle-yoke" refers to the place for animals; "Vessels" and "Wealth" refer to the treasury and storage; "Gods" refers to the shrine. The placement of these dwellings should be done in order, starting from the East. On this matter, **Narada** <note>a legendary sage often cited in architectural and legal texts</note> says: The bathroom should be in the East; the fire-temple <note>original: "agnimandira," meaning the kitchen</note> in the Southeast; the bedroom in the South; the dressing room in the Southwest; the dining hall in the West; the animal shed in the Northwest; the storehouse for vessels and wealth in the North; and the temple of the gods in the Northeast. 

Now he speaks of the location for water: water should be placed in the direction of the "Ruler" <note>Isana, the Northeast</note>, and not elsewhere. Thus it is said: if water is located in the East and other directions, there will be loss of sons, fear of fire, fear of enemies, quarrels with women, and defects caused by women. In the Southwest, it causes constant anxiety.

**Bathing, fire, sleeping, clothing, eating, cattle, wealth, and the gods—these are positioned starting from the East. Water should only be in the direction of the Ruler (Northeast); elsewhere, in the Northwest, should be the place for waste.**
**If the land or resources are limited, one may place them according to preference; however, the South of the house is best for the mortar, the grinding stone, the ancestral space, and the washing of feet. 18**
<note>Notations under verse: dhra 7, dhra 7, dhra 5, dhra 6, dhra 5</note>

Now he explains another point: "Superior." The group of items mentioned as "superior" should be placed in the Northwest direction, because other authors like **Lalla** state they belong in the Northwest. In the South, the "waste-place" <note>original: "mūtraka," referring to a latrine or drainage area</note> should be made. If space is restricted, these may be arranged within the house itself, as long as they are assigned to their respective relative positions. Now he explains what to do if the land or resources are limited: "If limited," meaning if the strength of the land is small, one may act according to one's liking. To the South of the house, according to the teachers, one should place the heavy tools like the mortar and pestle, the grinding mill <note>original: "piṣyantra"</note>, and the foot-washing area. As stated in another text: The mortar, the grinding mill, the fireplace, and the water-reservoir—on the Southern side of the house, one should also place the area for washing the feet of the ancestors <note>likely referring to ritual purification areas</note>. 18

---

Now he explains for which houses the **Gain-Calculations** (<term>āyādi</term> <note>a set of six mathematical formulas used in Vastu Shastra to ensure the building's dimensions are in harmony with the owner's astrology</note>) should be applied and for which they should not. In a house where the measurement is more than thirty-two **cubits** (<term>hasta</term> <note>a unit of measurement approximately the length of a forearm</note>), that is called a "house exceeding thirty-two cubits." For the four castes, for grass-huts, and for galleries—"galleries" <note>original: "ālinda"</note> are commonly known as verandas or porches—and for "ornaments" like turrets, for these, the Gain-Calculations are not required. Thus, in the **Daivajña-vallabha** it is said: For galleries, turrets, and projections in the four directions which serve as house ornaments, one should not worry about Gain-Calculations, as they do not fall under the primary architectural site. Likewise, in the **Chintamani**: where the length...

**For a house of more than thirty-two cubits, for grass-huts, for verandas and such, the Gain-Calculations are not prescribed; these are permitted in all months.**
**Once covered, with its doors and properly honored, one should enter the house according to the rules; for the first entry, one should avoid the months of the Sun, Venus, and Jupiter. 19**
<note>Notations under verse: dhra 9, dhra 9, dhra 9, dhra 9, dhra 9</note>

...and so on, if it is more than thirty-two cubits, a wise man should not bother to calculate the "Gain and Loss" (<term>āya-vyaya</term>). Thus, in the **Rajamartanda**: "One should consider the Gain, Loss, and the lunar month's fruit, but not for a hut of grass." Likewise, **Vasishtha** says: "From eleven cubits up to thirty-two cubits, the Gain-Calculations must be considered; beyond that, one need not worry." Now he gives a special rule for grass-huts: "Grass-huts are permitted in all months," meaning they can be built at any time. Now he describes entering the house once it is completed: "Covered," meaning once the roof is on, in the months prescribed for beginning construction, one should enter the house according to the rules of the scriptures. [Catchword: "A"]

<vocab>Vastu Shastra, house construction, bathroom, dining room, thirty-two cubits, Gain and Loss formulas, Subhashita, commentary, Daivajña-vallabha, Vasishtha</vocab>

<summary>This page details the specific cardinal directions for rooms (e.g., kitchen in the Southeast, shrine in the Northeast) and explains that technical astrological calculations for prosperity are only mandatory for permanent houses between 11 and 32 cubits in size.</summary>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on house construction and habitation rituals. While the previous page focused on the internal layout of rooms and water placement, this page details the specific timing (months and lunar mansions) and the physical readiness required for the first entry into a home, known as Gṛhapraveśa.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>॥ ॥</header>

<margin>Mu-Bha 75</margin> Regarding the months mentioned for the commencement of construction, it is said that the owner should enter the house in those same months according to the scriptural rites. What kind of house should it be? One that is enclosed by walls. Furthermore, what are its characteristics? 

It must have a "submissive" door <note>original: "vashya-kapāṭa," referring to a door that is well-fitted, easy to operate, and properly aligned</note>. If the door is not "submissive"—meaning it is poorly hung or opens on its own—it is not stable. Furthermore, the house must be "highly honored" <note>original: "architatama"</note>, meaning it is decorated with festoons <note>ornamental hangings or "torana"</note> and garlands. **Narada** <note>a sage and legendary authority on law and architecture</note> says: "One should not enter a house that is without a door, uncovered, where offerings and food have not been given, or that is devoid of Brahmins and cows." Similarly, **Chyavana** says: "If a door opens by itself, there is disease; if it closes by itself, there is the destruction of the family. If the door is larger than the prescribed measurement or placed in the Southwest direction, it leads to one's own ruin."

Now, regarding the specific months for entry according to the opinion of **Narada**: "One should enter the house in the months of Magha, Urja, and Shakra." Magha is well known; Urja refers to the month of Kartika; Shakra refers to Jyeshtha <note>these months correspond roughly to Jan-Feb, Oct-Nov, and May-June</note>. By saying "in the months mentioned for commencement," it is implied that for a grass or thatched house, since commencement is allowed in all months, entry is also allowed in all months. Thus **Narada** says: "Entry is known as middling in the months of Margashirsha and Kartika; it is excellent in the months of Magha, Phalguna, Vaishakha, and Jyeshtha." Similarly, in the **Jyotih-prakasha** <note>a classical astrological text</note>: "One should enter the house using the months, constellations, and days prescribed for beginning the house." Another text states: "Specifically, during the Northern transit of the sun <note>Uttarayana</note> for a stone or brick palace; but for a thatched house, it may be done at any time." Similarly, in the **Vyavahara-sara**: "During the Northern transit, excluding Shravana, Margashirsha, and Pausha, in an auspicious constellation and rising sign, having performed the Vastu worship of the house, one should enter the dwelling when the Moon and the Ascendant are pure." १९ <margin>Sha 75</margin>

Now, he describes the various types of entry, such as the first entry: Regarding the "commencement constellations," the sages state that the first entry into a new house should occur under the same constellations used to start the construction. Here, "commencement constellations" is a general term, meaning one should consider the dates (*tithis*) and other factors used for the beginning of the work.

---

The "first entry" <note>original: "Apurva," meaning unprecedented or new</note> refers to the very first time one inhabits a new house. Re-entry <note>original: "Sapurva"</note> refers to the entry made after returning from a long journey. Additionally, there is entry after a house has been renovated or rebuilt after damage by fire and so on <note>original: "Dvandva"</note>.

Thus, in the **Jyotih-prakasha**: "The first entry should be performed under the constellations used for the house's commencement; some say other constellations are middling." **Vasishtha** says: "They speak of three types of house entry: the first entry called 'Apurva,' the entry after a journey called 'Sapurva,' and the entry after a calamity such as fire or other fears."

Now, regarding the decision between day and night for house activities: house-related tasks should be done during the day and not at night. Only if there is a very auspicious messenger or sign during the day is a night entry permitted, but not otherwise. Thus **Vasishtha** says: "Entry during the day is auspicious, bringing sons, grandsons, and wealth. At night, it is influenced by the power of the Sun and the signs; without the strength of the Sun and the proper date, night entry should be avoided."

Now he explains the purity of the constellations for the "Vase-Vastu" ritual <note>a ritual involving a ceremonial pot or Kalasha</note>: Starting from the Sun’s constellation, in a cycle of six, five, and fourteen constellations... <note>this refers to a technical "Chakra" or grid used to determine if a day is auspicious based on the Sun's position</note> if the entry occurs in an unfavorable part of the cycle, it will not be auspicious. Others are auspicious. Here, one should understand the good and bad effects of various constellations. २०

Now he explains the purity of the Ascendant (*Lagna*) for entry: Entry into a house is auspicious when the Ascendant is in an "increase" house <note>original: "Upachaya," the 3rd, 6th, 10th, or 11th houses</note> from the birth-sign or birth-ascendant, and in a "fixed" sign <note>Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, or Aquarius</note>. By whom? By the Sun and other planets situated in the positions mentioned for the commencement of the house. This applies when the Sun is in a "leftward" movement. Furthermore, using constellations that face the same direction as the house's entrance. Alternatively, he describes the "Fish" characteristic: in the cavities/holes, in the 5th, 11th, and 8th positions from the Lagna...

<summary>This page outlines the astrological and physical requirements for entering a new or renovated home, emphasizing that the house must be structurally complete and ritualistically decorated. It distinguishes between the first entry (Apurva), re-entry after travel (Sapurva), and entry after repairs (Dvandva), while favoring daytime rituals and specific lunar months.</summary>
<keywords>Gṛhapraveśa, Nakshatra, Muhurta, Vastu Shastra, Narada, Vasishtha, Magha, Kartika, Apurva, Lagna, auspicious timing</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the final rules of Vastu Shastra (the science of architecture) regarding the proper timing for entering a new home to the beginning of the Yatra Prakarana (the chapter on travel and journeys). It maintains continuity by concluding the astrological calculations for house-entry orientation based on the Sun's position.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 76 / Ti 76</header>

Regarding the months from the eleventh onwards: when the Sun is positioned between the fifth and eighth houses <note>This refers to the astrological placement of the Sun relative to the house's orientation</note>, and a person enters a house facing East or other directions, that Sun will progressively be situated to their left. This means that if the Sun is moving between the eighth and eleventh months, it is "left-handed" (auspicious) for those entering an East-facing house. Similarly, if the Sun is between the fifth and eighth months, it is "left-handed" for those entering a South-facing house.

**Thus ends the chapter on Sacred Architecture (Vastu).**

**Now, the chapter on Journeys (Yatra) is explained.** First, it describes at what time a journey should be undertaken: during favorable planetary periods.

<insert>
When the Sun is situated in the houses of growth (Upachaya) <note>The 3rd, 6th, 10th, and 11th houses in a birth chart, considered auspicious for new beginnings</note> and in a sign corresponding to the direction of the house, entering the home is auspicious. When the Sun is between the fifth and eighth positions, it becomes "left-handed" for those entering a house facing East and so on. **21** 
**Thus ends the Chapter on Sacred Architecture.** 
Travel is declared favorable during the planetary periods (Dashas) of growth-promoting planets, on their respective days of the week, and under specific lunar mansions like Shravana (Karni) and others. The constellations of Mrigashira, Anuradha, and Hasta are favorable, while travel is forbidden during the birth constellation (Janmabha), or under the influence of Ashlesha (Sarpa), Ardra, Krittika (Agni), Bharani (Yama), Purva Bhadrapada (Ajaikapada), or the three constellations of Tvashtri (Chitra, Svati, Vishakha). One should also avoid the "Empty" days (Rikta: 4th, 9th, 14th), the 15th and 30th (Parva), the 6th (Guha), the 8th, the 12th (Hari), and the 1st of the bright fortnight.
</insert>

The learned teachers state that a journey is desired during the major and minor planetary periods (*Dashas* and *Antardashas*) of a planet that is positioned in an auspicious house of growth, and on the weekday associated with a planet that shows strength in the "eight-fold points" (Ashtakavarga) <note>A system of point-based planetary strength used in Indian astrology</note>. 

In which lunar mansions (*Nakshatras*) should one travel? In Shravana (*Karni*), Revati (*Antya*), and Punarvasu (*Aditi-bha*). This also includes the pairs associated with them: Dhanishtha (paired with Shravana), Ashvini (paired with Revati), and Pushya (paired with Punarvasu). Furthermore, in Mrigashira (*Mriga*), Anuradha (*Maitra*), and Hasta (*Arka*). 

---

In these nine lunar mansions, travel is considered auspicious. However, there is a specific exception: **Not on the birth constellation.** This means one should not travel on the *Janma-nakshatra* <note>The specific star the moon was in at the moment of one's birth</note>, even if it is one of the favorable ones like Shravana. 

Now, the inauspicious ones are mentioned: Ashlesha (*Sarpa*), Ardra, Krittika (*Agni*), Bharani (*Yama*), Purva Bhadrapada (*Ajaikapada*), Magha (*Pitara*), and the "Three of Tvashtri" (Chitra, Svati, and Vishakha). Travel in these nine constellations is considered inauspicious.

<insert>
Avoid traveling toward the East when Venus or the Moon's nodes are in certain positions... <note>original: "prakuchandra sitashakrabham..." - this verse details the concept of "Disha Shool" or the "spike of the direction," an astrological taboo against traveling in certain directions on specific days</note>. In the first three parts of the day, certain constellations are sharp; in the second part, others are soft. One should regulate travel according to the Sun's path and the divisions of the night. The constellations of Shravana, Hasta, Pushya, and Mrigashira are the best for specific rules of travel, providing success even without a specific "moment" (Kshana) being calculated. **5**
</insert>

The remaining nine constellations not mentioned as either very good or very bad are considered mediocre. 

Next, the inauspicious lunar days (*Tithis*) are described: the "Empty" days (*Rikta*) which are the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of the lunar month; the "Joint" days (*Parva*) which are the Full Moon and New Moon; the 6th day (*Guha*); the 8th day; the 12th day (*Hari*); and the 1st day of the bright fortnight (*Sitasyeva*). Travel on these days is declared inauspicious by the sages. By implication, travel on the other lunar days is permitted.

<summary>This page concludes the architectural guidelines for house-entry based on solar alignment and introduces the rules for travel (Yatra), listing auspicious and inauspicious lunar mansions and dates.</summary>
<keywords>Vastu Shastra, Yatra, Nakshatras, Tithi, Sun orientation, Shravana, Rikta Tithi, planetary periods, Indian astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Yatra Prakarana" (Chapter on Journeys), moving from general auspicious times into specific directional prohibitions (Shula), the movement of the Yogini (directional energies), and the use of the Saptashalaka-chakra (a seven-lined astrological grid) to determine safe passage.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 77</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 77</margin>
Regarding other auspicious and inauspicious matters, the author says: **Jna** refers to Mercury (Budha), and **Shukra** is the well-known Venus. The pairing of these two is considered a "pleasant vehicle" <note>original: "sukhayānaṃ"</note>, yet travel during the times previously mentioned for specific lunar dates and constellations is considered inauspicious if these two are in conjunction. In short, one should not begin a journey when Mercury and Venus are traveling together.

<margin>1</margin> Now, he describes the **Shula** <note>literally "spikes" or "spears"; these are astrological obstacles or directions to avoid on specific days</note>: In the **East** (Prāk), one should avoid travel on Monday (Chandra), Saturday (Asita), and under the constellation Jyeshtha (Shakrabham). These three should be abandoned for eastward travel. In the **South** (Avāk), one should avoid the five constellations starting from Dhanishtha (Vaparādhāni) and Thursday (Ijya). These should be avoided for southward travel. In the **West** (Paścāt), avoid Sunday (Arka), Friday (Sita), and the constellation Rohini. In the **North** (Udak), avoid Tuesday (Kuja), Wednesday (Budha), and the constellation Uttaraphalguni (Aryamā). The term "Shula" is applied to each of these directional prohibitions.

Now he describes the **Yogini** <note>a directional goddess/energy whose position changes daily; traveling toward her is considered unlucky</note>: regarding the lunar days (Tithis), one should avoid them in order, starting from the **Northeast** (Īshāna) and moving clockwise. Which lunar days are these?
- **Northeast:** 30th (New Moon) and 8th.
- **East:** 9th and 1st.
- **Southeast:** 3rd and 11th.
- **South:** 13th and 5th.
- **Southwest:** 12th and 4th.
- **West:** 6th and 14th.
- **Northwest:** 15th (Full Moon) and 7th.
- **North:** 10th and 2nd.
This is the explanation of the sequence.

<margin>2</margin> Now he mentions another forbidden practice regarding the **Indra** <note>East</note> and other directions using the **Agni** constellations (starting from Krittika). One should count in groups of seven. This is what it means: construct a **Saptashalaka-chakra** <note>a divinatory grid made of seven horizontal and seven vertical lines</note>. On the seven lines going East, starting from the Northeast, place seven constellations each, starting with Krittika. Place seven in the southern lines, and continue similarly with the West and North lines. Then, draw a line between the Northwest and Southeast. This line is named **Parigha** <note>the "iron bar" or "bolt"</note>. Having constructed this chakra, he explains its purpose: staying within the paths of the Wind (Northwest) and Fire (Southeast)...

<margin>77 Back</margin>

...is well known. The directions of Fire and Wind are Southeast and Northwest. The **Parigha** line situated there should not be crossed. 

Now he explains another purpose: in the **South** (Apāk), one should not travel under the western constellations (Varunaiḥ). Under eastern constellations, one should not travel to the **North** (Udak). Traveling in a direction while its corresponding constellations are active is generally avoided. 

However, under the constellations **Hasta** (Ravi), **Pushya** (Ijya), **Anuradha** (Mitra), and **Ashwini** (Turaga), one may travel in any direction. These constellations are auspicious for travel in all directions.

Now, he provides an exception for mandatory duties: If a journey is **absolutely necessary**, even if the constellations are considered "censured" or unfavorable for that direction, one may go if there is a **Lagna-labha** <note>the gain of an auspicious ascending sign</note> for the direction of travel. If the chosen time (Lagna) possesses the qualities that will be described later, one may proceed. Otherwise, one should not. This success is achieved by "breaking the Shula"—meaning one avoids the specific Shula-constellation. If the Shula-constellation is active, one should not go even if an auspicious Lagna is available.

<margin>3</margin> Now he says: During the three divisions of the day, in the first part, one should not travel under "Mixed" (Mishra) or "Fixed" (Dhruva) constellations. In the second part, do not travel under "Fierce" constellations. In the third part, do not travel under "Swift" (Kshipra) constellations. 

Regarding the three parts of the night: in the first part, travel under "Soft" (Mridu) constellations; in the second part, under "Fierce" (Ugra) constellations; and in the third part, under "Mobile" (Chara) constellations. 

He then mentions a special rule: For **Shravana** (Shruti), **Hasta** (Arka), **Pushya** (Ijya), and **Mrigashira** (Mriga), this rule does not apply. One may travel under these constellations during any part of the day or night. 

Finally, he describes the characteristics and results of the **Abhijit** <note>an intercalary "victorious" constellation</note>: It consists of the constellation Shravana (Vaishnava) and the last quarter (fourth pada) of Uttarashadha...

<summary>This page details the directional prohibitions (Shula) and timing constraints for travel, including the movement of the Yogini goddess and the construction of the Saptashalaka grid. It provides specific planetary and lunar exceptions that allow for travel even during generally unfavorable periods.</summary>

<keywords>Yatra, Shula, Yogini, Saptashalaka-chakra, Nakshatra, Tithi, Budha, Shukra, Parigha, Lagna</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the 'Yatra Prakarana' (Chapter on Journeys), specifically focusing on the auspicious timing provided by the constellation Abhijit and the alignment of travel with the movements of the Sun and Moon. It also provides practical remedies for astrological obstacles.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the 'Beautiful Gem'</header>

The first parts of the constellation Shravana <note>original: "Śravaṇa"</note> are divided into segments. The first fifteenth part of this constellation is considered the measure of **Abhijit** <note>a "hidden" twenty-eighth constellation used for selecting auspicious moments</note>. This Abhijit provides success in all directions except for the South. Even a brief moment <note>original: "muhūrta"</note> occurring under Abhijit is effective and fulfills the desired fruits of those setting out on a journey.

Next, the author says: **Movement should be aligned with the Sun and Moon.** This refers to the paths of the Sun and Moon <note>original: "arkendra-ayana"</note>. Moving in alignment with these paths is beneficial both day and night. If there is a difference between day and night, the following rule applies: The Sun is the lord of the day, and the Moon is the lord of the night. If both are in their Northern path <note>original: "uttarāyaṇa," the period between the winter and summer solstices</note>, then traveling North is beneficial day and night. If both are in their Southern path <note>original: "dakṣiṇāyana," the period between the summer and winter solstices</note>, then traveling South is beneficial day and night. At night, one should specifically travel toward the direction where the Moon is positioned.

<insert>
When the movement is aligned with the Sun and Moon, it is always beneficial. In the event of a difference between day and night: travel East for Mars, South for Saturn and the Moon, West for Mercury and Jupiter, and North for Venus and the Sun. According to the sage Devala, movement during the periods of 'Dala' and 'Vidala' is also beneficial. 5
</insert>

Otherwise, the travel is not recommended. Here, the East is traditionally associated with the North. Therefore, when travel to the North is recommended, travel to the East is also auspicious. Similarly, the West is associated with the South; when southward travel is recommended, westward travel is also auspicious. As stated in the *Ratnakosha* <note>a classic astrological compendium</note>: "When the Sun is in the sign of Cancer and others, one should travel South or West." 

Next, regarding the planets: For **Mars** (Bhauma), traveling East (Prāk) is beneficial. For **Saturn** (Shani) and the **Moon** (Chandra), traveling South (Apāk) is beneficial. For **Mercury** (Budha) and **Jupiter** (Guru), traveling West (Paścāt) is beneficial. For **Venus** (Bhrigu) and the **Sun** (Arka), traveling North (Udak) is beneficial.

---

Next, the author describes the auspicious time-segments known as **Dala** and **Vidala**. Starting from the Sun's constellation, count to the constellation of the day. Divide this number by seven <note>original: "aṅgaiḥ saptabhiḥ taṣṭam"</note>. If the remainder is two, it is called **Dala**. If the remainder is three or six, it is called **Vidala**. According to the teachings of the divine sage **Devala** <note>original: "Devaloktyā"</note>, beginning a journey during these 'Dala' or 'Vidala' times brings prosperity. 5 

Now, he describes the remedy for the **Day-Spikes** <note>original: "Vāraśūla," specific directions that are considered 'piercing' or dangerous on certain days of the week</note>. By consuming certain items, one can neutralize the ill effects of the Day-Spikes. Beginning with Sunday <note>original: "Ina"</note>, if one consumes ghee <note>original: "ājya"</note> and other items, the journey will not result in misfortune. 

Next, he explains the **Directional Longings** <note>original: "dig-dohada," ritual requirements for directions</note>: By consuming ghee and other substances, and using specific vehicles like elephants, the journey becomes successful. The vehicles mentioned are: the elephant <note>original: "hastī"</note>, the cart or carriage <note>original: "anaḥ"</note>, the horse <note>original: "hayaḥ"</note>, and the palanquin carried by men <note>original: "naraḥ"</note>. This means:

<insert>
By consuming ghee, milk, jaggery, sesame, curd, barley, or mung beans before departing on Sunday and other days, the "spike" of the day does not cause harm. One gains success by traveling with elephants, carts, horses, or men after consuming ghee, sesame-rice, fish, or milk. This brings more than a hundred thousand benefits. 6
</insert>

Specifically: 
- Consume **ghee** and travel by **elephant** to succeed in the **East**. 
- Consume **sesame-rice** <note>original: "tilodana"</note> and travel by **chariot** to succeed in the **South**. 
- Consume **fish** <note>original: "matsya"</note> and travel by **horse** to succeed in the **West**. 
- Consume **milk** and travel by **men** (palanquin) to succeed in the **North**. 

If these specific foods or vehicles are unavailable, the author provides a decision: The journey is still valid through the intention of the ritual. Finally, regarding the purification of the **Ascendant** <note>original: "lagna-śuddhi"</note>: If **Jupiter** or **Mercury** are placed in the fourth house from the Ascendant, it provides more than a hundred thousand strengths <note>original: "lakṣādhikaṃ," meaning it outweighs countless minor faults</note>. 6

-> 6 7 8 9 10 11 <-

<vocab>Abhijit (intercalary constellation), Uttarayana (Northern path), Dakshinayana (Southern path), Ratnakosha (astrological text), Vidala/Dala (time divisions), Varashula (directional obstacles by day), Dig-dohada (directional rituals), Ajya (clarified butter/ghee), Dadhi (curd/yogurt), Devalokti (sayings of Sage Devala)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the specific rules for 'Yatra' (Journeys) from the 'Ratnakosha' (Jewel-Treasury), moving from the general benefits of the Abhijit constellation to specific planetary alignments in the journey-chart. It introduces the concept of 'Lalata' (the forehead), an astrological placement that prohibits travel in certain directions.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>Beautiful Gem 79 | Ri 79</header>

One should not abandon a **journey ascendant** <note>original: "yātrā lagna," the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment one departs</note> if it is in its own sign or its own division <note>original: "svarkṣa-svarkṣāṃśe," referring to the varga or sub-divisional charts</note>. However, one should avoid a sign that is not productive <note>original: "anupacaya," houses that do not lead to growth—specifically the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 5th, 7th, 8th, 9th, and 12th</note> for the traveler’s own birth sign. One must also avoid Aquarius, a sign that is "setting" <note>combust due to proximity to the Sun</note>, or a sign that is "crooked" <note>original: "anṛjugṛhaṃ," referring to a planet in retrograde</note>. One should also avoid the sign that is sixth from the traveler's birth sign, or signs that are **back-rising** <note>original: "pṛṣṭodaya," signs that rise with their rear first, considered generally inauspicious for new beginnings</note>. As stated in the *Brihat Jataka* <note>a foundational 6th-century astrology text by Varahamihira</note>: Taurus, Aries, Sagittarius, Cancer, and Capricorn are considered back-rising signs. Furthermore, one should avoid an ascendant whose lord is an enemy to the lord of the traveler’s birth sign or its divisional sign, or a sign occupied by a "cruel" <note>malefic</note> planet, or one lacking strength at the time of birth.

### -> One should not abandon the journey ascendant unless it is unfavorable, setting, retrograde, back-rising, or an enemy to the birth-lord, or lacking strength. Now, the lords of the directions are the Sun, Venus, Mars, Rahu, Saturn, the Moon, Mercury, and Jupiter. One may depart when the lord of the direction is in a center. 7 <-

The text explains that if the lord of the direction <note>original: "digīśa"</note> is in a **center** <note>original: "kendra," the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses, which are the pillars of a chart</note>, one may proceed with the journey. Specifically, one should travel toward the direction whose ruler is in a center, provided that the sign is not "on the forehead" <note>original: "lālāṭagaṃ," a technical term for a planet positioned directly in front of the traveler's path</note>. This "forehead" restriction will be explained in the next verse. 7 
[Propitious markings]

---

Now, the author explains the prohibition of traveling when a direction-lord is "on the forehead" and defines the characteristics of this "forehead" position. One should not set out on a journey when the lord of that direction is in the "forehead" position. If the **Sun** <note>original: "arka"</note> is in the first house <note>the Ascendant</note>, it is considered to be on the forehead for the **East**. If **Saturn** <note>original: "arkaja," literally 'son of the Sun'</note> is in the seventh house, it is on the forehead for the **West**. If **Mercury** <note>original: "budha"</note> is in the fourth house, it is on the forehead for the **North**. 

### -> One should not travel when the lord of the direction is on the forehead: the Sun in the first house faces East; Saturn in the seventh house faces West; Mercury in the fourth faces North; and Mars in the tenth faces South. <-
### -> Jupiter in the second or third house faces the Northeast; Venus in the eleventh or twelfth faces the Southeast; the Moon in the sixth or fifth faces the Northwest; and Rahu in the eighth or ninth faces the Southwest. 8 <-

If **Mars** <note>original: "kuja"</note> is in the tenth house <note>the zenith</note>, it is on the forehead for the **South**. If **Jupiter** <note>original: "guru"</note> is in the second or third house, it is on the forehead for the **Northeast**. If **Venus** <note>original: "bhṛgu"</note> is in the eleventh or twelfth house, it is on the forehead for the **Southeast**. If the **Moon** <note>original: "vidhu"</note> is in the sixth or fifth house, it is on the forehead for the **Northwest**. Finally, if **Rahu** <note>the North Node of the Moon</note> is in the eighth or ninth house, it is on the forehead for the **Southwest**. 8
[Propitious markings]

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for choosing an auspicious time for a journey (Yatra). It transitions from general prohibitions to the specific influence of retrograde planets and the "birth stars" of the planets themselves. It also introduces the "Veshi" configuration and cites the "Yavana" (Greek-influenced) tradition as an authority on using birth-lords for success.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

<margin>Su-Ma- 80</margin>

Regarding the term **retrograde** <note>original: "vakrī"</note>: A retrograde planet <note>a planet that appears to move backward in the sky</note> situated in a **quadrant** <note>original: "kendra," the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a chart</note> causes the destruction of the traveler. The specific day associated with that retrograde planet is also destructive for the one departing. Furthermore, even the **divisional sign** <note>original: "varga"</note> of that planet, if it falls in the ascendant, is destructive. Even if a planet is naturally benefic, if it is an enemy to the **birth constellation** <note>original: "janm-arkṣa"</note> or the birth ascendant, it will bring about the traveler's destruction. This refers to the lords of the birth sign and birth ascendant and their respective enemies. Now, the text lists the birth constellations of the Sun and the other planets: <margin>Commentary</margin>

---

-> A retrograde planet in a quadrant, its day, or its division in the ascendant, brings destruction to the traveler. Even a benefic is destructive if it is an enemy to the birth constellation or ascendant. <-
-> The birth constellations for the Sun and others are: Vishakha, Krittika, Purvashadha, Shravana, Purvaphalguni, Pushya, Revati, Bharani, and Ashlesha. An afflicted planet in the ascendant causes harm. 9 <-

---

Starting from the Sun <note>original: "Arka"</note>, the **birth constellations** of the planets are as follows:
1. **Sun**: Vishakha <note>original: "Dīśā"</note>
2. **Moon**: Krittika <note>original: "Agni"</note>
3. **Mars**: Purvashadha <note>original: "Ambu"</note>
4. **Mercury**: Shravana <note>original: "Aja"</note>
5. **Jupiter**: Purvaphalguni <note>original: "Bhāgya"</note>
6. **Venus**: Pushya <note>original: "Guru"</note>
7. **Saturn**: Revati <note>original: "Pauṣṇa"</note>
8. **Rahu**: Bharani <note>original: "Yāmya"</note>
9. **Ketu**: Ashlesha <note>original: "Sārpa"</note>

Regarding the term **afflicted** <note>original: "upapluva-gata"</note>: If a planet's birth constellation is struck by three types of portents <note>astrological or environmental omens</note>, then that planet, if situated in the ascendant, causes harm.

---

Regarding the **Veshi** configuration: This occurs when a planet is in the second sign from the Sun. If this sign is occupied by a benefic planet and is also the **ascendant** <note>original: "tanu-ga," the sign rising at the start of the journey</note>, it is extremely auspicious. Those zodiac signs which were occupied by benefic planets at the time of one’s birth are also very auspicious when they serve as the journey ascendant. Similarly, positions of **increase** <note>original: "upacaya," specifically the 3rd, 6th, 10th, and 11th houses</note> relative to the **birth Moon** <note>original: "janmābja"</note> or the birth ascendant are highly auspicious.

---

-> A planet in the second from the Sun, signs joined by benefics at birth, or those in positions of increase from the birth Moon—even if in their own, exalted, or friendly signs... <-
-> Even malefic planets are very auspicious in the ascendant if they are in such positions. The two lords of birth in quadrants, joined by their own friends, bestow auspicious results; a benefic joined by an enemy is not fruitful, while a malefic joined by a friend bestows wealth. 10 <-

---

<meta>catchword: yasthāḥ (those situated in)</meta> Those situated in the ascendant are extremely auspicious. Planets that were in their **own sign** <note>original: "svakṣa"</note>, their **exaltation sign** <note>original: "ucca"</note>, or a **friendly sign** <note>original: "mitrakṣa"</note> at the time of birth are auspicious in the journey-ascendant even if they are naturally **malefic** <note>original: "pāpa," literally "sinful" or "cruel" planets like Saturn or Mars</note>. If the two "Lords of Birth" (the ruler of the birth sign and the ruler of the birth ascendant) are in quadrants, they are extremely auspicious. However, a benefic planet <note>original: "śubha"</note> in the ascendant provides no result if it is joined by its enemy. Conversely, a malefic planet joined by a friend in the ascendant bestows wealth. 

As the **Greek authority** <note>original: "Yavana," referring to the Yavanas or Indo-Greeks whose astrological techniques were integrated into Indian systems</note> says: "The lords of birth are the protectors and the increasers; one should employ them in the journey-ascendant without any doubt, even if they are naturally malefic," and so on. 10

<summary>This page details how retrograde planets and specific birth stars (Janma Nakshatras) of planets affect travel, while emphasizing that planets favorable at birth remain beneficial for journeys. It highlights the "Veshi" yoga and cites the Yavana tradition to justify using malefic planets if they are birth-lords.</summary>
