<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for journeys (Yatra) from page 80, specifically focusing on the placement of "malefic" planets in the birth chart at the time of departure and the exceptions to the rule of "Facing Venus" (Prati-Shukra).</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>Mu-Ma- Commentary</header>

<meta>The page features a double-line border around the text area. Section breaks are marked with repetitive 'छ' characters. Marginal notes are present on both left and right sides.</meta>

Now, the author explains the placement of malefics <note>original: "khala," referring to naturally harsh planets like Mars or Saturn</note> in a single verse. If malefics are not in the 3rd, 11th, or 6th houses—specifically the 12th, 1st, 2nd, 4th, 5th, 7th, 8th, 9th, and 10th—then they are considered "gone to other places." There, the malefic planets—the "sinful planets" <note>original: "pāpagrahāḥ"</note>—are described. Even though the Sun being in the 10th house is already established by the phrase "not in the 3rd, 11th, or 6th," it is specifically mentioned here to indicate a special visionary result. The houses mentioned are the 12th, 1st, 6th, 8th, and others.

-> If malefics are not in the 11th, 3rd, or 6th; if the Sun is in the 10th; if the Moon is waning; if Mercury is in the 12th; if Venus is in the 7th; or if Jupiter is in the 8th house; <-
-> Or if the Moon is conjoined with any other planets in the 12th house—these situations provide great obstacles for the traveler. If they are in other positions, they grant victory, wealth, and happiness, provided the previously mentioned prohibitions do not apply. 11 <-

"With the Moon" <note>original: "sa-candrah"</note> refers to a waning Moon, specifically during the dark fortnight of the lunar month. "Setting" refers to the 7th house. "Mercury" <note>original: "bu-suta"</note> in the 12th. Venus in the 7th. "Jupiter" <note>original: "ijyā"</note> in the 8th. "Mercury" <note>original: "bu-suta"</note> in the 12th. A Moon conjoined with any one, two, or many planets—each of these is a "giver of great obstacles" <note>original: "mahā-vighna-dāḥ"</note> for one setting out on a journey. This means they bring about various forms of death or failure. If the planets are in positions other than those stated, they grant victory, wealth, and happiness. This is true unless a previously mentioned "to be avoided" <note>original: "tyājya"</note> condition exists. <note>Characters below: छ छ छ छ</note>

---

Now, the author discusses the exceptions to the prohibitions regarding Mercury and Venus. "One’s own village" is well known. "Single town" is well known. "One’s own residence" <note>original: "ātma-sadma"</note> refers to one's own house. "Four-faced" <note>original: "catur-mukha"</note> refers to a four-hall mansion <note>original: "catuḥ-śāla"</note>. "Weddings" refers to the various forms of marriage, such as the Brahma rite. Among these, the "entry of the bride" <note>original: "vadhū-praveśa"</note> is specifically mentioned. "Fear of kings" <note>original: "nṛpa-bhī"</note>, "affliction by famine" <note>original: "durbhikṣa-pīḍā"</note>, "pilgrimage" <note>original: "tīrtha-gamana"</note>, and "command of the master" <note>original: "svāmi-nideśa"</note> are also listed. Regarding the lineages <note>original: "gotra"</note>—the groups of Bhrigu, Vashistha, Atri, Angirasa, Kashyapa, Bharadvaja, and Vatsya—in these cases, traveling while "facing Venus or Mercury" <note>original: "sita-jñor-unmukha"</note> is beneficial and brings happiness. 

Regarding the "Facing Venus" <note>original: "prati-śukra"</note> fault during the entry of a bride: this applies when she is traveling with her husband. If she is traveling without her husband, the fault is seen. It is said that for a newlywed, this could cause barrenness. Thus, in the text *Bhimaparakrama*:

-> In one's own village or town, in one's own home, in four-hall buildings, in weddings, in the entry of the bride, in fear of kings, in famine, on a pilgrimage, or by a master's command; <-
-> Or for those of the Bhrigu, Vashistha, Atri, Angirasa, Kashyapa, Bharadvaja, and Vatsya lineages, it is said that traveling while facing Venus or Mercury is acceptable. 12 <-

As stated in the *Bhimaparakrama*: "When being led by the husband, the 'Facing Venus' fault does not exist." 
Likewise, in another tradition, it is said that for a newlywed, the fear of barrenness is removed in this context. This should be understood by the wise to apply specifically to the "second entry" <note>original: "dvirāgame," the formal return of the bride to her husband's home</note>. 
As *Lalla* says: "In entering one's own house or town, in disputes involving territory, in weddings, and in the bride's entry to the home, there is no consideration of 'Facing Venus'." 
Also, in the *Matsya Purana*: "A four-hall building with four doors, joined on all sides, is named 'Sarvatobhadra'; there, 'Facing Venus' is not a factor." 
Vashistha also says: "For the Kashyapas, the Bhrigus, the Vashisthas, the Angirasas, the Bharadvajas, and the Vatsyas, 'Facing Venus' does not exist." 12 <note>Characters below: छ छ छ छ</note>

<vocab>astrology, 12th-11th-6th houses, malefic planet, bride's entry, facing Venus, lineage, sinful planet, auspicious time, clan, sage Bhrigu, sage Vashistha</vocab>

<summary>This page details specific planetary alignments that cause obstacles during a journey, such as a waning Moon or malefic planets in certain houses. It also lists significant exceptions to the "Facing Venus" prohibition, allowing travel for specific lineages (Gotras) or during events like weddings and pilgrimages.</summary>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the astrological conditions for travel (Yatra), specifically addressing the "blindness" of the planet Venus and the ritual protocols a king must observe when beginning a journey.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma. 82</header>
<header>Di 82</header>

Now, the author states another exception regarding the Moon. When the Moon is positioned in the signs of Taurus or Aries, Venus <note>original: "Sita," a name for the planet Venus</note> is considered "blind." In such a state, traveling toward it <note>original: "sammukha-yanam," traveling in the direction where Venus is currently located</note> is considered good and auspicious. Regarding this, the sage Garga says: "From the month of Pausha onwards, as long as the Moon remains in the specific stars associated with those directions, Venus remains blind. During this period, both entering and departing are auspicious."

Now, he addresses travel that is absolutely necessary. In the west and east, due to the "reversal" of Venus, if a task is mandatory, the journey is considered good. This means: if Venus is in the eastern half of the sky, one may travel toward the west; if it is in the western half, one may travel toward the east. Thus, it is stated in the *Light of Astrology* <note>original: "Jyotih-prakasha"</note>: "As long as Venus remains in an 'enthusiastic' quadrant, one should travel toward that direction; similarly for the east and west."

Now, he describes the rules for the journey itself:

### When the Moon is in Taurus or Aries, Venus becomes blind; then traveling toward it is auspicious. In the west or east, due to the reversal of Venus, travel is always permitted if the task is necessary.

The text says: "The King." A king should travel one, four, and then six *kroshas* <term>krosha: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 2 miles or 3 kilometers</term> over the first three days. This means: on the first day, he travels one *krosha*; on the second day, four *kroshas*; and on the third day, six *kroshas*. After that, he may travel as he pleases. What kind of king? One who has satisfied the fire, the "gods of the earth" <note>original: "bhusura," meaning Brahmins</note>, and his friends. "Fire" refers to the sacrificial fire; "gods of the earth" are the Brahmins; and "friends" include his kinsmen. The meaning is this: having satisfied the fire with wood, offerings, and sesame; the Brahmins with donations; and his friends and wives with clothing and jewelry, he should depart. How should he act? He should reflect on his eventual return with affection. The sentiment is: after setting out for the sake of the journey, he should not turn back to look at his home out of attachment to his children and family. What other qualities should the king possess?

---

He should be satisfied—meaning he should not depart while hungry. Furthermore, what are his characteristics? His mind should be focused on the task at hand. Thus, in the *Ratnavali*: "One should depart from one's own residence or palace as a sage leaves a hermitage, even if it is difficult to leave; having performed the fire rituals and consumed the sacrificial offering with the permission of the Brahmins." Similarly, the sage Brihaspati says: "Having honored the astrologer and the wise according to one's ability, and having performed a circumambulation, one should stand and meditate on the Lord of Uma <note>the god Shiva</note>." Likewise, in the *Light of Astrology*: "Having satisfied one's own people and the Brahmins, and having honored those who would oppose him, one should depart pure, wearing white clothes and a turban, and spreading flowers." And Kashyapa says: "At the time of travel, at the threshold of the house, one should worship the astrologer with devotion, offering cows, land, gold, and clothes." In the *Great Journey* <note>original: "Brihad-yatra"</note>, it says he should be eager for the bath—

### The King, having satisfied the fire, the Brahmins, and his friends, and reflecting with affection on his eventual return, should depart with his mind focused on the task, covering one, four, and six kroshas over the first three days. 13

He should be in a state of fulfillment, adorned with all his ornaments, wearing white garments, and looking magnificent. In the *Beloved of Kings* <note>original: "Bhupala-vallabha"</note>, it says: "He should not slight the Brahmins, nor should he anger his own wife. If he desires success, once he has set out for the journey, he must not re-enter his house." Lalla says: "He who, having started a journey, slights a Brahmin, will find his life at an end and shall not return." In the *Great Journey*: "If a traveler departing on a journey does not cheer his wife, or if the wife does not cheer him, he will not return." And in the *Ratnavali*: "The King should travel one *krosha* or even half a *krosha* on the first day. On the second, having traveled a *yojana* <term>yojana: a distance of about 8 or 9 miles</term>, he should rest. On the third day, having covered one and a half *yojanas*, he may thereafter travel to distant places as he wishes." 13  &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; छ &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; छ &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; छ <note>The 'छ' characters mark the end of the section.</note>

<summary>This page discusses astrological exceptions for traveling when Venus is "blind" and outlines the ritual conduct, physical preparation, and specific distances a king must cover during the first three days of a journey.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, travel rules, Venus, King's duties, Garga, Brihaspati, krosha, yojana, ritual purity, Brahmins</keywords>

<meta>The text continues from page 82, transitioning from the discussion of planetary positions (Venus) to the specific behavioral and dietary restrictions a traveler must observe before and during a journey.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>Su. Ma.</header>
<header>Ri-</header>

<margin>Su. Ma. 83</margin>

Now he states the rules for journeys. **Anger** is well-known; **shaving** <note>original: "kshaura"</note>; **sexual pleasure and intercourse** <note>original: "rati-maithuna"</note>; **exhaustion**; **distress**; **meat** <note>original: "amisha" or "mamsa"</note>; **jaggery** <note>original: "guda," a traditional unrefined sugar</note> is well-known; **ghee** <note>clarified butter</note>; **tears** <note>original: "ashu-mochana," the shedding of tears</note>; **milk** is well-known; **liquor** <note>original: "asava" or "madya"</note>; **alkaline or salty grains** such as *masha* <term>masha: black gram pulse, often considered "heavy" or ritually impure in certain travel contexts</term>; **oil massage**; **fear** in the sense of things that cause alarm; **dark clothing** <note>original: "asitambarani," meaning blue, black, or dark red unlucky garments</note>; **vomiting**; and **oil** or **pungent spices** like black pepper—these one should abandon when embarking on a journey. Now, he explains for how many days one should avoid these specific items: one should avoid **milk, shaving, and sexual pleasure** for three, five, and seven days respectively. This means: avoid milk for 3 days, shaving for 5 days, and sexual pleasure for 7 days. For the remaining items, one should avoid them on the actual day of departure. One should also avoid them if suffering from illness or pain.

<insert>
->Anger, shaving, sexual pleasure, exhaustion, meat, jaggery, ghee, tears, milk, liquor, alkaline foods, oil massage, fear, dark clothing, vomiting, oil, and pungent spices should be abandoned when departing until the completion of the journey.<-
</insert>

Regarding **forehead marks** <note>original: "tilaka"</note>, one should avoid any mark that is not white, such as those made with yellow saffron <note>original: "kunkuma"</note> and the like, on the day of travel. This should also be avoided during the **symbolic departure** <note>original: "prasthana"</note>. A "symbolic departure" is when one moves a small distance at the exact auspicious moment <term>muhurta: an auspicious window of time, roughly 48 minutes</term> when a full departure is not yet possible. Regarding this, the *Ratnamala* <note>a famous medieval astrological treatise</note> says: "A seeker of victory should move his parasol, weapons, or other favored items toward the desired direction." This is done for the sake of the task at hand. In a symbolic departure, the full result is not obtained as it is when one actually walks a *kosha* <term>kosha: a measure of distance, about 2 miles</term> himself. Some teachers say: "When leaving, one should move the parasol, weapons, and favored items for the sake of victory." And so says Garga: "If one personally departs with a restrained mind, the entire fruit of his journey will be successfully achieved on the path." The limit for this movement, according to other texts, is moving from one's house to another house, or to the boundary of the property, or as far as an arrow can be shot from the house, or outside the city.

<margin>83</margin>
---

Once this symbolic departure is performed, one should not remain for too many days before actually leaving. Thus, in the *Light of Astrology* <note>original: "Jyotih-prakasha"</note>: "Having made a symbolic departure, one should not stay through days afflicted by great astrological flaws, nor should one stay if the period of the 'embryo-yoga' <note>an astrological calculation</note> has passed." In the *Ratnamala*: "A supreme king may stay for thirteen days, a local ruler <note>original: "mandalika"</note> for seven, and any other person for five nights; after that, the journey should be restarted." During such a symbolic departure, one must still avoid anger, shaving, sex, and the rest. Garga states: "One should abandon anger, shaving, vomiting, oil massage, blood-letting, ghee, meat, jaggery, oil, and liquor when traveling." In the *Ratnakosha*: "The use of sandalwood paste, cinnabar, saffron, red sandalwood, and the wearing of yellow or red clothing brings no peace to the traveler at that moment." In the *Great Journey* <note>original: "Brihat-yatra"</note>: "Eating pungent oil, jaggery, alkaline foods, or cooked meat—if one goes while eating these out of delusion—

<insert>
->Milk, shaving, and sex: avoid for three, five, and seven days respectively; for the others, on that day. Avoid white-excluding tilaks at the time of symbolic departure. 15<-
</insert>

—he returns afflicted by disease." One who is foolish, intoxicated, sick, terrified, weary, angry, or hungry should not set out on the road. Likewise for one who has been obstructed. In the *Ratnavali*: "If one sets out after striking a woman or a man, whether they are his own or another's, he abandons his very life." Bhrigu says: "Avoid milk for three nights, the act of shaving for five days, and sexual intercourse for seven days; for the rest, avoid them on the day itself." Regarding the signs of beginning a journey, Garga says: "When the auspicious moment arrives, one should depart like a swan or an elephant, surrounded by astrologers, ministers, priests, and kinsmen. A king should first walk thirty-two steps on foot toward the east or south, and then mount his vehicle to proceed. Then, having properly observed omens—both auspicious and inauspicious—and likewise listening for good or bad sounds and signs, he should proceed." 15

<summary>This page details the specific ascetic and ritual prohibitions required for a successful journey, including dietary restrictions (avoiding meat, jaggery, and milk) and behavioral codes. It also explains the "Prasthana," a symbolic ritual departure used when a traveler cannot fully embark during the most auspicious astrological window.</summary>
<keywords>travel rules, Yatra, Prasthana, auspicious timing, dietary prohibitions, Ratnamala, Garga, astrological omens, symbolic departure, purification</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the manual for travelers. While the previous page (83) focused on internal discipline and things to avoid (ascetic-like restrictions), pages 84 and 85 shift to external "shakuna" or omens. These are specific objects, people, or sounds that a traveler might encounter upon leaving their home, which are interpreted as signs of future success.</meta>

<margin>Su. Ma. 84</margin>

Now he describes the omens <note>original: "shakuna"</note>. Regarding clothing: "Clothing" refers to white garments. Other items mentioned among the omens include "ornaments," meaning jewelry made of gold and other precious materials. The "full pot" <note>original: "purna-kalasha," a vessel filled with water and sprouts symbolizing abundance</note> is well-known. "Sound of music" <note>original: "taurya-dhvani"</note> refers to the sound of musical instruments. "A man with a son" refers to a father whose son is present or thriving. The "conch shell" <note>original: "shankha"</note> is well-known. "Mirror" <note>original: "adarsha"</note> or looking-glass. "King" means the ruler. "Horse" is well-known. "Elephant" <note>original: "danti," literally "the one with tusks"</note>. "Cow" <note>original: "surabhi"</note>. "A woman" who is fortunate or auspicious. "Flowers" should be white.

# White clothing and ornaments, a full water-pot, the sound of music, a man with his son, a conch shell, a mirror, a king, a horse, an elephant, a cow, an auspicious woman, flowers, and grains.
# Swings, a lamp, smokeless fire, fragrant substances, a white bull, an umbrella, a canopy, a fly-whisk, a young girl, fish, and cow-pigment. || 15 ||

"Grains" should not be red or black; those are listed among bad omens. The word "and so forth" <note>original: "adi"</note> includes metals like gold. "Swing" <note>original: "dola"</note> is well-known. "Lamp" is well-known. "Smokeless fire" means a fire without smoke. "Fragrant substances" include scents like camphor and sandalwood. "White bull" means a bull of white color. "Prostitute" <note>original: "veshya"</note> is well-known <note>In ancient Indian divination, seeing a professional dancer or prostitute was often considered a lucky omen for worldly success</note>. "Umbrella" is well-known. "Canopy" <note>original: "vitanam"</note> means a cloth ceiling or awning. "Fly-whisk" <note>original: "chamara," a symbol of royalty</note> is well-known. "Young girl" <note>original: "kumari"</note> is well-known. "Fish" means a fish just taken from the water. "Cow-pigment" <term>gorochana: a bright yellow or gold pigment found in the gallbladders of cows, used for tilaks and considered highly auspicious</term> is well-known. || 15 ||
<margin>Tika 84</margin>

---

He explains without hesitation that "whatever object" <note>original: "yad vastu"</note> brings "complete satisfaction to the mind" <note>original: "nitaanta-toshaka-paranam"</note>. The mind <note>original: "svantam" or "antah-karana"</note> refers to the inner self; whatever causes it great joy is a good sign. To put it briefly, he now lists those things that previous masters have categorized as omens when there is no lack of satisfaction. He mentions "cow dung lifted from the earth" <note>original: "koruddhruta-gomayam"</note>; "Ko" means the earth, so it is dung taken from the ground. "Soil" <note>original: "mrrt"</note> is earth that has been dug up. "A companion carrying a water-pot" <note>original: "ambhortha-ghatanvitah sahacharah"</note> refers to a person walking nearby who is carrying a vessel for the purpose of water. A person seeking water who carries an empty pot should be avoided by the traveler; one should return home if that happens. It is said that just as a full pot signifies a successful traveler, an empty one is the opposite. "A friend with washed clothes" <note>original: "dhautambaro maitrakah"</note> refers to a washerman carrying clean, laundered garments. "Durva grass" is well-known; "Darbha grass" <term>darbha: a sacred grass used in Vedic rituals</term> is well-known. Even if Durva and Darbha are seen in three-fold quantities, they are auspicious, though ordinary grass is listed among bad omens.

# Whatever object brings deep satisfaction to the heart; cow dung lifted from the earth; soil; a companion with a water-pot; a laundryman with clean clothes. Besides these: a chariot, meat...

"Meat" <note>original: "palam mamsam"</note>; "Blood" <note>original: "asruk raktam"</note>; "Wine" <note>original: "madira madyam"</note>; "Students" <note>original: "varnino"</note> meaning a celibate student <note>original: "brahmachari"</note>; "Teacher" <note>original: "guru"</note> is well-known. "Astrologer" <note>original: "daiva-vijno"</note>; "Priest" <note>original: "vipro"</note> or a leader among the twice-born. "A friend" or someone dear. "Deer" <note>original: "mrga"</note> is well-known. "A corpse without weeping" <note>original: "ashedanashavam"</note> refers to a dead body being carried without the sound of wailing. "White mustard seeds" <note>original: "siddharthau"</note>. "Curds and so forth" refers to milk, curds, and ghee. Now, one might ask: if "whatever brings satisfaction to the heart" was already mentioned, and milk is satisfying, why mention it again? It is true, but since milk was forbidden to be *consumed* before a journey (on the previous page), it is mentioned here as a *sight* to remove any doubt that seeing it is actually auspicious. This is how the matter should be considered. Thus says the authority Vasanta <note>original: "Vasanta-raja-shakuna," a standard 12th-century text on omens</note>: "Curds, ghee, Durva grass, unbroken grains, full pots, white mustard, sandalwood, mirror, conch, sword, fish, wine, cow-pigment, cow dung, soil, and gold." Bharadvaja also says: "A king, a priest, a teacher, a beloved, a son, a woman, a young girl, a handsome man or woman, an elephant..."
<margin>85</margin>

<summary>This section lists auspicious omens (shakuna) that a traveler should hope to see upon departing, ranging from royal symbols like elephants and fly-whisks to ritual items like curds, mirrors, and specific grasses. It concludes by citing historical authorities like Vasanta and Bharadvaja to validate these signs of good fortune.</summary>

A white horse or bull, a bull tied with a rope, a cow of a different color accompanied by her calf; freshly cleared cow dung, blue soil, a washerman carrying washed clothes, a prostitute, the sound of three musical instruments playing together, the sounds of victory and celebration, prayers for peace <note>original: "shanti-patha"</note>, lamps, a burning fire, a full water-pot, and the king’s throne. Also: wine, meat, blood, various kinds of sweets, sugarcane, honey, betel nut, fine clothes and ornaments, silver, copper, gems, gold, a mirror, unbroken rice <note>original: "akshata," used in rituals</note>, a canopy, a fly-whisk, an umbrella, flags with emblems, a swing, a beautiful woman, an elder, one accompanied by sons and grandsons, a person who is ritually pure, sandalwood, fragrances, flowers, yellow pigment <note>original: "rochana," a bright yellow pigment used for marks on the forehead</note>, ghee, yogurt, milk, beverages, cooked rice, and auspicious words. One should also look for an astrologer <note>original: "daivajna"</note>, a teacher, a deity, and things that provide encouragement. If a man sees these things as he departs, he will attain all his goals. || 16 ||

-> Durva grass, Darbha grass, Kusha grass, fruits, things the color of wine, a teacher, a god, a scholar, a priest, a friend, or a deer. Having these on the right side always brings success. Other things, such as ash, bones, feces, salt, shoes, blacksmiths, oil cake, medicine, black grains, crying, and smoke, should be avoided. || 17 || <-

The author explains the verse: The things just mentioned should be kept on the "right side" <note>original: "dakshina-bhaga"</note> when one is traveling. Other inauspicious or "unhealthy" things should be kept to the left. These include: ash, bones, firewood, feces, the husks or chaff of grain, stones, sea salt, shoes, grass, and "blacksmiths" <note>original: "ayaskara," which literally means iron-workers, but here implies the sound or sight of metal-working</note>. Also: oil cake <note>original: "pinyaka," the residue of pressed oil seeds</note>, medicine, black grains like black gram <note>original: "masha"</note>, and the sound of crying.

---

The list continues: Fire accompanied by smoke, cotton, red flowers like the hibiscus <note>original: "japa"</note>, leather, jaggery, oil, iron, and mud. || 17 ||

Next, he describes people and animals to avoid: A drunkard, one who has vomited, a hungry person, a killer of animals, a villain, one with a shaven head <note>original: "mundi," often referring to monks of certain orders</note>, one with matted hair <note>original: "jati"</note>, a sick person, a lame person, one with a missing limb, a naked person, a wandering ascetic <note>original: "yati"</note>, an enemy, one wearing ochre-colored robes, one with loose or disheveled hair, a thief, one smeared with oil, a dirty person, and an outcast. 

-> A drunkard, one who has vomited, the hungry, a killer, a villain, the shaven-headed, the matted-haired, the sick, the lame, the deformed, the naked, the ascetic, the enemy, the ochre-robed, those with loose hair, thieves, the oil-smeared, the dirty, the outcast, those holding nooses, those holding door-bars, pregnant women, barren women, black snakes, scorpions, frogs, monitors, boars, and hares. || 18 || <-

A "noose-bearer" <note>original: "pashi"</note> refers to someone carrying a rope for binding animals. An "argali" is someone carrying a door-bolt or bar. A pregnant woman, a barren woman, a "black snake" <note>original: "krishna-sarpa"</note>, a frog, a monitor lizard <note>original: "godha"</note>, a boar, and a hare are also mentioned. || 18 || 

The text continues with: A cat <note>original: "otu" or "marjara"</note>, a buffalo, a donkey, a camel, or someone riding a buffalo. An empty pot, a sneeze <note>original: "kshuta"</note>, the trembling of an animal's head, and "path-binding" <note>original: "padavi-bandha," referring to something blocking the way, such as a spider's web</note>. Also, "bad speech" <note>original: "kuvak," meaning harsh words or insults</note> and conflict <note>original: "ahava," a fight or battle</note>.

It transitions from positive signs like gold and music to negative ones like ash, sick people, and specific animals.</summary>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of "shakuna" or omens. The previous page listed auspicious sights for a traveler (white horses, teachers, etc.). Page 86 shifts focus to omens that indicate obstacles or death, before introducing the concept of "contrary omens" where a typically "bad" sign becomes "good" depending on the context of the activity or the traveler's breath.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Illumination of Auspicious Moments</header>

A battle between cats, buffaloes, and the like; the falling of clothes or other items; the fleeing of a vehicle or a person who has already set out; a dog fight; seeing a eunuch <note>original: "shandha," referring to a person of the third gender or a neuter animal</note>; an injury to a cow; seeing fire or glowing stones—these are omens that act as "preventers" or obstacles to one's work. Sneezing and similar bad omens are "death-givers" <note>original: "mrityuda," signifying extreme ill-fortune</note>. As stated in another scripture: grass, oil, cotton, ash, leather, straw, medicine, sea salt, jaggery, mud, snakes, wind, water, and injuries, etc. || 19 ||

Now he mentions other omens: horned animals, camels, owls, those that dig with claws, and female-named creatures...

**The falling of objects, the fleeing of a vehicle, dog fights, injury to a eunuch or a cow, and glowing stones are obstacle-making bad omens. Sneezing and similar signs give death. || 19 || Horned animals, camels, owls, those that dig with claws, and female-named creatures on the left; the buffalo, the cock, and male-named ones with their sounds on the right, are givers of one's heart's desires. The skylark, peacock, blue jay, mongoose, and swan—these, when seen or coming toward you, give desired results. The braying of a donkey on the left or even behind gives wealth. || 20 ||**

<gloss>jashruka</gloss>: dogs and similar animals; <gloss>narikhana</gloss>: those that dig with claws; <gloss>female-named</gloss>: sparrows and such; if these are on the left, they fulfill one's desires. <gloss>Kali</gloss>: a female buffalo; the cock; <gloss>male-named</gloss>: crows and such; if these make their sounds on the right, they fulfill one's desires. The <term>Bharadwaja</term> <note>often identified as a skylark or a malabar whistling thrush</note>, the peacock, the <term>Chasha</term> <note>the Indian Roller or Blue Jay</note>, the mongoose, the swan, and the goat—if these are seen or come toward the traveler, they result in the attainment of one's goals. This means whether they are on the left, right, or in front, merely seeing them is auspicious. The braying of a donkey on the left or even from behind is a "wealth-giver," meaning it grants the desired outcome. || 20 || <margin>Finished, finished, finished, finished, finished</margin>

---

Now he mentions those specific activities in which "contrary" omens are actually auspicious:

Crossing a river, situations of fear, entering a village or fort, battle, gambling, looking for lost items, and the treatment of a disease—in these cases, "contrary omens" <note>original: "viloma-shakuna," omens that are usually considered bad for beginning a journey</note> give good results. This means that what is considered an inauspicious omen for departing is actually auspicious for crossing a river and so on. Similarly, when the <term>Ida</term> <note>the left nostril breath channel in Yoga, associated with cooling and lunar energy</note> is full, contrary omens are auspicious. As Sage Vasishtha says: "When the *Ida* is fully filled, every omen on the left is excellent."

->**In crossing a river, in fear, in entering, in battle, in gambling, in looking for lost items, and in disease, contrary omens are auspicious; also when the Ida breath channel is full.**<-

When the <term>Pingala</term> <note>the right nostril breath channel, associated with heating and solar energy</note> is full, every omen on the right is excellent. In the work of battle, entering a house, looking for lost people, in disease, and in crossing rivers, forts, or forests, it is auspicious if the omen is the opposite of what is required for starting a journey. Furthermore, in crossing a river, fear, war, entry, or looking for lost items, "inverted" omens are as auspicious for a king as they are for a journey.

Now, he explains what should be done if an inauspicious omen occurs at the start. If a bad omen happens at the very beginning, one should return, sit in a clean place, and wash one's hands and feet...

<summary>This page lists inauspicious omens like dog fights or injuries to cows that hinder work, while also detailing "contrary omens" where typically bad signs become auspicious during specific tasks like crossing a river or gambling.</summary>
<keywords>omens, shakuna, Ida breath, Pingala breath, Bharadwaja bird, contrary omens, traveling etiquette, ritual purity</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a transition in the *Muhurta Martanda*. It concludes the "Travel Chapter" (Yatra Prakaranam) by providing ritual remedies for bad omens encountered during a journey, and begins the "Miscellaneous Chapter" (Mishra Prakaranam), focusing on the auspicious timing for royal coronations and the installation of sacred idols.</meta>

<margin>
Su-Ma
87
</margin>

One should have their feet washed and have performed <term>Achamana</term> <note>the ritual sipping of water for purification</note>. Regarding "Pranayama": the word *Asavah* refers to the breaths or life-forces, and *Ayama* means their restraint or extension; thus, <term>Pranayama</term> is the regulation of breath. If one has set out on a journey and a bad omen <note>original: "dushakuna"</note> occurs, one should stop, become pure, perform the sipping of water, and remain still while performing eight or sixteen cycles of breath regulation. If a bad omen occurs on the second or third attempt to leave, one should give a gift of gold and then proceed. This rule applies when the travel is absolutely necessary. One who is not capable of breath regulation should simply wait for that duration of time before moving. As stated in the *Jyotirnibandha* <note>a classical compendium of Vedic astrology</note>: "When a contrary omen appears, one should first perform eight breath regulations; on the second occurrence, one should double that; on the third, one should not travel at all."

->**Upon the first bad omen, return, purify yourself, and perform breath restraint eight times. If it happens again, perform it further; on a subsequent occurrence, give gold and then proceed. || 21 ||**<-

->### Thus ends the Chapter on Travel in the *Muhurta Martanda* composed by Narayana, the performer of the Chaturmasya sacrifice and son of the illustrious Shri Ananta. ###<-

<margin>
Thus, 
even on other paths, 
one may proceed 
in health. 21
</margin>

And so says Vasantaraja: "When a contrary omen occurs, a wealthy person should not turn back but should wash their feet, perform the ritual sipping of water, and look at the cut wood beneath a milk-bearing tree." 
Likewise, Angirasa says: "When one sees an animal or a bad omen presenting itself in a contrary way, one should touch gold, give a gift, and proceed without doubt."

<margin>
Shri
87
</margin>

->This concludes the commentary on the Chapter on Travel in the *Muhurta Martanda*. <-

---

Now, wishing to speak on the "Miscellaneous Chapter," the author describes the coronation of a king in the first half of a verse, beginning with "Birth..." One’s "birth-limb-star-period" refers to the strength of the birth ascendant, the birth moon sign, and the current planetary periods. When these are powerful, along with favorable planets like Mars and the Sun, and when the Moon is in a "gentle," "swift," or "fixed" constellation <note>referring to specific qualities of Nakshatras</note>, the establishment of a king is recommended. Specifically, it should be done in a fixed or dual-natured sign, on a day other than Tuesday, and in a favorable ascendant relative to the birth sign.

**When the birth ascendant, the birth sign, and the current planetary periods are powerful; during gentle, swift, or fixed stars; in a fixed or dual-natured sign; on a day other than Tuesday; the establishment of a king is auspicious. With one's own colors/golds, during prosperous, fixed, or movable stars, the installation of people's deities should occur in the months of Magha, Phalguna, Vaishakha, or Jyeshtha, on a day other than Tuesday, in the forenoon. || 1 ||**

<margin>The establishment of a king; that is, the royal inauguration should be auspicious. Small strength...</margin>
Generally, this should be observed during the transit of the Moon. Now, the author describes the "Installation of Deities" in the latter half of the verse, starting with **"With one's own colors..."** The installation of a particular deity should be done during the constellations associated with that deity, or during prosperous, fixed, or movable stars. This should be done during the pair of months <note>original: "yugala"</note> of Magha and Phalguna, or the pair of Vaishakha and Jyeshtha. It should be on a "day without Mars" <note>Tuesday</note>, in the forenoon—the first of the two parts of the day.

<summary>This page concludes the guidelines for overcoming bad omens during travel through purification and breath-work, then transitions into the astrological requirements for coronating a king and installing religious idols.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Achamana, Travel Omens, Royal Coronation, Devasthapana, Muhurta, Nakshatras, Narayana, Ritual Purification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on miscellaneous ritual timings (Mishra Prakaranam). Having concluded the rules for travel, the text now transitions to the auspicious timing for daily activities, specifically the wearing and maintenance of new clothing and jewelry.</meta>

<margin>
Mu. Ma.
88
</margin>
<margin>
Comm.
88
</margin>

Regarding the placement of deities mentioned previously, the remaining details for "The Installation of All Immortals" <note>original: "sarvāmara-sthāpanam"</note> should be understood as clearly stated. The strength of the Ascendant <note>original: "lagna-balam"</note> remains as previously described. 

Now, the author speaks regarding the wearing of [new] clothes, their washing, and the omens associated with clothes bitten by rats and other animals. He says "One should wear" <note>original: "dhāryam"</note>: New clothes should be worn when the Moon is in the following lunar mansions <term>Nakshatras</term>: <note>the 27 stations of the moon used in Vedic astrology</note> Dhanishtha <note>associated with the Vasus</note>, Punarvasu <note>associated with Aditi</note>, the five stars of Hasta <note>associated with the Sun, "Ina"</note>, Pushya <note>associated with the Guru/Brihaspati</note>, Ashwini, the final star Revati, and the "Fixed" stars <note>Dhruva: Rohini, Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>. These should be paired with the "three wise days": Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday. One should not wear them on other days, for it is said: "Sunday makes clothes worn out; Monday brings a little luck; Tuesday brings sorrow; Wednesday and Thursday bring knowledge; Friday brings happiness; and Saturday brings filth." The strength of the Ascendant should be considered as before.

Next, he speaks of washing clothes. In these same lunar mansions mentioned for wearing clothes, one may wash them, but "without Wednesday" <note>original: "jñone"</note>. It is taught that regarding the wearing of gold and ornaments, one should use Ashwini and the others.

->**One should wear new clothes during Dhanishtha, Punarvasu, the five stars of Hasta, Pushya, Ashwini, Revati, and the fixed stars, on Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday. One may wash clothes during these same stars, except on Wednesday. Clothing eaten by a rat in the middle third is inauspicious.**<-

Even if it was previously stated that a person should perform their regular duties on any day, the exclusion of Wednesday is specifically for the act of washing. This prohibition of Wednesday is echoed in other traditions, which say: "Washing clothes on Saturday, Monday, the sixth lunar day, or the New Moon day burns the family up to the seventh generation." 

Now he describes the result of clothes being bitten by rats: "Eaten by a rat" <note>original: "ākhu-bhakshitam"</note>. If a piece of clothing is eaten by a rat in its middle third, it brings a wicked or bad result. This means that if you fold the cloth into three parts and the bitten portion falls in the middle section, it is considered a bad omen. The word "rat" is used as a general category; damage from fire or being eaten by a cow should also be understood as included here.

---

<margin>
88
</margin>

As it is stated in the "Hundred Verses" <note>original: "Vritta-shate"</note>: "A cloth that is burnt, torn, smeared with cow dung, ink, or mud, or bitten by mice or birds, brings misfortune if the damage is in the middle third."

Next, he describes the wearing of red clothes and ornaments by women, and which planets favor which colors. "A woman" <note>original: "stri"</note>: A woman should wear her clothes and golden ornaments except during the stars of the Sun <note>Hasta</note>, Pushya, and the "Fixed" stars. In those specific stars, a defect is seen for women wearing red clothes or ornaments. As it is said:

->**A woman should not wear red clothes or ornaments during the stars of Hasta, Pushya, or the fixed stars. White is favored for the Moon, red and gold for Mars and the Sun, and black for Saturn. || 2 ||**<-

One who desires a long life for her husband should not wear new gold or red ornaments during Pushya, Hasta, or the fixed stars. The strength of the Ascendant is as before. Now he speaks of the planetary preferences: White <note>original: "shvete"</note>—including silver and pearls—is favored on Monday. Red and gold are favored for Mars and the Sun. Black or blue clothing is favored on Saturday. However, on the "three wise days" (Wednesday, Thursday, Friday), all colors may be worn, as stated previously.

<summary>This page details the astrologically auspicious times for wearing and washing new clothes and jewelry. It specifically warns against washing on Wednesdays and interprets the bad omen of clothing damaged by rats, particularly in the middle section.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurta, Nakshatras, auspicious timing, clothing rituals, rat omens, planetary colors, women's ornaments</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from folio 88 regarding daily life and rituals. Having previously covered the washing and initial wearing of new clothing, the text now transitions to the proper times for putting on jewelry (ornamentation), engaging in commerce, harvesting or storing grass, entering service, and the solemn rite of renunciation (Sanyasa).</meta>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 89</margin>
Now, the author speaks regarding the wearing of ornaments for men in the latter part of the verse, beginning with "With clothes..." <note>original: "vastraiḥ"</note>. This refers to clothes made of linen, silk, and other materials mentioned previously. Regarding the placement of these items on the body in stable Ascendants <term>Lagnas</term>, the ornamentation <note>original: "alaṅ-kṛti"</note> of a person will be beneficial. This applies to all clothing and ornaments, except when the Moon is in the "Two Winds" <note>Anila-dvaya: the lunar mansions Swati and Vishakha</note> and the "Twin Physicians" <note>Ashvini</note>. This means the wearing is favorable in Nakshatras other than Swati, Vishakha, and Ashwini. The strength of the Ascendant should be considered as before.

Now, he speaks of a special case in the second line regarding "The Brahman's command..." <note>original: "viprājñā"</note>. When there is a command from a Brahman, a festival <note>such as a wedding</note>, or a sudden gain <note>original: "labdhi"</note>, the wearing of ornaments and clothes is considered auspicious even in the absence of the previously mentioned favorable Nakshatras. Thus, Vyasa has said: "In the case of a gain, by the grace of one's birth [stars], or when traveling to another country, or during a joyful festival, clothing may be worn [without further calculation]."

<margin>Comm. 89</margin>
->**Ornamentation is auspicious with linen and clothes in [various] body-parts, except in the two Wind-stars and Ashvini. Wearing them is also beneficial upon the command of a Brahman, during festivals, or when a gain is realized. Buying and selling of goods [should be done] in the stars of Maitra [Anuradha], Aka [Arka/Hasta], the Wind [Swati], Mula, Dasra [Ashwini], and Hari [Shravana]—**<-
->**as well as in the stars of the Gods [Pushya], the Teacher [Jupiter/Pushya], and the 'Endings' [the three Uttaras and Rohini]. Commerce should be expanded in the signs from the pot [Aquarius] onwards, provided one's own faith and the purity of the [astrological] union are present. (3)**<-

<note>The text continues explaining the second half of the verse regarding commerce.</note>
Regarding the buying and selling of goods: "Maitra" refers to the Nakshatra Anuradha. The rest is easy to understand. "In the signs beyond the Pot" <note>original: "ghaṭa-parārdhe"</note>: "The Pot" is Aquarius <term>Kumbha</term>. One should expand the buying and selling of goods—such as betel nuts, fruits, and gold—in the five Ascendants <term>Lagnas</term> other than Aquarius <note>likely referring to the signs following Aquarius in a specific astrological sequence</note>. This should be done "when one's own faith is present," meaning when there is confidence, and when there is "purity of the union," meaning an absence of malefic planetary influences. The rest is as before. As it is said: "One should perform commercial activities when the benefics are in the 10th, 11th, the wealth house [2nd], or the angles and trines, avoiding the rise of Aquarius." (3)

---

Now, with the following half-verse, he states what is to be avoided during the "Five Stars of Dhanishtha" <note>original: "dhaniṣṭhā-pañcake," a period often considered inauspicious for certain earthly tasks</note>:
When the Moon <note>original: "vidhau"</note> is in the "Two Pots" <note>the signs Aquarius and Pisces</note>, one should avoid certain actions. Specifically, one should entirely avoid the cremation of the dead <note>original: "preta-jvalana"</note>, the making of beds, traveling to the south, and the gathering or stacking of grass. This is clear.

Now, with the latter part of the verse, he discusses service <note>original: "sevā"</note>: Service is considered desirable in the "Fixed" Nakshatras and others. This is easy. This is when the Sun <note>original: "ravi"</note> or Mars <note>original: "kuja"</note> is in the 11th house <note>original: "āya"</note> or the 10th house from the Ascendant in which the service is commenced. In such cases, entering service is beneficial. The rest is as before.

With the fourth part of the verse, he speaks of renunciation <note>original: "sanyāsa"</note>: Renunciation is desirable in the Fixed Nakshatras and Fixed Ascendants when the malefic planets are "devoid of strength" <note>original: "gatabalaiḥ"</note>. This occurs when Venus <note>original: "bhārgave"</note>—

->**One should avoid the cremation of the dead, making beds, traveling south, and gathering grass when the Moon is in the two signs of the Pot [Aquarius and Pisces]. Service is desired in the Fixed, Varuna [Shatabhisha], and final [Revati] stars when the Sun or Mars is in the 11th or 10th. Renunciation is [auspicious] in Fixed signs and stars when malefic planets are weak and Venus is in the 6th or 12th. (4)**<-

—is in the 6th or 12th house. The strength of the Ascendant is as described. It is said: "When Saturn is in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th, and Venus is in the 12th or 6th, and the Lord of Dharma [9th house] and Jupiter are strong in the angles, it creates initiation <term>Diksha</term> and detachment." (5)

Next, he speaks of the wedding <note>original: "udvaha"</note> rituals... the eleven stars previously mentioned such as Mula, etc., are well known. "Two-foot" refers to Vishakha, "Indra" refers to Jyeshtha. The "Light" stars are those like Chitra. These should be done on days other than those of Saturn <note>original: "ārki"</note>, Mercury <note>original: "jña"</note>, and Mars <note>original: "āra"</note>. This means on days excluding Saturday, Wednesday, and Tuesday...

<summary>This page details the astrological timing for daily activities including wearing jewelry, engaging in trade, and entering service, while providing specific prohibitions for the "Five Dhanishtha" stars and requirements for the rite of renunciation.</summary>
<keywords>ornamentation, commerce, Nakshatras, Aquarius, service (seva), Sanyasa (renunciation), Dhanishtha Panchaka, astrology</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for daily activities from folio 89, transitioning from the wearing of ornaments and clothing to the specific astrological and ritual requirements for beginning agricultural work (plowing and sowing).</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>Muhurta Martanda 90 Commentary</header>

<margin>Mu. Ma. 90</margin>
<margin>Comm.</margin>
The meaning is as follows: regarding the Ascendants <term>Lagnas; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</term> of Gemini, Pisces, Taurus, and Virgo—"Yugma" refers to Gemini, "Jhasha" to Pisces, "Go" to Taurus, and "Kanya" is well-known as Virgo. On a lunar day <term>Tithi</term> that is an odd number <note>original: "oja"</note> and excludes the ninth—"Vyangarka" means the ninth is gone, because the ninth is the "Sun's number" in certain systems. Thus, it means on odd lunar days other than the ninth. What are the characteristics of these odd days? They should be "with the direction" <note>original: "sadishi"</note>, meaning together with the tenth lunar day. The favorable days are: 1, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 15, and 10. In these, agriculture is praised. As it has been said: "When the Moon is in Shravana or Rohini, on a ಮಂಗಳ (auspicious) day such as Thursday, Friday, Monday, or Wednesday, in the Ascendants of Taurus, Gemini, Pisces, or Virgo, one should begin the work of sowing grain." Furthermore: "The tenth, eleventh, third, thirteenth, seventh, fifth, and the first lunar days are all providers of happiness." These are the eight—

->**Agriculture is praised in the Ascendants of Gemini, Pisces, Taurus, and Virgo, on odd lunar days (except the ninth) and the tenth.**<-
->**Having bowed to the planets and the Earth-gods <term>Brahmans</term>, dressed in white, one should lead the plow with gold and excellent bulls three times toward the north or northeast. 5**<-

One should lead the plow toward the north or northeast. The ninth lunar day is a "crop-destroyer." The fourth destroys the grain, and the fourteenth is also harmful. On Sunday, Thursday, Monday, and Friday, agriculture is especially successful. Mercury, Saturn, and Mars are not considered givers of fruit in this regard. Now, the author explains the ritual procedure: "Dressed in white" <note>original: "sita-acchadana"</note> means wearing white garments. The practitioner should be skillful and capable. "Bowing to the planets and Earth-gods"—before beginning the agriculture, the "Sacrifice to the Furrow" <term>Sita-yajna; a ritual honoring the goddess of the plowed field</term> is performed as mentioned by the ancients. There, having worshiped and circumambulated the planets, the earth, and the sacred fire, and having bowed with mind, speech, and body, one begins. "With gold and excellent bulls"—with gold attached to the plow and with "uncastrated" <note>original: "samushka," referring to bulls with their virility intact to symbolize fertility</note>, beautiful, healthy bulls that possess all the good qualities of their breed.

---

<margin>90</margin>
With such bulls, one should lead the plow three times toward the north or northeast. The intention is that one should create furrows <note>original: "paddhati"</note> with the plow. By leading it three times toward the north, five lines <term>rekha</term> are produced. How? On the first forward path, one line; on the return, a second; on the second forward path, a third; on the second return, a fourth; and on the third forward path toward the north, the fifth line is created. The idea is that one should not make more than this. The use of the word "skillful" <note>original: "patu"</note> in the instruction "should lead toward the northeast" is to ensure the creation of unbroken, straight lines. As it has been said: "Under praised lunar mansions and a strong Moon, being pure and in white clothing, having bathed and especially worshiped with scents and flowers; having worshiped the Earth along with the household and the Lord of Creatures <term>Prajapati</term>; having circumambulated the fire and given gifts and donations—then two black oxen should be yoked. One should avoid a bull that is weak, has a docked tail, or is tawny. The plowing should be done by healthy, non-exhausted farmers. With beneficial plows and tools, safety is ensured; otherwise, it is inauspicious. If the bulls or the farmers fight, there will always be obstacles. One should not allow the furrows to be broken. Facing the north, one should offer a libation of milk. Then, all the tools for the crops will be free of obstacles. One line brings victory, three bring success, and five lines provide many fruits of the harvest. One should not plow upward at the very end, as a great defect arises from that." For the pacification of all obstacles, one should remember the Vasus, Indra, the King Prithu, the Moon, Parashurama, and Balarama <note>original: "Halayudha," the plow-wielder</note>. Regarding bad omens, it is said: "If a tortoise appears while the plow is moving, the mistress of the house will die or there will be fear of fire. If the plow-pole breaks, there is doubt regarding the farmer's life. If the yoke breaks, a child dies. If the plow-share gets stuck or breaks, crop loss occurs. If a bull falls while plowing, or if the harness breaks, one should predict the failure of agriculture due to fever or disease."

<summary>This page details the auspicious astrological timings and ritual procedures for beginning agricultural work, specifically the first plowing. It emphasizes the "Sacrifice to the Furrow" (Sita-yajna), the use of specific bull types, and interprets various omens that might occur during the process.</summary>
<keywords>agriculture, plowing ritual, Sita-yajna, auspicious timing, Muhurta, bulls, omens, five furrows, Prithu, Balarama</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for agricultural astrology from page 90, moving from the timing of plowing to the specific cycles used to predict harvest success and the influence of the shadow planet Rahu on sowing and harvesting.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>Su-Ma- 91, Di- 91</header>

...if the bull, immediately after being harnessed to the plow, begins to dig or scrape the ground, it is said that the harvest will be fourfold. 5.

Now, regarding agricultural matters, the author describes the "Plowing Cycle" <term>Langala-chakra; a diagram used to determine auspicious days for plowing based on the Sun's position</term>, starting with the words "three, three" <note>original: "tri-tri"</note>. From the constellation <term>Nakshatra</term> occupied by the Sun, one should count groups of constellations. The results—bad or good—occur repeatedly. This is what is being said: starting from the Sun’s current constellation, the first three stars yield bad <note>original: "asat"</note> results. The next three are good <note>original: "sat"</note>. The next three are bad. The next five are good. The next three are bad. The next five are good. The next three are bad. Finally, the next three are good.

Next, the author discusses the "Sowing of Seeds" <term>Bija-vapa</term> using the agricultural constellations. He cites Varahamihira:

### Sowing seeds is auspicious in the agricultural constellations—excluding Shatabhisha, Shravana, and Jyeshtha—when counted in groups of three, three, fire (3), wealth (5), Rama (3), fire (3), and earth (1) from the constellation occupied by the Sun.

Sowing seeds is declared auspicious when done under these specific conditions. What are the characteristics of these "agricultural constellations"? They must be "without Varuna, Hari, and Indra" <note>original: "viva-runa-harindra"</note>. This means excluding the constellations of Shatabhisha (ruled by Varuna), Shravana (ruled by Hari/Vishnu), and Jyeshtha (ruled by Indra). This mention of agricultural constellations is an "indicative mark" <term>Upalakshana; a technical term meaning one example represents a whole category</term>; therefore, all previously mentioned lunar days <term>Tithis</term> and weekdays should also be considered.

As it has been stated: "On Tuesdays, there is fear of mice; on Saturdays, fear of locusts and insects. On the ninth lunar day, the danger is terrible, and on Mondays, there is a particular deficiency. One should sow all crops in the Ascendants <term>Lagnas</term> of Taurus, Pisces, Virgo, or Gemini if one desires a wealth of harvest." The strength of the Ascendant should be evaluated as explained before.

Now, regarding the sowing of seeds, the author describes the two-fold "Serpent Cycle" <term>Phani-chakra; a method of calculation using the nodes of the moon to avoid 'poisonous' or unlucky times</term>. First, counting from the constellation occupied by the Sun: the 2nd, 3rd, 7th, 9th, 10th, 11th, 18th, and 19th constellations are successful.

---

The second method is described by the words "by others" <note>original: "anyaih"</note>. Other teachers say that sowing seeds is auspicious in the twelve constellations counted from the "Equal to the Serpent" <note>original: "phani-sama"</note>, which is the seventh constellation from Rahu <term>the North Node of the Moon, depicted as a serpent's head</term>. This means that whichever constellation Rahu is currently in, the seventh star from it is called the "Serpent's Equal." From that point, the next twelve stars are favorable.

The name "Phani" (Serpent) for Rahu is well-known in other texts. The teacher Varahamihira, in his own compendium <note>the *Brihat Samhita*</note>, cites various opinions from other works regarding the nature of Rahu: "Some say that although his head was severed, it attained the status of a planet because it had tasted the nectar <term>Amrita</term> and did not lose its life. Others say Rahu is like the circular disks of the Moon or Sun, but being dark, he is not seen in the sky except during the eclipse seasons <term>Parva-kala</term> due to a boon from the Creator (the Lotus-born Brahma). Others teach that his body is divided into a head and a tail, appearing in the form of a serpent." Because he is described as having a serpent's form, the name "Phani" is famous.

In the *Ratnamala* as well, it is clearly stated that the Serpent Cycle is used for the time of sowing seeds. Furthermore, in another sacred text <term>Agama</term>, it says: "One should avoid sowing seeds from the constellation of Rahu up to the eighth star."

Next, the author discusses the "Harvesting of Grain" <term>Dhanya-chedanam</term> with the words "harvesting is done in the sowing stars." The cutting of those crops (harvesting) is prescribed in the same constellations used for sowing seeds. One might ask: "It was previously said that a limb should perform what the whole body does; why then repeat that harvesting happens in the sowing stars?" It is true, but there is a slight difference between general agricultural stars and specific sowing stars. Because both are common in practice, to remove any doubt about which stars to use, it is explicitly stated that harvesting should be done in the sowing stars. 6 5 4 5

It also discusses Rahu's mythological nature and identifies specific constellations and days to avoid to prevent crop damage from pests.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the agricultural and ritual instructions from page 91. While the previous page focused on plowing and sowing cycles (the Plowing Cycle and the Sowing of Seeds), this page moves forward to the post-harvest stage: setting up the threshing floor and the ritual of eating the first grains of the season.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>Su· Ma· 92 / Ti· 92</header>

Now that the grain has been produced, the author describes the next step in the sequence—the installation of the threshing post—with the words **"the methi."** One should use a "milky tree" <term>Kshira-taru; trees that exude a milky sap, such as the Banyan or Peepal</term> like the Banyan <note>original: "Vata"</note> and others. One should erect <note>original: "aropayet" or "nisvanet"</note> the threshing post <term>Methi; a central pillar in the threshing floor to which oxen are tethered to tread out the grain</term> during marriage constellations <term>Udvaha-bhaih</term> such as Mula, the final stars, or those of the Sun, and so on. A *methi* is the name of the wood placed in the threshing floor for the purpose of crushing grain and for yoking rows of bulls. As the Amarakosha <note>an ancient Sanskrit lexicon</note> says: "The bulls are joined or 'measured' <note>original: "medhyate"</note> at this post, thus it is called *methi*. In the masculine it is *medhi*: the wood fixed in the threshing floor to which animals are tied." What kind of marriage constellations? Those "without Upa and Anu" <note>original: "upan-anu-rahitaih"</note>. *Upa* refers to Uttara Phalguni and *Anu* refers to Anuradha; it should be free from those two. It is also said: "Revati, Rohini, Mula, Swati, Hasta, Mrigashirsha, Ashadha, Uttara Phalguni, Hasta, and Bhadrapada are considered auspicious for the binding of all seeds and for entering the granary. Anuradha and Uttara Phalguni are said to give pain like a fire." One should use the leaves of the Banyan tree and others.

### One should erect the threshing post made of a milky tree during marriage constellations, excluding Uttara Phalguni and Anuradha. The wise have declared the "New Grain" ritual to be performed in the spring and autumn, similar to the first feeding of a child.

<margin>Gumbhari, Shalmali, and also Audumbari</margin>
Similarly, the Dhatri tree and any other tree that "carries milk" <note>has milky sap</note>. For women, the *methi* should always be made from these for the sake of fruitfulness. Now, regarding the context of new grain, the author describes the "New Grain Ceremony" <term>Navanna; the ritual of eating the first harvest of the season</term> in the second line with the words **"like the first feeding."** The ritual of the New Grain is prescribed by the wise during the seasons of Spring <note>original: "Vasanta"</note> and Autumn <note>original: "Sharada"</note>, using the same auspicious constellations as the first feeding of a child <term>Anna-prashana</term>. The phrase "constellations of the first feeding" is an illustration; therefore, one should also include the lunar days <term>Tithis</term> and days of the week prescribed for that ceremony. For one who maintains the sacred fires <term>Sa-agnikasya</term>, this ritual is mandatory; otherwise, eating various new grains like rice <note>original: "vrihi"</note> and others without performing the New Grain ceremony results in a spiritual fault. These are the nine grains: rice, barley, wheat, wild rice, millet, bamboo seeds, shali rice, mung beans, and black gram. Among these, those produced in autumn should not be eaten without the first-fruit sacrifice <term>Agrayana</term>—

---

and those produced in spring should likewise not be eaten without the sacrifice. This is the rule. The ritual is performed in Autumn and Spring. The meaning is that the New Grain ritual should be done using the lunar days and stars prescribed for the first feeding, specifically in the sixth or eighth month. Next, the author describes the commencement of dancing and music with the words **"Maitra and the stars of the Sun."** *Maitra* is Anuradha. *Arka* is Hasta. *Antya* is Revati. *Vasu* is Dhanishtha. *Uttara* refers to the three "latter" stars. *Ijya* is Pushya. *Varuna* is Shatabhisha. *Indra* is Jyeshtha. Under these stars, one should begin dancing <note>original: "nartana"</note>. One should also begin a musical performance <term>Sangitaka</term> when a specific ascendant <term>Lagna</term> is present, provided it is "viewed by the Guru" <note>Jupiter</note> and when "benefic planets are in the fourth house" <note>original: "hibuka-gaih saumyaih"</note>.

### Under Anuradha, Hasta, Dhanishtha, the three Uttaras, Pushya, Shatabhisha, and Jyeshtha, dancing is auspicious. Music is auspicious when the ascendant is aspected by Jupiter and benefic planets are in the fourth house. 7

### Under Vishakha, Hasta, Mrigashirsha, Punarvasu, Pushya, Dhanishtha, Ardra, Ashwini, Revati, Krittika, Shatabhisha, Jyeshtha, and the three Purvas, the various activities involving animals are auspicious, excluding the sign of Leo. 8

<margin>With benefic planets situated in the fourth house; the rest is as before. 7 Now,</margin>
The author describes the activities involving various animals <term>Pashuna-kriya</term> in this single verse starting with **"Isha."** *Isha* is Vishakha. *Arka* is Hasta. *Indu* is Mrigashirsha. *Aditi* is Punarvasu. *Ijya* is Pushya. *Vasava* is Dhanishtha. *Shiva* is Ardra. Ashwini is well-known. *Antya* is Revati. *Agni* is Krittika. *Pashi* is Shatabhisha. *Indra* is Jyeshtha. With these and the three "Purva" stars. "Excluding the sign of Leo" <note>original: "vi-simha-bha-nanau"</note> means those signs from which Leo has been removed...
